Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Ezekiel 1:1
And it happened in the thirtieth year, in the fourth month, on the fifth of the month, while I was among the captives by the River Chebar: the heavens opened, and I saw visions of God.
  Acts 7:55-56 — parallel opened heavens and sight of divine glory: Ezekiel sees God's visions when the heavens open during captivity by Chebar. Stephen likewise sees the heavens opened and God's glory, there with Jesus standing at God's right hand; the witnesses and settings differ.

Ezekiel 1:2
On the fifth of the month—this was the fifth year of King Jehoiakim’s captivity—
  Ezekiel 40:1-2 — related dating of Ezekiel's visions by captivity years: The opening dates the vision to the fifth year of King Jehoiakim's captivity. Ezekiel 40 dates a later vision to the twenty-fifth year of our captivity and fourteenth after the city's capture, connecting the book's chronology through captivity while preserving its differing dates and native royal name.

Ezekiel 1:3
the Lord’s word came to Ezekiel son of Buzi, the priest, in the land of the Chaldeans by the River Chebar; and the Lord’s hand came upon me,
  Ezekiel 3:22 — repeated Lord's hand and speech upon Ezekiel: The Lord's word comes to Ezekiel at Chebar and His hand comes upon the speaker. Ezekiel 3 again reports the Lord's hand upon him together with a spoken direction, there sending him to the plain.

Ezekiel 1:4
and I looked, and behold, a sweeping wind was coming from the north, and a great cloud was within it, with brightness around it and fire flashing like lightning; within it was something like the appearance of electrum, within the fire, and brightness within it.
  Psalms 17:9-15 — shared wind, cloud, fire and lightning imagery of divine manifestation: Ezekiel's vision begins with a sweeping wind, cloud, brightness and flashing fire. The psalm likewise portrays God's coming with wind, clouds, radiance, fiery coals and lightning; it does not repeat the origin from the north or electrum appearance.

Ezekiel 1:5
And within it was something like a likeness of four living creatures. This was their appearance: a human likeness was upon them,
  Ezekiel 10:20 — later explicit identification of the Chebar living creature: Ezekiel first sees four living creatures with a human likeness. The later vision expressly identifies the living creature seen beneath Israel's God at Chebar as cherubim, supplying the book's own identification rather than an identification from another vision tradition.

Ezekiel 1:6
and each had four faces, and each had four wings.
  Ezekiel 10:21 — repeated four faces with differing native wing counts: Both accounts assign four faces to each creature. This unit gives each four wings, while Ezekiel 10:21 gives each eight wings and adds human hands beneath them; the relationship preserves rather than normalizes that difference.

Ezekiel 1:7
Their legs were straight, and their feet were winged, with sparks like flashing bronze; and their wings were light.
  Daniel 10:6 — shared flashing-bronze appearance of visionary feet: Ezekiel describes winged feet with sparks like flashing bronze. Daniel's visionary figure likewise has arms and feet like flashing bronze; Daniel does not give those feet wings or reproduce Ezekiel's whole description.

Ezekiel 1:8
A human hand was beneath their wings on their four sides; and the faces of all four of them
  Ezekiel 10:8 — repeated human-hand likeness beneath the creatures' wings: The Chebar description places a human hand beneath the wings. Ezekiel 10 explicitly describes the likeness of human hands beneath the cherubim's wings, repeating this unusual combination of human and winged features.

Ezekiel 1:9
did not turn as they walked; each went straight ahead.
  Ezekiel 10:22 — repeated straight-ahead movement of Chebar's creatures: The faces of all four do not turn as they walk, and each goes straight ahead. Ezekiel 10 explicitly recalls the faces seen at Chebar and likewise says each went straight ahead, linking the movement descriptions through the stated earlier vision.

Ezekiel 1:10
The likeness of their faces was a human face and a lion’s face on the right for all four, and a calf’s face on the left for all four, and an eagle’s face for all four.
  Ezekiel 10:22 — later explicit recollection of the faces seen at Chebar: This unit describes the human, lion, calf and eagle faces in the Chebar vision. Ezekiel 10:22 explicitly identifies its faces with those seen beneath God's glory at Chebar, although that later unit does not repeat the four individual face names.

Ezekiel 1:11
The wings of all four were stretched out above; each had two joined to one another, and two covered the top of their bodies.
  Isaiah 6:2 — related heavenly wings covering the creatures' bodies: Ezekiel's four-winged creatures have two wings joined and two covering their bodies' upper part. Isaiah's six-winged seraphim likewise use wings to cover themselves, there two for faces and two for feet, with another two for flying; the beings and arrangements are distinct.

Ezekiel 1:12
Each went straight ahead. Wherever the spirit was going, they went and did not turn.
  Ezekiel 1:20 — same-chapter spirit and coordinated movement: The creatures follow where the spirit goes without turning. Ezekiel 1:20 locates the spirit for movement where the cloud is and explains creatures and wheels rising together through a spirit of life in the wheels, developing the coordination without dropping its cloud clause.

Ezekiel 1:13
And amid the living creatures was an appearance like burning coals of fire, like the sight of torches whirling among the living creatures, and brightness of the fire; lightning came out of the fire.
  Ezekiel 10:2 — repeated burning coals among the living creatures or cherubim: The Chebar vision places burning coals and torch-like fire among the living creatures. Ezekiel 10 likewise locates burning coals among the cherubim and commands that they be taken and scattered over the city, adding a judgment action to the shared fiery setting.

Ezekiel 1:15
I looked, and behold, one wheel was on the earth adjoining the four living creatures;
  Ezekiel 10:9 — related wheels adjoining the visionary creatures: Ezekiel first notices one wheel on the earth adjoining the four living creatures. The later vision describes four wheels, one beside each cherub, providing a more distributed arrangement without changing this unit's singular wording.

Ezekiel 1:16
the appearance of the wheels was like the appearance of tharsis, and all four had one likeness; their workmanship was as though a wheel were within a wheel.
  Ezekiel 10:10 — repeated common wheel appearance and wheel-within-wheel workmanship: Both descriptions say all four wheels share one likeness and compare their workmanship or appearance with a wheel within a wheel. The target does not repeat this unit's tharsis comparison.

Ezekiel 1:17
They went toward their four sides; they did not turn as they went,
  Ezekiel 10:11 — repeated four-direction movement without turning: Both passages say the wheels move toward their four sides or directions without turning. Ezekiel 10 further explains that they follow wherever the leading part looks.

Ezekiel 1:18
nor did their backs; they were tall, and I saw them. The backs of all four were full of eyes all around.
  Ezekiel 10:12 — repeated backs full of eyes all around: Ezekiel describes the backs of all four as full of eyes all around. The later account likewise includes their backs among the parts full of surrounding eyes, also naming hands, wings and wheels; neither explanation substitutes rims for the supplied backs.

Ezekiel 1:19
When the living creatures went, the wheels moved beside them; when the living creatures rose from the earth, the wheels rose.
  Ezekiel 10:16-17 — repeated coordinated movement and rising of creatures and wheels: The wheels move beside the living creatures and rise when they rise. Ezekiel 10 likewise describes wheels accompanying the cherubim and rising with them, also adding their coordinated standing and the spirit of life.

Ezekiel 1:20
Wherever the cloud was, there was the spirit for movement; the living creatures and the wheels went and rose together with them, because a spirit of life was in the wheels.
  Ezekiel 10:17 — repeated spirit of life explaining coordinated rising: Ezekiel 1 explains the creatures and wheels rising together through a spirit of life in the wheels, after naming the cloud and spirit for movement. Ezekiel 10 likewise explains coordinated rising through a spirit of life in them, without repeating the cloud clause.

Ezekiel 1:21
When they went, these went; when they stood, these stood; when they rose from the earth, these rose together with them, because a spirit of life was in the wheels.
  Ezekiel 10:16-17 — repeated movement, standing and rising together: Ezekiel lists the wheels' movement, standing and rising with the living creatures. The later cherubim account repeats these coordinated actions and the explanation that a spirit of life is in them.

Ezekiel 1:22
Above the living creatures’ heads was a likeness as of a firmament, like the appearance of crystal, stretched out above their wings;
  Exodus 24:10 — related firmament and luminous stone imagery in a divine vision: Ezekiel sees a crystal-like firmament above the creatures. Exodus describes the place beneath God's feet as sapphire brickwork resembling heaven's clear firmament; the shared visual vocabulary preserves the different materials and positions.

Ezekiel 1:23
and beneath the firmament their wings were stretched out, fluttering one toward another; each had two joined together, covering their bodies.
  Ezekiel 1:11 — same-chapter joined wings and bodily covering: Both descriptions mention joined wings and bodily covering. Ezekiel 1:11 distinguishes two joined wings from two covering the top of the bodies; Ezekiel 1:23 describes two joined together covering their bodies beneath the firmament, preserving the different descriptions.

Ezekiel 1:24
As they went, I heard the sound of their wings like the sound of much water; when they stood, their wings came to rest.
  Ezekiel 10:5 — repeated audible sound of the creatures' wings: Ezekiel hears the wings like much water as the creatures move. In the later vision the cherubim's wings are heard as far as the outer court, there compared with God Shaddai's speaking voice rather than water.

Ezekiel 1:25
And behold, a voice came from above the firmament that was over their heads.
  Ezekiel 10:1-2 — related speech within the vision above the cherubim: A voice comes from above the firmament over the creatures' heads. Ezekiel 10 again presents the throne above the firmament over the cherubim and then records a spoken command to the robed man, connecting speech with that repeated visionary setting.

Ezekiel 1:26
Like the appearance of a sapphire stone was the likeness of a throne upon it; and above the likeness of the throne was a likeness like the form of a human being.
  Ezekiel 10:1 — repeated sapphire-like throne above the firmament: Ezekiel describes a sapphire-like throne above the firmament, with a human-like form above it. Ezekiel 10 repeats the sapphire stone and throne likeness above the cherubim's firmament, without repeating the human-form clause in that unit.

Ezekiel 1:27
I saw something like the sight of electrum from the appearance of a waist upward; and from the appearance of a waist down below I saw something like the appearance of fire, with its brightness all around.
  Ezekiel 8:2 — repeated electrum above the waist and fire below: The visionary appearance is electrum-like from the waist upward and fire-like below. Ezekiel 8 repeats the likeness of a man with electrum above the waist and fire below, a close correspondence in material and bodily division.

Ezekiel 1:28
Like the appearance of a bow when it is in the cloud on a rainy day, so was the arrangement of the brightness all around. This was the appearance of the likeness of the Lord’s glory. I saw it, and I fall upon my face, and I heard the voice of One speaking.
  Genesis 9:13-16 — shared bow-in-cloud appearance with different stated functions: Ezekiel compares the surrounding brightness of the Lord's glory with a bow in a rainy cloud. Genesis describes God's bow appearing in the cloud as a covenant sign; the visual counterpart does not make Ezekiel's unit explicitly restate that covenant.
  Daniel 10:9-11 — related prostration and speech in a visionary encounter: Ezekiel falls upon his face after seeing the Lord's glory and hears a speaking voice. Daniel likewise reports falling face down before being raised to hear commands, but says he initially did not hear the speech; the auditory sequence is preserved.

Ezekiel 2:1
He said to me, “Son of man, stand on your feet, and I will speak to you.”
  Acts 26:16 — parallel commissioning command to stand on one's feet: Ezekiel is commanded to stand on his feet so that he may hear the speaker. In the Damascus-road account Jesus likewise commands Saul to rise and stand on his feet before appointing him a servant and witness, a particular commissioning posture in another encounter.

Ezekiel 2:2
A spirit came upon me, took me up, raised me, and set me on my feet; I heard Him speaking to me,
  Daniel 10:10-11 — related raising of a vision recipient to receive speech: A spirit raises Ezekiel and sets him on his feet as he hears the speaker. Daniel is likewise raised by an approaching hand and commanded to stand and understand the words, preserving the hand's action rather than calling it a spirit in Daniel.

Ezekiel 2:3
and He said to me, “Son of man, I am sending you out to the house of Israel, those who embitter Me, who have embittered Me, they and their fathers, until this present day,
  Isaiah 6:8-10 — parallel prophetic sending to a resistant people: Ezekiel is sent to Israel's house, described as embittering God across generations. Isaiah is likewise sent to a people whose heart, hearing and sight are unresponsive, connecting prophetic commission with resistant recipients while retaining each diagnosis.

Ezekiel 2:5
If indeed they listen or are terrified—for it is an embittering house—they will know that you are a prophet among them.
  Ezekiel 33:33 — repeated recognition that a prophet was among them: Ezekiel's commission says the people will know that a prophet is among them, with the condition worded as listening or being terrified. Ezekiel 33 likewise predicts their recognition that a prophet was among them when the announced event arrives.

Ezekiel 2:6
And you, son of man, do not fear them or be shaken before their faces, because they will rage and gather against you all around, and you dwell among scorpions. Do not fear their words or be shaken before their faces, for it is an embittering house.
  Jeremiah 1:17-19 — parallel assurance against fear amid prophetic opposition: Ezekiel must not fear the people's words or faces despite their surrounding hostility. Jeremiah likewise must rise, speak the commanded words and not fear his opponents, with assurance that their fighting will not prevail.

Ezekiel 2:7
You will speak My words to them, if indeed they listen or are terrified, for it is an embittering house.
  Jeremiah 33:2-3 — related command to deliver divine words despite uncertain response: Ezekiel must speak God's words, if indeed the people listen or are terrified. Jeremiah likewise must deliver all commanded words without subtraction, with the possibility that hearers may turn from evil; the response clauses differ and are not rewritten as a shared refusal formula.

Ezekiel 2:8
And you, son of man, hear the One speaking to you. Do not become embittering like the embittering house. Open your mouth wide and eat what I give you.”
  Ezekiel 3:1-2 — command to eat answered by the prophet's obedience: Ezekiel is told to open his mouth and eat what he is given instead of becoming embittering. The following chapter identifies the food as the scroll and records his opened mouth and being fed it, the particular enactment of this instruction.

Ezekiel 2:9
I looked, and behold, a hand was stretched toward me, and in it was a scroll of a book;
  Ezekiel 3:1-3 — presented scroll subsequently commanded as food: Ezekiel sees a hand stretched toward him with a scroll. Ezekiel 3 then commands him to eat the roll before addressing Israel and describes his being fed it and tasting its sweetness, following the use of this particular presented object.

Ezekiel 2:10
and He unrolled it before me. It had writing on the back and the front, and upon it were written a lament, a song and woe.
  Exodus 32:15-16 — shared writing on both sides of its material: Ezekiel's scroll is written on back and front with lament, song and woe. Exodus likewise describes God's writing on both sides of the testimony tablets; the medium and contents differ, while the unusual two-sided inscription corresponds.

Ezekiel 3:1
He said to me, “Son of man, eat this scroll, then go and speak to the sons of Israel.”
  Ezekiel 2:9-10 — identification of the scroll commanded as food: Ezekiel is told to eat the scroll before speaking to Israel. The preceding vision has shown the extended hand and unrolled, two-sided scroll bearing lament, song and woe, identifying the particular object now given as food.

Ezekiel 3:2
I opened my mouth wide, and He fed me the scroll.
  Ezekiel 2:8 — the prophet's opened mouth answers the eating command: Ezekiel opens his mouth and is fed the scroll. This enacts the earlier command to open his mouth and eat what is given, in contrast with becoming embittering like the people.

Ezekiel 3:3
He said to me, “Son of man, your mouth will eat, and your belly will be filled with this scroll that has been given to you.” I ate it, and in my mouth it became like sweet honey.
  Psalms 118:103 — shared sweetness of divine sayings compared with honey: The scroll given to fill Ezekiel's belly tastes like honey in his mouth. The psalm likewise calls God's sayings sweeter than honey and honeycomb to the mouth, applying the food comparison to divine speech rather than reporting a swallowed scroll.

Ezekiel 3:4
He said to me, “Son of man, go, enter the house of Israel, and speak My words to them;
  Jeremiah 1:7 — parallel sending to speak the words God commands: Ezekiel must go to Israel's house and speak God's words to them. Jeremiah likewise must go to everyone to whom God sends him and speak everything commanded, establishing the speaker's commission and the message's source.

Ezekiel 3:5
for you are not sent out to a people of deep speech and heavy tongue, but to the house of Israel,
  Ezekiel 3:6 — same-chapter explanation of the difficult speech Ezekiel is not sent to: Ezekiel is sent to Israel rather than a people of deep or heavy speech. The next unit develops that distinction as many peoples whose speech he would not understand, adding that they would listen if he were sent to them.

Ezekiel 3:6
nor to many peoples of foreign speech or foreign tongues, nor those strong in tongue whose words you will not understand. If I had sent you to such people, they would have listened to you.
  Matthew 12:41 — related contrast between foreign response and resistant hearers: Ezekiel is told that peoples of unintelligible speech would listen if he were sent to them, before Israel's refusal is stated. Jesus likewise contrasts Nineveh's actual repentance at Jonah's preaching with the generation before Him; this is a counterpart in responsiveness, not an identification of the foreign peoples.

Ezekiel 3:7
But the house of Israel certainly will not be willing to listen to you, because they do not wish to listen to Me; the entire house of Israel is contentious and hard-hearted.
  1 Samuel 8:7-8 — parallel rejection of God's messenger as rejection of God: Israel's house will not listen to Ezekiel because it will not listen to God. God likewise explains to Samuel that the people's treatment concerns His own rejected rule, and links their behavior toward Samuel with their longstanding abandonment of Him.

Ezekiel 3:8
Behold, I have made your face strong against their faces, and I will strengthen your contention against their contention,
  Jeremiah 1:18-19 — parallel strengthening of a prophet against hostile opponents: God strengthens Ezekiel's face and contention against those who resist him. Jeremiah is similarly made like a fortified city and strong bronze wall against rulers and people who will fight him, using different images for strength to withstand opposition.

Ezekiel 3:9
and it will always be stronger than rock. Do not fear them or be terrified before their faces, for it is an embittering house.”
  Isaiah 50:7 — shared rock imagery for steadfastness without fear or shame: Following the strengthening language of 3:8, God says that it will always be stronger than rock and forbids fear of the opponents' faces. Isaiah's speaker likewise sets his face like solid rock because the Lord helps him and he will not be ashamed; the comparison does not substitute a forehead or diamond into Ezekiel.

Ezekiel 3:10
He said to me, “Son of man, take into your heart all the words that I have spoken with you, and hear with your ears,
  Deuteronomy 6:6-7 — parallel inward reception of divine words before speaking them: Ezekiel must receive all God's words in his heart and hear them with his ears. Deuteronomy likewise places commanded words in heart and soul before directing their repeated teaching and speaking, linking inward attention with verbal transmission.

Ezekiel 3:11
and go, enter the captivity, to the sons of your people; you will speak to them and say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord,’ if indeed they listen, if indeed they yield.”
  Ezekiel 3:15 — same-chapter command to address the captives followed by arrival among them: Ezekiel is sent into captivity to his own people with the Lord's words. Ezekiel 3:15 then records his entry among the captives by Chebar and seven days living among them, giving the concrete arrival rather than inventing a different place name or silence.

Ezekiel 3:12
A spirit took me up, and I heard behind me the sound of a great shaking: “Blessed be the Lord’s glory from His place!”
  Isaiah 6:3-4 — related heavenly acclamation of the Lord's glory: As a spirit raises Ezekiel, he hears a great shaking and an acclamation blessing the Lord's glory from His place. Isaiah's seraphim likewise proclaim the Lord's holiness and earth-filling glory, with the lintel lifted at their voice; the settings' disturbances are described differently.

Ezekiel 3:13
I saw the sound of the living creatures’ wings fluttering one toward another, and the sound of the wheels adjoining them, and the sound of the shaking.
  Ezekiel 1:24 — repeated description of the creatures' wing sound: Ezekiel 3 describes the sound of fluttering wings, wheels and great shaking, using the wording that he saw the sound. Ezekiel 1 had described hearing the wings like much water, supplying the earlier audible feature without normalizing the later sensory wording.

Ezekiel 3:14
The spirit raised me and took me up. I went, impelled by my spirit, and the Lord’s hand came strongly upon me.
  Ezekiel 8:3 — parallel lifting and transport by a spirit: Ezekiel is raised and taken up by the spirit, impelled by his spirit with the Lord's hand strong upon him. Ezekiel 8 likewise depicts a spirit lifting him between earth and heaven and bringing him in a vision to Jerusalem; the source does not add bitterness to this movement.

Ezekiel 3:15
I entered the captivity aloft and went around those dwelling by the River Chebar, those who were there; I sat there seven days, living among them.
  Ezekiel 3:11 — same-chapter arrival at the commanded destination among captives: Ezekiel enters among the captives and lives among the Chebar inhabitants for seven days. This supplies the destination previously commanded when he was sent into captivity to his people, without importing a statement that he sat silent.

Ezekiel 3:17
“Son of man, I have appointed you a watchman for the house of Israel. You will hear a word from My mouth and convey My warning to them.
  Ezekiel 33:7 — repeated appointment as Israel's watchman: Both passages appoint the son of man as watchman for Israel's house and say he will hear a word from God's mouth. Ezekiel 3:17 explicitly adds conveying the warning, which the later chapter develops in its following units.

Ezekiel 3:18
When I say to the lawless person, ‘You will surely be put to death,’ and you do not warn him or speak to warn the lawless person to turn from his ways so that he may live, that lawless person will die in his injustice; but I will require his blood from your hand.
  Ezekiel 33:8 — repeated blood responsibility for an unwarned lawless person: Both warnings distinguish the lawless person's death in wrongdoing from the watchman's additional responsibility when he fails to warn. In each, God requires the person's blood from the watchman's hand.
  Acts 20:26-27 — related freedom from blood responsibility through full proclamation: Ezekiel is held responsible for blood when he withholds the warning that could call a lawless person back. Paul conversely declares himself clean from everyone's blood because he did not withhold God's whole purpose, connecting faithful proclamation with freedom from that liability.

Ezekiel 3:19
But if you warn the lawless person and he does not turn from his lawlessness and his way, that lawless person will die in his injustice, while you will rescue your life.
  Ezekiel 33:9 — repeated distinction between a refused warning and the watchman's rescued life: Both passages say that a warned lawless person who does not turn dies in wrongdoing, while the messenger has rescued his own life. The watchman's responsibility is discharged by warning, without a guarantee that the hearer repents.

Ezekiel 3:20
When a righteous person turns from his righteous deeds and commits a transgression, and I place torment before his face, he will die because you did not warn him. He will die in his sins, because the righteous deeds he did certainly will not be remembered, and I will require his blood from your hand.
  Ezekiel 33:13 — parallel former righteousness not remembered after wrongdoing: Ezekiel 3 says the righteous person's former deeds are not remembered when he turns to injustice and dies, also addressing the watchman's failure. Ezekiel 33 likewise says prior righteous deeds will not be remembered if the person does lawlessness, without repeating the watchman's blood liability in that unit.

Ezekiel 3:21
But if you warn the righteous person not to sin, and he does not sin, the righteous person will surely live because you warned him; and you will rescue your own life.”
  1 Timothy 4:16 — related saving of both the teacher and attentive hearer: A warning that keeps the righteous person from sin preserves that person's life and rescues the watchman's own life. First Timothy likewise links continued attention to oneself and the teaching with saving both oneself and one's hearers; the forms of instruction and situations differ.

Ezekiel 3:22
The Lord’s hand came upon me, and He said to me, “Rise and go out to the plain, and you will be spoken to there.”
  Ezekiel 37:1-3 — repeated Lord's hand and revelation in the plain: The Lord's hand comes upon Ezekiel and he is directed to the plain for speech. Ezekiel 37 again begins with the Lord's hand, transport in spirit to a plain and a spoken question, there concerning the bones seen in it.

Ezekiel 3:23
I rose and went out to the plain, and behold, the Lord’s glory stood there, like the vision and like the glory I had seen by the River Chebar; and I fall upon my face.

A ruler will clothe himself in devastation, and the hands of the people of the land will be paralyzed. According to their ways I will deal with them, and by their judgments I will exact retribution upon them; they will know that I am the Lord.’”
  Jeremiah 17:10 — parallel divine repayment according to people's ways: After describing the ruler's devastation and people's paralyzed hands, Ezekiel says God will deal with them according to their ways and exact retribution by their judgments. Jeremiah likewise states that God gives each according to his ways and the fruits of his practices, without repeating Ezekiel's social figures or bodily reaction.

Ezekiel 8:1
In the sixth year, in the fifth month, on the fifth of the month, I was sitting in the house, and Judah’s elders were sitting before me. The Lord’s hand came upon me,
  Ezekiel 14:1-2 — repeated elders sitting before Ezekiel when revelation comes: Judah's elders sit before Ezekiel in his house when the Lord's hand comes upon him. Ezekiel 14 similarly opens with Israel's elders sitting before him and the Lord's word arriving, while omitting this unit's year and month details.

Ezekiel 8:2
and I saw, and behold, the likeness of a man: from His waist downward, fire, and from His waist upward, like the appearance of electrum.
  Ezekiel 1:27 — repeated visionary division into fire and electrum: The human likeness has fire below the waist and electrum above. The Chebar vision gives the same division of appearance at the waist, placing this later sight in the book's established visual vocabulary.

Ezekiel 8:3
He stretched out the likeness of a hand and lifted me by the crown of my head. A spirit lifted me between earth and heaven and brought me to Jerusalem in a vision of God, to the entrance of the inner gate facing north, where the pillar of the one acquiring stood.
  Ezekiel 11:24 — parallel transport by a spirit within a divine vision: A spirit lifts Ezekiel between earth and heaven and brings him to Jerusalem in a vision. Ezekiel 11 likewise describes a spirit lifting him and bringing him to the Chaldeans' land among the captives in a vision, before he rises out of it; the destinations differ.

Ezekiel 8:4
And behold, the glory of the Lord God of Israel was there, in accordance with the vision I had seen in the plain.
  Ezekiel 3:23 — explicit recurrence of the glory seen in the plain: Ezekiel recognizes the Lord God's glory as in the vision seen in the plain. Ezekiel 3 records that sight in the plain and itself compares it with Chebar, supplying the stated earlier encounter.

Ezekiel 8:6
He said to me, “Son of man, have you seen what these people do? They commit great lawlessness here, to keep away from My holy places. You will yet see greater lawlessness.”
  Jeremiah 7:30 — related lawlessness associated with God's holy place: Ezekiel exposes great lawlessness in the sanctuary vision, wording its relation to God's holy places as keeping away from them. Jeremiah likewise condemns abominations placed in the house bearing God's name to defile it; the comparison does not replace Ezekiel's keeping-away clause with a claim that God is driven out.

Ezekiel 8:8
and said to me, “Son of man, dig.” I dug, and behold, there was a door.
  Ezekiel 8:9-12 — same-chapter discovered door leading to the concealed idolatrous scene: Digging reveals a door. The following command to enter leads to the sight of depicted idols and elders with incense, then the explanation about their hidden bedchambers, identifying the particular scene the opening reveals.

Ezekiel 8:9
He said to me, “Go in and see the lawlessness these people are committing here.”
  Ezekiel 8:10-12 — same-chapter command to inspect lawlessness answered by its description: Ezekiel is told to enter and see the lawlessness being done. His entry reveals the depicted idols and elders with censers, followed by an explanation of their hidden practices and assertion that the Lord does not see.

Ezekiel 8:10
I went in and saw, and behold, vain abominations and all the idols of the house of Israel were drawn upon it all around,
  Deuteronomy 4:15-16 — related prohibition of made religious likenesses: Ezekiel sees vain abominations and Israel's idols drawn all around. Deuteronomy warns against making a graven likeness or image after Israel saw no likeness at Horeb; the representation's technique differs, and Ezekiel's unit does not enumerate animals.

Ezekiel 8:11
and there were seventy men from the elders of the house of Israel. Jaazaniah son of Shaphan stood among them before their faces; each had his censer in his hand, and the vapor of incense rose.
  Exodus 24:9-10 — contrast between seventy elders in holy revelation and an idolatrous scene: Ezekiel's seventy elders appear amid the depicted idols, each with a censer as incense rises. Exodus describes seventy elders ascending with Moses and seeing the place where God stood; these are different groups of elders and contrasting religious settings, not an identification of the same men.

Ezekiel 8:12
He said to me, “Son of man, have you seen what the elders of the house of Israel do, each in their hidden bedchamber? For they said, ‘The Lord does not see; the Lord has abandoned the land.’”
  Psalms 93:7-9 — parallel claim that the Lord does not see: The elders justify their concealed practices by saying the Lord does not see and has abandoned the land. The psalm likewise reports the claim that the Lord will not see and answers it by asking whether the maker of the eye fails to perceive; it does not repeat the abandonment claim.
  Isaiah 29:15 — parallel secret conduct defended by assumed invisibility: Ezekiel exposes each elder's hidden bedchamber and the claim that God does not see. Isaiah likewise condemns secret counsel and deeds in darkness whose practitioners ask who has seen or knows them, specifically paralleling the concealment that accompanies the false confidence.

Ezekiel 8:16
He brought me into the inner court of the Lord’s house, and behold, at the entrance of the Lord’s temple, between the porches and the altar, were about twenty men, their backs toward the Lord’s temple and their faces opposite. These were bowing to the sun.
  Deuteronomy 4:19 — legal prohibition corresponding to the visionary sun worship: About twenty men have their backs toward the Lord's temple while bowing to the sun. Deuteronomy expressly warns against seeing the sun and other heavenly bodies and being led to bow to and serve them, without specifying this temple scene or its participants.
  2 Kings 23:5 — historical suppression of sun worship compared with its visionary presence: Ezekiel sees men bowing to the sun in the Lord's inner court. Kings describes the king putting an end to those burning incense to the sun and other heavenly objects in Judah and around Jerusalem, contrasting suppression with the visionary practice while preserving incense versus bowing.

Ezekiel 8:17
He said to me, “Have you seen, son of man? Is it a small thing for the house of Judah to commit the lawlessness they have committed here? For they have filled the land with lawlessness, and behold, they are as though sneering.
  Jeremiah 7:9-11 — related religious wrongdoing joined with broader lawlessness: Ezekiel joins the practices exposed in the sanctuary with Judah's filling the land with lawlessness and acting as though sneering. Jeremiah likewise addresses crimes and foreign-god worship together with standing in the Lord's house, naming particular offenses that Ezekiel's unit leaves general.

Ezekiel 8:18
I too will act toward them in wrath. My eye will not spare, nor will I have mercy.”
  Ezekiel 5:11 — repeated refusal to spare after profanation of holy places: The sanctuary vision ends with God's wrath, His eye not sparing and His refusal of mercy. Ezekiel 5 likewise grounds the same refusal in the people's defilement of His holy places with abominations, connecting the repeated judgment formula to that particular offense.

Ezekiel 9:1
He cried into my ears with a loud voice, saying, “The city’s retribution has drawn near.” Each had the implements of destruction in his hand.
  Ezekiel 7:2-4 — judgment_announcement: The announcement that the city's retribution has drawn near takes up the earlier announcement that Israel's end has arrived and its day has drawn near. Both place the threatened judgment at hand.

Ezekiel 9:2
And behold, six men came from the direction of the high gate facing north, each with an axe in his hand. One man among them wore a full-length robe, with a sapphire belt at his waist. They entered and stood beside the bronze altar.
  Ezekiel 10:6-7 — narrative_continuation: The man distinguished among the executioners by his full-length robe reappears as the robed recipient of fire from among the cherubim. The later scene continues his commissioned service within the temple vision.

Ezekiel 9:3
The glory of the God of Israel, which had been upon the cherubim, rose from them to the forecourt of the house. He called the man wearing the full-length robe, who had the belt at his waist,
  Ezekiel 10:4 — parallel_vision: Both descriptions move the divine glory from the cherubim to the house's forecourt. The later scene also describes the cloud filling the house and the court's radiance.

Ezekiel 9:4
and said to him, “Pass through the midst of Jerusalem and put the sign upon the foreheads of the men who sigh deeply and grieve over all the lawlessness being committed within her.”
  Exodus 12:13 — protective_sign_parallel: The forehead sign distinguishes those grieving Jerusalem's lawlessness. Exodus likewise makes a visible sign the ground for protection during judgment, although its sign is blood upon houses rather than a mark upon persons.

Ezekiel 9:5
To these others He said while I listened, “Go behind him into the city and strike. Do not spare with your eyes, and have no mercy.
  Ezekiel 7:8 — announced_judgment_and_execution: The order that the agents must not spare or have mercy implements the earlier divine declaration that His eye would not spare and He would not have mercy. Both belong to the judgment on Israel's conduct.

Ezekiel 9:6
Old man, young man, maiden, infants and women—kill them to obliteration. But do not approach anyone upon whom the sign is. Begin at My holy places.” They began with the elderly men who were inside the house.
  1 Peter 4:17 — judgment_beginning_at_gods_house: The execution begins at the holy places with the elders inside the house. Peter likewise says judgment begins from God's house, applying that starting point to the believing community's situation.
  Exodus 12:23 — protective_sign_parallel: The agents are forbidden to approach people bearing the sign. Exodus similarly forbids the destroyer entry to houses whose doorways bear the blood sign; the protected objects and signs differ.

Ezekiel 9:7
He said to them, “Defile the house and fill the roads with the dead as you go out, and strike.”
  Psalms 78:1-3 — temple_and_city_desecration: The vision orders the house defiled and the roads filled with dead. The psalm laments a defiled temple, exposed servants' bodies and blood poured around Jerusalem, giving a corresponding picture of desecrated sanctuary and city.

Ezekiel 9:8
As they struck, I fall upon my face and cried aloud and said, “Alas, Lord! Art Thou obliterating the survivors of Israel as Thou pourest out Thy wrath upon Jerusalem?”
  Ezekiel 11:13 — prophetic_intercession_parallel: Ezekiel falls on his face and cries out over the destruction of Israel's survivors. He repeats that posture and cry when Pelatiah dies, again fearing that the survivors are being brought to an end.

Ezekiel 9:9
He said to me, “The injustice of the house of Israel and Judah has grown very, very great, because the land is full of many peoples, and the city full of injustice and uncleanness. For they said, ‘The Lord has abandoned the land; the Lord does not look upon it.’
  Ezekiel 8:12 — repeated_indictment: The explanation for judgment repeats the elders' claim that the Lord does not see and has abandoned the land. Chapter 8 records that claim within the concealed practices exposed by the vision.

Ezekiel 9:10
My eye will not spare, nor will I have mercy. I have brought their ways upon their heads.”
  Ezekiel 7:8 — repeated_judgment_formula: Both declarations join refusal to spare or show mercy with repayment of the people's own way. The later statement brings their ways upon their heads, while the earlier locates their abominations among them.

Ezekiel 9:11
And behold, the man wearing the full-length robe, with the belt fastened at his waist, answered, saying, “I have done as Thou didst command me.”
  Ezekiel 9:3-4 — same_chapter_command_and_completion: The robed man's report that he has done as commanded answers the earlier call and instruction to mark the grieving inhabitants. The repeated garment identifies the commissioned figure within the same scene.

Ezekiel 10:1
I saw, and behold, above the firmament over the cherubim’s head was something like sapphire stone, a throne’s likeness above them.
  Ezekiel 1:26 — parallel_throne_vision: The sapphire-like throne above the cherubim recalls the earlier sapphire throne above the firmament. Both descriptions place the throne above the visionary creatures.

Ezekiel 10:2
He said to the man wearing the robe, “Go in among the wheels beneath the cherubim, fill your hands with burning coals from among the cherubim, and scatter them over the city.” He went in before me.
  2 Kings 25:9-10 — symbolic_judgment_and_narrative_parallel: Scattering burning coals over the city portrays fiery judgment. Kings recounts the chief cook burning the Lord's house, the royal house and Jerusalem's houses; the comparison is between the visionary fire action and the narrated destruction.

Ezekiel 10:3
The cherubim stood to the right of the house as the man entered, and the cloud filled the inner court.
  1 Kings 8:10-11 — temple_cloud_parallel: The cloud filling the inner court recalls the cloud that filled the temple at its dedication. Kings explicitly associates that cloud with the Lord's glory and the priests' inability to continue serving.

Ezekiel 10:4
The Lord’s glory departed from the cherubim to the forecourt of the house. The cloud filled the house, and the court filled with the radiance of the Lord’s glory.
  1 Kings 8:10-11 — temple_glory_parallel: Both passages describe the house filled by cloud and divine glory. Ezekiel places this manifestation within the glory's movement toward the forecourt, whereas Kings describes the temple's dedication.

Ezekiel 10:5
The sound of the cherubim’s wings was heard as far as the outer court, like the voice of God Shaddai speaking.
  Ezekiel 1:24 — parallel_vision_with_different_comparisons: Both visions describe the audible sound of the creatures' wings. Here it resembles God Shaddai speaking and reaches the outer court; the earlier account compares the sound to much water.

Ezekiel 10:6
When He commanded the man wearing the holy robe, saying, “Take fire from among the wheels, from among the cherubim,” he entered and stood beside the wheels,
  Ezekiel 10:2 — same_chapter_command_and_execution: The repeated order to take fire from among the wheels recalls the earlier instruction to enter beneath the cherubim and obtain burning coals. The man now stands beside the wheels in response.

Ezekiel 10:7
and he stretched out his hand into the midst of the fire among the cherubim, took it and gave it into the hands of the man wearing the holy robe. He took it and went out.
  Ezekiel 10:2 — same_chapter_command_and_execution: Fire is taken from among the cherubim and placed in the robed man's hands. This supplies the coals commanded earlier for the action over the city; the explanation does not assign the unstated hand's owner.

Ezekiel 10:8
I saw the cherubim, the likeness of human hands beneath their wings.
  Ezekiel 1:8-9 — parallel_creature_description: The human hands beneath the cherubim's wings correspond to the human hand beneath the earlier creatures' wings. The connection rests on that bodily feature within the two linked visions.

Ezekiel 10:9
I saw, and behold, four wheels stood beside the cherubim, one wheel beside one cherub. The wheels’ appearance was like the appearance of a carbuncle stone.
  Ezekiel 1:15-16 — parallel_wheel_description: Both scenes associate the creatures with wheels and describe their stone-like appearance. The later scene explicitly places one wheel beside each cherub and uses carbuncle, while the earlier description uses tharsis.

Ezekiel 10:10
Their appearance was one likeness for all four, as when a wheel is within a wheel.
  Ezekiel 1:16 — parallel_wheel_description: Both accounts say the four wheels share one likeness and describe a wheel within a wheel. This is a particular repeated feature of the visionary vehicle.

Ezekiel 10:11
As they went, they went toward their four directions; they did not turn as they went. For wherever the one leading part looked, they went, and they did not turn as they went.
  Ezekiel 1:17-18 — parallel_wheel_movement: The wheels move in four directions without turning in both accounts. The later account adds their movement toward the direction faced by the leading part.

Ezekiel 10:12
Their backs, their hands, their wings, and the wheels were full of eyes all around, upon their four wheels.
  Ezekiel 1:18 — parallel_vision_with_expanded_detail: Both descriptions place eyes all around the visionary forms. The earlier unit speaks of the backs of all four; the later description includes backs, hands, wings and wheels, so their distribution is preserved separately.

Ezekiel 10:15
The cherubim lifted themselves. This was the living creature I had seen at the river Chebar.
  Ezekiel 1:5-6 — explicit_vision_identification: Ezekiel identifies the rising cherubim as the living creature seen at Chebar. The opening vision introduces the four living creatures with human likeness and four faces, providing the earlier scene to which he refers.

Ezekiel 10:16
When the cherubim went, the wheels went beside them. When the cherubim raised their wings to rise from the earth, their wheels did not turn.
  Ezekiel 1:19 — parallel_creature_and_wheel_movement: In both visions the wheels accompany the creatures as they move and rise from the earth. The later scene adds that the wheels did not turn when the cherubim raised their wings.

Ezekiel 10:17
When they stood, these stood; when they rose, these rose with them, because a spirit of life was in them.
  Ezekiel 1:20-21 — parallel_creature_and_wheel_movement: Both accounts synchronize the standing and rising of creatures and wheels and explain their movement by a spirit of life. The earlier account explicitly locates that spirit in the wheels.

Ezekiel 10:18
The Lord’s glory went out from the house and mounted upon the cherubim,
  Ezekiel 11:22-23 — glory_departure_sequence: The glory leaves the house and mounts the cherubim here. The following chapter continues its departure from the city to the mountain opposite it, tracing the successive locations of the same glory.

Ezekiel 10:19
and the cherubim lifted their wings and rose from the earth before me as they went out, with the wheels beside them. They stood at the entrance of the opposite gate of the Lord’s house, and the glory of the God of Israel was above them.
  Ezekiel 11:1 — connected_vision_location: The cherubim and glory stand at the opposite gate of the Lord's house. The next scene brings Ezekiel to that opposite gate and specifies its eastward orientation.

Ezekiel 10:20
This is the living creature I had seen beneath the God of Israel at the river Chebar, and I knew that it was cherubim.
  Ezekiel 1:1-6 — explicit_vision_identification: Ezekiel expressly identifies these cherubim with the creature beneath Israel's God at Chebar. The first chapter locates his divine vision by that river and introduces the living creatures within it.

Ezekiel 10:21
Each had four faces, each had eight wings, and the likeness of human hands was beneath their wings.
  Ezekiel 1:6-9 — parallel_vision_with_variant_description: Both descriptions give the creatures four faces and human hands beneath their wings. The supplied counts differ: this unit says eight wings each, while the earlier unit says four; neither count is harmonized.

Ezekiel 10:22
As for the likeness of their faces, these are the faces I had seen beneath the glory of the God of Israel at the river Chebar; each of them went straight ahead.
  Ezekiel 1:8-12 — explicit_vision_identification: The later faces are explicitly identified with those seen at Chebar, and the straight-ahead movement recurs. The earlier passage describes the faces and repeats that the creatures do not turn as they go.

Ezekiel 11:1
A spirit lifted me and brought me to the opposite gate of the Lord’s house, facing east. And behold, at the gate entrance were about twenty-five men. Among them I saw Jaazaniah son of Ezer and Pelatiah son of Benaiah, leaders of the people.
  Ezekiel 8:3 — visionary_transport_parallel: A spirit lifts Ezekiel and brings him to a temple gate in each scene. The earlier transport reaches the inner gate facing north, while this scene identifies the opposite gate facing east.

Ezekiel 11:2
The Lord said to me, “Son of man, these are the men who devise vain things and counsel wicked counsel in this city,
  Micah 2:1-2 — planned_wickedness: The city's leaders are accused of devising vain things and counseling wicked counsel. Micah likewise condemns people who plan evil before carrying it out, naming their coveting of fields and their oppression and plundering of households and inheritance.

Ezekiel 11:3
who say, ‘Have the houses not been built recently? She is the cauldron, and we are the meat.’
  Ezekiel 24:3-6 — reused_cauldron_image: The leaders call the city a cauldron and themselves meat. The later parable uses a cauldron containing cuts of meat against the city of bloodshed, turning the same kind of image into a judgment scene.

Ezekiel 11:5
The Lord’s spirit fell upon me, and He said to me, “Say, ‘Thus says the Lord: You have said this, house of Israel, and I understand the deliberations of your spirit.
  1 Chronicles 28:9 — divine_knowledge_of_thoughts: The Lord declares that He understands Israel's inner deliberations. David's charge to Solomon likewise says the Lord examines every heart and knows every thought, grounding both passages in divine knowledge of inward motives.

Ezekiel 11:6
You have multiplied your dead in this city and filled her roads with the wounded.
  Ezekiel 22:3-4 — city_bloodguilt: The leaders have multiplied the city's dead and filled its roads with wounded people. Chapter 22 similarly indicts the city for shedding blood within itself and becoming guilty through that bloodshed.

Ezekiel 11:7
Therefore thus says the Lord: Your dead whom you struck within her are the meat, and she is the cauldron; but I will bring you out of her.
  Ezekiel 24:3-6 — reused_cauldron_image: God identifies the city's dead as its meat and the city as their cauldron. The later cauldron parable explicitly addresses the city of bloodshed and its cooked pieces, continuing the association of the vessel with the guilty city.

Ezekiel 11:8
You fear the sword, and I will bring a sword upon you, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 49:16 — feared_judgment_overtakes_people: Both oracles say that the very sword feared by the hearers will come upon them. Jeremiah directs the warning to people seeking safety in Egypt; Ezekiel addresses the city's leaders.

Ezekiel 11:9
I will bring you out of her midst, hand you into foreigners’ hands, and execute judgments among you.
  Jeremiah 52:24-27 — judgment_and_narrative_counterpart: The threatened removal into foreigners' hands for judgment has a narrative counterpart in Jerusalem's priests, officials and people being taken to Babylon's king and struck at Deblatha. The passages describe judgment following removal from the city.

Ezekiel 11:11
She will not be a cauldron for you, and you certainly will not become meat within her. At Israel’s borders I will judge you,
  Ezekiel 11:7 — same_chapter_reversal_of_claim: The city will not be a cauldron with these leaders as its meat. The earlier correction has already assigned that image to their victims and announced that the leaders themselves would be brought out.

Ezekiel 11:13
As I prophesied, Pelatiah son of Benaiah died. I fall upon my face and cried aloud with a loud voice and said, “Alas, alas, Lord! Thou art bringing Israel’s survivors to a complete end!”
  Ezekiel 9:8 — prophetic_intercession_parallel: Pelatiah's death prompts Ezekiel to fall on his face and cry over the possible end of Israel's survivors. His earlier response to the city's slaughter uses the same posture and concern for the remnant.

Ezekiel 11:15
“Son of man, your brothers and the men of your captivity and the whole house of Israel have been brought to an end. To them Jerusalem’s inhabitants said, ‘Keep far from the Lord; the land has been given to us as an inheritance.’
  Ezekiel 33:24 — contested_land_possession: Jerusalem's inhabitants claim the land while excluding the captives. The later inhabitants of devastated places likewise claim possession, arguing from Abraham and their greater numbers; both speeches assert entitlement within judgment settings.

Ezekiel 11:16
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: I will thrust them away into the nations and scatter them through all the earth, and I will be a little sanctuary for them in the countries where they may enter.’
  Psalms 89:1-2 — god_as_refuge: God promises to be a little sanctuary to those scattered abroad. Moses' prayer likewise calls the Lord the people's refuge through every generation, locating shelter in God Himself rather than a particular dwelling place.

Ezekiel 11:17
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: I will receive them from the nations and gather them from the countries where I scattered them, and I will give them the land of Israel.
  Ezekiel 36:24 — restoration_promise: Both promises gather the scattered people from the nations and lands and bring them into their own land. The later formulation restates the return promised to the displaced people here.

Ezekiel 11:18
They will enter there and remove all her abominations and all her lawlessness from her.
  Ezekiel 37:23 — restoration_and_cleansing: Removal of the land's abominations accompanies the people's return. The later restoration similarly ends defilement by idols and joins deliverance from lawless deeds with cleansing.

Ezekiel 11:19
I will give them another heart and put a new spirit within them. I will tear the stone heart from their flesh and give them a heart of flesh,
  Ezekiel 36:26 — renewed_heart_and_spirit: Both promises replace the heart of stone with a heart of flesh and give a new spirit. The earlier says another heart, while the later says a new heart, preserving each formulation.

Ezekiel 11:20
so that they may walk in My commands, keep My ordinances and carry them out. They will be My people, and I will be their God.
  Ezekiel 36:27-28 — renewal_for_covenant_obedience: Both passages connect inner renewal with keeping God's ordinances and conclude that the people will be His and He will be their God. The later passage explicitly says His Spirit enables that obedience.

Ezekiel 11:21
As their heart went toward the heart of their abominations and their lawlessness, I have brought their ways upon their heads, says the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 9:10 — retribution_for_own_ways: God brings the people's ways upon their heads in both judgments. Here the explanation names hearts directed toward abominations and lawlessness; the earlier verdict accompanies the city's execution scene.

Ezekiel 11:22
The cherubim raised their wings, with the wheels beside them, and the glory of the God of Israel was upon them, above them.
  Ezekiel 10:19 — continued_glory_departure: The cherubim again raise their wings with wheels beside them and the divine glory above them. The preceding scene gives the same arrangement as they rise from the earth toward the gate.

Ezekiel 11:23
The Lord’s glory rose from the midst of the city and stood upon the mountain opposite the city.
  Ezekiel 43:1-5 — glory_departure_and_return: The glory leaves the city and stands on the opposite mountain. In the later vision it comes by the eastern gate and fills the house again, creating a departure-and-return correspondence without naming the earlier mountain.

Ezekiel 11:24
A spirit lifted me and brought me into the Chaldeans’ land, to the captives, in a vision by God’s spirit. I rose out of the vision I had seen,
  Ezekiel 8:3 — return_from_visionary_journey: The Spirit brings Ezekiel back to the captives in Chaldea as the vision ends. The earlier transport had brought him to Jerusalem in a divine vision, supplying the outward movement answered by this return.

Ezekiel 11:25
and I spoke to the captives all the Lord’s words that He had shown me.
  Ezekiel 2:7 — commission_and_execution: Ezekiel reports to the captives all the Lord's words shown to him. This carries out his commission to speak God's words to the embittering house.

Ezekiel 12:2
“Son of man, you dwell in the midst of their injustices. They have eyes to see and do not see, and ears to hear and do not hear, because they are an embittering house.
  Jeremiah 5:21 — unresponsive_senses: Both indict the people for having eyes without seeing and ears without hearing. Ezekiel associates that refusal with the embittering house, while Jeremiah calls his audience foolish and senseless.

Ezekiel 12:3
And you, son of man, get your belongings ready for captivity by day before them. You will be taken captive from your place to another place before them, so that they may see, because they are an embittering house.
  Ezekiel 12:11 — same_chapter_sign_and_interpretation: The commanded preparation of captivity belongings is interpreted later as a portent of the people's deportation. The explanation directly connects Ezekiel's enacted departure with what will happen to them.

Ezekiel 12:4
You will bring your belongings out like belongings for captivity by day before their eyes, and you yourself will go out in the evening as a captive goes out.
  Ezekiel 12:7 — same_chapter_command_and_execution: The command distinguishes bringing belongings out by day and departing in the evening. Ezekiel's report reproduces that sequence as he performs the sign before the people.

Ezekiel 12:5
Before them, dig for yourself through the wall, and you will pass out through it.
  Ezekiel 12:12 — same_chapter_sign_and_interpretation: Digging through the wall in the enacted sign corresponds to the ruler's predicted concealed departure through the wall. The later unit explains the public action in terms of the ruler's fate.

Ezekiel 12:6
Before them you will be lifted upon shoulders and go out concealed. Cover your face, and you certainly will not see the land, for I have made you a portent to the house of Israel.”
  Ezekiel 12:12 — same_chapter_sign_and_interpretation: Being lifted upon shoulders, departing concealed and covering the face recur in the prediction concerning the ruler. Both units also state that the covered person will not see the land.

Ezekiel 12:7
I did so according to all that He commanded me. I brought belongings out like belongings for captivity by day; in the evening I dug through the wall for myself and went out concealed. I was lifted upon shoulders before them.
  Ezekiel 24:24 — prophetic_enactment_parallel: Ezekiel performs the commanded deportation sign before the people. A later symbolic action is likewise explained by saying Ezekiel is their portent and that what he has done will happen to them, though the enacted actions differ.

Ezekiel 12:9
“Son of man, has not the house of Israel, the embittering house, said to you, ‘What are you doing?’
  Ezekiel 24:19 — audience_questions_prophetic_action: The audience asks what Ezekiel is doing in both scenes. Each question arises from a visible prophetic action and opens the way to its interpretation, rather than being a request about an ordinary task.

Ezekiel 12:11
Say, ‘I perform portents in her midst. As I have done, so it will be for them; in deportation and captivity they will go.’
  2 Kings 25:11 — deportation_prediction_and_narrative: The sign announces that the people will go into deportation and captivity. Kings recounts the removal of the remaining city population and those who had gone over to Babylon's king.

Ezekiel 12:12
The ruler among them will be lifted upon shoulders and go out concealed through the wall. He will dig through it to go out through it. He will cover his face so that he may not be seen by an eye, and he himself will not see the land.
  Jeremiah 52:7-9 — rulers_concealed_departure: The ruler's concealed exit has a narrative counterpart in the king's nighttime flight from the breached city and subsequent capture. Jeremiah specifies a gate between walls; it does not reproduce every feature of Ezekiel's wall-digging sign.

Ezekiel 12:13
I will spread My net over him, and he will be caught in My enclosure. I will bring him to Babylon, to the Chaldeans’ land, and he will not see it, and there he will die.
  Jeremiah 52:8-11 — capture_and_unseen_babylon: The ruler is to be caught, brought to Babylon, unable to see it, and die there. Jeremiah narrates Zedekiah's capture, blinding and removal to Babylon, where he remained in a mill house until his death.

Ezekiel 12:14
All those around him, his helpers and all who support him, I will scatter to every wind; I will draw a sword after them.
  Jeremiah 52:8 — scattered_royal_supporters: The threat scatters the ruler's helpers and supporters. Jeremiah's account of the king's capture explicitly says all his servants scattered away from him.

Ezekiel 12:15
They will know that I am the Lord when I scatter them among the nations, and I will disperse them among the countries.
  Leviticus 26:33 — covenant_judgment_of_scattering: Both passages announce scattering among the nations and lands as divine judgment. Leviticus places that dispersal among the covenant penalties, alongside sword and desolation.

Ezekiel 12:16
I will spare a few men among them from sword, famine and death, so that they may recount all their lawlessness among the nations where they entered, and they will know that I am the Lord.”
  Ezekiel 6:8-10 — survivors_among_the_nations: Both passages preserve survivors of judgment among the nations and connect their survival with acknowledgment of the Lord and their abominations. Here they recount their lawlessness; the earlier passage emphasizes remembrance and grief.

Ezekiel 12:18
“Son of man, eat your bread with pain, and drink your water with torment and distress,
  Ezekiel 4:16-17 — distressed_bread_and_water: The enacted eating and drinking in pain and distress corresponds to the earlier announcement of Jerusalem's measured bread and water in need and devastation. Both make bodily deprivation part of the siege warning.

Ezekiel 12:19
and say to the people of the land, ‘Thus says the Lord to Jerusalem’s inhabitants upon Israel’s land: They will eat their bread with want and drink their water with devastation, so that the land may be destroyed with its fullness, for all who dwell in it are in impiety.
  Leviticus 26:26 — bread_in_deprivation: Jerusalem's inhabitants will eat bread in want. The covenant warning similarly describes scarce bread distributed by weight and eaten without satisfaction; Ezekiel also includes distressed water and the land's devastation.

Ezekiel 12:20
Their inhabited cities will be made desolate, and the land will be laid waste. You will recognize that I am the Lord.’”
  Leviticus 26:31-33 — desolated_cities_and_land: Both announcements join desolate cities with a wasted land. Leviticus supplies the covenant-warning setting, while Ezekiel declares the coming condition of the inhabited cities.

Ezekiel 12:22
“Son of man, what is this proverb you have concerning Israel’s land, saying, ‘The days are far off; vision has perished’?
  Ezekiel 12:27 — same_chapter_delayed_vision_claim: The proverb about distant days and a perished vision is restated as the claim that Ezekiel speaks of far-off times. The two formulations expose the audience's dismissal of the warning's immediacy.

Ezekiel 12:23
Therefore say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: I will turn back this proverb, and the house of Israel certainly will no longer say this proverb.’ For you will speak to them: ‘The days have drawn near, and the word of every vision,
  Habakkuk 2:3 — assured_fulfillment_of_vision: Ezekiel announces that the days and the word of every vision have drawn near. Habakkuk likewise speaks of an appointed vision and says that he who comes will come and not linger, while instructing the hearer to wait if he delays.

Ezekiel 12:24
because there will no longer be any false vision or anyone divining pleasing things among the sons of Israel,
  Zechariah 13:2 — removal_of_false_prophecy: The end of false visions and pleasing divination corresponds to Zechariah's promise to remove false prophets and the unclean spirit from the land. Both anticipate the removal of deceptive religious speech.

Ezekiel 12:25
because I, the Lord, will speak My words; I will speak and act, and I certainly will delay no longer. For in your days, embittering house, I will speak a word and act, says the Lord.’”
  Numbers 23:19 — god_speaks_and_acts: The Lord promises to speak and act without further delay. Balaam's declaration similarly asks whether God could speak and fail to act or abide by His word; it supports the certainty of action without supplying Ezekiel's timetable.

Ezekiel 12:27
“Son of man, behold, the embittering house of Israel keeps saying, ‘The vision this man sees is for many days ahead; this man prophesies of times far ahead.’
  2 Peter 3:3-4 — dismissal_of_delayed_fulfillment: The audience treats Ezekiel's vision as belonging to distant times. Peter describes scoffers questioning the promised coming because events seem unchanged; the settings differ, but both challenge dismissive responses to an apparently delayed warning.

Ezekiel 12:28
Therefore say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: None of My words that I may speak will be prolonged any longer. I will speak and act, says the Lord.’”
  Isaiah 55:10-11 — effective_divine_word: Ezekiel guarantees that God's words will be acted upon. Isaiah similarly compares His effective word to rain that accomplishes its work, declaring that it will not return before His purpose is fulfilled.

Ezekiel 13:2
“Son of man, prophesy against Israel’s prophets; you will prophesy and say to them, ‘Hear the Lord’s word!
  Jeremiah 23:9-11 — prophetic_indictment_of_prophets: Ezekiel is commissioned to speak against Israel's prophets. Jeremiah likewise introduces a prophetic indictment in which both prophet and priest are defiled and their evils have entered God's house.

Ezekiel 13:3
Thus says the Lord: Woe to those who prophesy from their own heart and see nothing at all!
  Jeremiah 23:16 — self_generated_prophecy: Both passages condemn prophecy arising from the speaker's own heart. Ezekiel says these prophets have seen nothing; Jeremiah contrasts their heart's speech with the Lord's mouth.

Ezekiel 13:6
They see false things and divine vain things, those who say, ‘The Lord says,’ although the Lord has not sent them; and they began to establish a word.
  Jeremiah 23:21 — uncommissioned_prophets: Both distinguish a claim to prophetic authority from an actual divine sending. Ezekiel's prophets say the Lord speaks though He did not send them; Jeremiah says they ran and prophesied without being sent or addressed.

Ezekiel 13:7
Have you not seen a false vision and spoken vain divinations?
  Jeremiah 14:14 — false_vision_and_divination: Both accuse the prophets of false visions and vain divination. Jeremiah further denies having sent or commanded the speakers and locates their messages in their own heart's choices.

Ezekiel 13:8
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: Because your words are false and your divinations vain, therefore behold, I am against you, says the Lord,
  Jeremiah 23:30-32 — divine_opposition_to_false_prophets: God declares Himself against those speaking falsehood and vain divinations. Jeremiah repeats that He is against prophets whose false dreams and lies lead His people astray without His commission.

Ezekiel 13:9
and I will stretch out My hand against the prophets who see false things and utter vain things. They will not belong to My people’s instruction, nor be written in the register of the house of Israel, nor enter Israel’s land. They will know that I am the Lord.
  Psalms 68:29 — exclusion_from_the_register: The false prophets will not be written in Israel's register. The psalm likewise uses exclusion from a written roll, asking that enemies be erased from the book of the living and not written with the righteous; the named registers differ.

Ezekiel 13:10
Because they misled My people, saying, ‘Peace, peace,’ when there was no peace, and this one builds a wall, and they coat it—if it is going to fall,
  Jeremiah 6:14 — false_assurance_of_peace: Both indict a peace announcement that conceals the people's actual condition. Ezekiel says there is no peace, while Jeremiah challenges the contemptuous treatment of the people's fracture with the question of where peace is.

Ezekiel 13:11
say to those coating it, ‘It will fall.’ There will be a flooding downpour, and I will put stones for hurling into their bonds, and they will fall; there will be an upending wind, and it will be split.
  Matthew 7:26-27 — unstable_building_in_a_storm: The coated wall falls under downpour and wind. Jesus' foolish builder likewise loses a house to rain, rivers and wind; the images expose false security, with a wall in Ezekiel and a house in Matthew.

Ezekiel 13:12
And behold, the wall has fallen! Will they not say to you, ‘Where is your coating with which you coated it?’
  Ezekiel 13:14 — same_chapter_collapse_and_exposure: The question about the missing coating after the wall falls corresponds to the announced tearing down of that coated wall and exposure of its foundations. Both expose the failure of the peace assurance.

Ezekiel 13:13
Therefore thus says the Lord: I will unleash an upending blast with wrath; a flooding downpour will come in My anger, and I will bring the projectile stones in wrath for complete destruction,
  Isaiah 28:17-18 — storm_against_false_security: Ezekiel's furious blast, downpour and projectile stones destroy the deceptive wall. Isaiah likewise confronts trust in deceit with a rushing storm that overturns false hope; its wording does not reproduce Ezekiel's full list of destructive elements.

Ezekiel 13:14
and I will tear down the wall you coated, and it will fall. I will lay it upon the ground; its foundations will be uncovered, and it will fall, and you will be brought to an end with rebukes. You will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Micah 1:6 — uncovered_foundations_in_judgment: The coated wall is brought to the ground and its foundations uncovered. Micah uses the same exposure of foundations when announcing Samaria's destruction, applying the image to a different city.

Ezekiel 13:15
I will complete My wrath upon the wall and those coating it, and it will fall. I said to you, ‘There is no wall, nor those coating it—
  Ezekiel 13:16 — same_chapter_image_and_identification: The announcement that neither wall nor coaters remain is followed by identification of the condemned prophets who proclaim peace for Jerusalem. The following unit makes the target of the wall imagery explicit.

Ezekiel 13:16
the prophets of Israel who prophesy concerning Jerusalem and see peace for her, when there is no peace,’ says the Lord.’
  Jeremiah 6:14 — false_assurance_of_peace: The prophets see peace for Jerusalem when there is none. Jeremiah's charge against those saying Peace, peace while treating the people's fracture with contempt supplies the same criticism of comforting deception.

Ezekiel 13:17
And you, son of man, set your face firmly against the daughters of your people who prophesy from their own heart, and prophesy against them,
  Ezekiel 13:3 — same_chapter_parallel_indictment: The charge against daughters who prophesy from their own heart parallels the preceding charge against prophets whose messages arise from their own heart. Both groups are judged by the origin of their claimed revelation.

Ezekiel 13:19
They were profaning Me among My people for a handful of barley and scraps of bread, putting to death lives that ought not die and preserving lives that ought not live, as you uttered things to a people listening to vain pronouncements.
  Micah 3:5 — religious_deception_for_food: The women profane God for barley and bread while distorting judgments about life and death. Micah likewise condemns prophets whose peace or war pronouncement depends on whether someone puts food into their mouths.

Ezekiel 13:20
Therefore thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am against your cushions, on which you entangle lives. I will tear them from your arms and send the lives out—their lives that you twist away—into dispersion.
  Ezekiel 13:21 — same_chapter_deliverance_from_entanglement: Tearing cushions from the women's arms and releasing entangled lives corresponds to tearing their coverings and rescuing the people from their hands. The paired actions identify the promised deliverance without explaining the devices' uncertain ritual mechanism.

Ezekiel 13:21
I will tear your coverings and rescue My people from your hand. They will no longer be in your hands for entanglement, and you will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Ezekiel 13:20 — same_chapter_deliverance_from_entanglement: The promised destruction of the coverings and rescue from the women's control completes the parallel promise to tear away their cushions and release lives. Both units repeat divine intervention against the entanglement.

Ezekiel 13:22
Because you distorted the righteous person’s heart unjustly, though I was not distorting him, and strengthened the lawless person’s hands so that he does not turn at all from his evil way and live,
  Jeremiah 23:14 — strengthening_wicked_hands: Both indict religious deceivers for strengthening evil hands so that people do not turn from their evil way. Ezekiel also names their unjust distortion of the righteous person's heart.

Ezekiel 13:23
therefore you certainly will no longer see false things or divine divinations. I will rescue My people from your hand, and you will know that I am the Lord.’”
  Zechariah 13:2 — removal_of_false_prophecy: God promises to stop the women's false visions and divination and rescue His people. Zechariah similarly promises removal of false prophets and the unclean spirit from the land.

Ezekiel 14:1
Men from Israel’s elders came to me and sat before me.
  Ezekiel 20:1 — elders_consult_the_prophet: Men from Israel's elders come and sit before Ezekiel in both scenes. The later account explicitly states that they have come to question the Lord, clarifying the kind of audience depicted here.

Ezekiel 14:3
“Son of man, these men have set their thoughts upon their hearts and set the punishment of their injustices before their faces. Shall I indeed answer them?
  Ezekiel 14:7 — same_chapter_inquiry_and_answer: The opening question about answering men whose thoughts occupy their hearts is answered in the later formulation: God will answer the alienated inquirer in what entangles him. Both preserve the heart, face and punishment language.

Ezekiel 14:4
Therefore speak to them and say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: Anyone of the house of Israel who sets his thoughts upon his heart, places the punishment of his injustice before his face, and comes to the prophet—I, the Lord, will answer him in the things in which his mind is entangled,
  Ezekiel 14:7 — same_chapter_expanded_warning: The warning to anyone of Israel whose inward fixations accompany an approach to the prophet is repeated with resident sojourners included. Both say God will answer the person in what entangles his mind.

Ezekiel 14:6
Therefore say to the house of Israel, ‘Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Turn back and turn away from your practices and all your impieties, and turn your faces.
  Ezekiel 18:30-32 — call_to_turn_from_impiety: Both call the house of Israel to turn away from its impieties. The later appeal links that turning with a new heart and spirit and with God's refusal to desire the sinner's death.

Ezekiel 14:7
For anyone of the house of Israel or of the sojourners living among Israel who becomes alienated from Me, sets his reflections upon his heart, places the punishment of his injustice before his face, and comes to the prophet to question him concerning Me—I, the Lord, will answer him in that in which he is entangled.
  Ezekiel 14:4 — same_chapter_expanded_warning: The formula concerning an entangled inquirer repeats the earlier warning while expressly including sojourners among Israel. The repeated conditions and answer emphasize the warning's extended scope.

Ezekiel 14:8
I will set My face firmly against that person, make him a wilderness and a devastation, and remove him from the midst of My people. You will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 20:3-6 — divine_face_and_exclusion: God sets His face against the offender and removes him from His people. Leviticus repeatedly uses that sanction for specified violations, including following mediums; the shared penalty does not identify all the offenses as identical.

Ezekiel 14:9
If the prophet is led astray and speaks, I, the Lord, have led that prophet astray. I will stretch out My hand against him and destroy him from the midst of My people Israel.
  1 Kings 22:20-23 — judicial_deception_of_prophets: Ezekiel attributes the prophet's being led astray to the Lord before announcing his destruction. Micaiah's vision likewise depicts divine authorization of a lying spirit in Ahab's prophets as part of the king's judgment.

Ezekiel 14:10
They will receive their injustice; according to the inquirer’s offense and according to the offense, it will likewise be for the prophet,
  Jeremiah 14:15-16 — prophet_and_audience_share_judgment: The prophet and the inquirer receive consequences for their respective offenses. Jeremiah similarly announces destruction both for lying prophets and for the people to whom they prophesy, including the audience's households.

Ezekiel 14:11
so that the house of Israel may no longer wander from Me or be defiled by any of their transgressions. They will be My people, and I will be their God, says the Lord.’”
  Jeremiah 38:33 — covenant_people_and_obedience: The end of Israel's wandering and defilement culminates in the promise that they will be God's people and He their God. Jeremiah gives the same covenant relationship while promising God's law within their understanding and hearts.

Ezekiel 14:13
“Son of man, if a land sins against Me by committing a transgression, and I stretch My hand against it, shatter its support of bread, send famine upon it, and remove human and livestock from it,
  Leviticus 26:26 — judgment_through_bread_scarcity: God shatters the land's support of bread and sends famine. Leviticus likewise threatens scarcity, bread distributed by weight and eating without satisfaction as a covenant judgment.

Ezekiel 14:14
even if these three men—Noah, Daniel and Job—were within it, they themselves would be saved by their righteousness, says the Lord.
