  Genesis 6:9 — named_example_of_righteousness: Noah is one of the three righteous people named in Ezekiel's hypothetical judgment. Genesis explicitly calls Noah righteous and blameless and says he pleased God, supplying the corresponding characterization.
  Job 1:1 — named_example_of_righteousness: Job is named among the three whose righteousness could save their own lives. His narrative opens by describing him as true, blameless, righteous and God-fearing, keeping away from evil.
  Daniel 6:22-23 — named_example_of_righteous_deliverance: Ezekiel names Daniel among those saved by righteousness. Daniel's answer from the lions' pit expressly says God saved him because righteousness was found in him, providing a narrative of that named righteous deliverance.

Ezekiel 14:15
Even if I bring evil beasts upon the land and punish it, so that it becomes a devastation and no one passes through because of the beasts,
  Leviticus 26:22 — beasts_as_covenant_judgment: The land becomes impassable because God brings evil beasts upon it. Leviticus likewise threatens wild animals, loss of livestock and population, and desolate roads.

Ezekiel 14:16
and these three men were within it, as I live, says the Lord, neither sons nor daughters would be saved; only they themselves would be saved, and the land would be destroyed.
  Genesis 7:1 — contrast_in_household_deliverance: Ezekiel's hypothetical righteous men would save only themselves, not sons or daughters. In the flood account God invites righteous Noah and his whole household into the ark, making the limit emphasized in Ezekiel particularly clear.

Ezekiel 14:17
Or even if I bring a sword upon that land and say, ‘Let a sword pass through the land,’ and remove human and livestock from it,
  Leviticus 26:25 — sword_as_covenant_judgment: God orders a sword through the land as a means of judgment. Leviticus likewise promises a sword exacting the covenant's penalty, placing armed devastation among the sanctions for disobedience.

Ezekiel 14:18
and these three men were within it, as I live, says the Lord, they certainly would not rescue sons or daughters; they alone would be saved.
  Ezekiel 14:16 — same_chapter_repeated_limit: The sword scenario repeats the preceding beasts scenario's limit: the righteous men save themselves, not their children. Repetition across different judgments reinforces the refusal of automatic household deliverance.

Ezekiel 14:19
Or even if I send death upon that land and pour My wrath upon it in blood, to destroy human and livestock from it,
  Leviticus 26:25 — death_as_covenant_judgment: God sends death through the land in wrath. Leviticus likewise announces death among the people within its covenant penalties; neither supplied formulation requires replacing death with a more specific disease name.

Ezekiel 14:20
and Noah, Daniel and Job were within it, as I live, says the Lord, neither sons nor daughters would be left; they would rescue their own lives by their righteousness.
  Ezekiel 18:20 — individual_righteousness_and_responsibility: The three men rescue their own lives by their righteousness. The later teaching explicitly places the righteous person's righteousness upon himself and refuses to transfer a parent's injustice to a child or a child's to a parent.

Ezekiel 14:21
Thus says the Lord: Even if I send My four evil retributions—sword, famine, evil beasts and death—against Jerusalem, to destroy human and livestock from her,
  Leviticus 26:22-26 — four_judgments_and_covenant_curses: Ezekiel gathers sword, famine, evil beasts and death into four judgments against Jerusalem. Leviticus's covenant warnings name wild beasts, sword, death and scarcity of bread within the same sequence of threatened penalties.

Ezekiel 14:22
and behold, her rescued survivors are left in her, bringing sons and daughters out of her. Behold, they are coming out to you. You will see their ways and their reflections and feel regret over the evils I brought upon Jerusalem—all the evils I brought upon her,
  Ezekiel 6:8-10 — survivors_and_recognition_of_judgment: Survivors emerge whose ways and reflections will be seen. The earlier survivor oracle likewise preserves people among the nations and connects their experience with remembrance of the Lord and recognition of their abominations.

Ezekiel 14:23
and they will comfort you, because you will see their ways and their reflections, and recognize that I have not done in vain all that I did in her, says the Lord.”
  Deuteronomy 29:23-27 — judgment_explained_by_conduct: Seeing the survivors' conduct will show that God's action against Jerusalem was not in vain. Deuteronomy likewise asks why the land suffered devastation and explains it through the people's abandonment of the covenant and service to other gods.

Ezekiel 15:2
“And you, son of man, what could vinewood be among all the branch wood found among the forest trees?
  Isaiah 5:1-7 — vine_imagery_for_judged_people: Ezekiel questions the usefulness of vinewood before applying it to Jerusalem. Isaiah likewise uses a vine and vineyard for Israel and Judah, but focuses on expected grapes becoming thorns and on failed justice rather than usefulness as timber.

Ezekiel 15:3
Will they take wood from it to make anything useful? Will they take a peg from it to hang any vessel upon it?
  Ezekiel 15:5 — same_chapter_example_and_conclusion: The inability to make even a useful peg from vinewood supplies the example for the later statement that the wood cannot serve for work even when whole. The argument then intensifies when the wood has burned.

Ezekiel 15:4
It has only been given to the fire to be consumed. Fire consumes its yearly prunings, and it is utterly spent. Surely it will not be useful for work?
  John 15:5-6 — vine_branches_and_fire: Ezekiel gives the vine's prunings to the fire as unusable wood. Jesus likewise describes a branch separated from the vine being gathered and burned; the settings differ, but both images end in disposal by fire.

Ezekiel 15:5
Even when it is whole, it will not serve for work. How much less if fire consumes it completely—will it still serve for work?
  Ezekiel 15:3-4 — same_chapter_argument_from_condition: The question whether burned vinewood can serve for work draws together the earlier inability to make a peg and the description of its consumption by fire. It is the conclusion of that particular timber argument.

Ezekiel 15:6
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: As with the vinewood among the forest trees, which I have given to the fire to be consumed, so I have given Jerusalem’s inhabitants.
  Psalms 79:9-17 — people_as_a_burned_vine: Ezekiel applies the burned vinewood directly to Jerusalem's inhabitants. The psalm likewise portrays the people transplanted from Egypt as a vine and laments that vine burned with fire and dug up.

Ezekiel 15:7
I will set My face against them. They will come out of the fire, and fire will devour them. They will recognize that I am the Lord when I set My face firmly against them.
  Amos 5:18-19 — inescapable_judgment: Those emerging from one fire are consumed by fire again. Amos similarly pictures escape from a lion leading to a bear and then a snake, using successive dangers to deny easy escape from the Lord's day.

Ezekiel 15:8
I will lay the land waste, because they have fallen into transgression, says the Lord.’”
  Leviticus 26:31-33 — transgression_and_land_desolation: The land is laid waste because of transgression. Leviticus's covenant warnings likewise announce desolate cities and land, presenting that devastation as the consequence of disobedience.

Ezekiel 16:2
“Son of man, solemnly testify to Jerusalem about her lawlessness,
  Micah 3:8 — commission_to_expose_peoples_sins: Ezekiel must testify to Jerusalem about her lawlessness. Micah similarly describes his task as declaring Jacob's impieties and Israel's sins through the Lord's Spirit, judgment and strength.

Ezekiel 16:3
and say, ‘Thus says the Lord to Jerusalem: Your root and your birth are from the land of Canaan; your father was an Amorite and your mother a Hittite.
  Ezekiel 16:45 — same_chapter_repeated_parentage_image: The Amorite father and Hittite mother recur later within the charge that Jerusalem resembles her morally unfaithful family. The repeated parentage belongs to the chapter's portrayal of the city and its conduct.

Ezekiel 16:6
I passed by you and saw you befouled in your blood, and said to you, ‘From your blood, life!
  Deuteronomy 32:9-10 — divine_care_in_vulnerable_beginnings: God grants life to the city portrayed as an abandoned infant. Moses likewise describes Israel's early dependence on God, who sustained and guarded His people in a waterless wilderness; the images of vulnerability differ.

Ezekiel 16:7
Multiply!’ I have made you like the growth of the field. You multiplied, grew great and entered cities of cities. Your breasts stood firm and your hair grew, but you were naked and exposed.
  Exodus 1:7 — growth_of_israels_people: The infant-city multiplies and grows like the field. Exodus similarly describes Israel's people increasing, multiplying and becoming exceedingly strong, providing a national growth counterpart to the personified city's development.

Ezekiel 16:8
I passed through you and saw you; behold, your season was the season of those lodging. I spread My wings over you and covered your exposure. I swore to you and entered a covenant with you, says the Lord, and you became Mine.
  Ruth 3:9 — covering_with_the_wing: God spreads His wings over Jerusalem, covers her exposure and enters covenant with her. Ruth likewise asks a near kinsman to spread the wing of his garment over her, using the covering image within a relationship-forming appeal.

Ezekiel 16:9
I washed you with water, washed your blood off you, and anointed you with oil,
  Ruth 3:3 — washing_and_anointing_parallel: Jerusalem is washed and anointed after the preceding declaration of covenant. Ruth is instructed to wash and anoint herself before going to Boaz. The passages share those acts of personal care in different narrative positions.

Ezekiel 16:10
and clothed you in patterned garments, gave you blue-purple footwear, girded you with fine linen, and wrapped you in hair-woven cloth,
  Psalms 44:14-15 — ornamented_female_figure: The city is dressed in patterned clothing and fine fabrics. The royal psalm likewise portrays the king's daughter in varied embroidery and gold-fringed dress as women are brought to the king.

Ezekiel 16:11
and adorned you with ornaments, placed bracelets around your hands and a necklace around your neck,
  Genesis 24:22 — bridal_adornment: Bracelets are placed on the city portrayed as God's adorned partner. Abraham's servant likewise takes bracelets for Rebekah's hands during the narrative seeking Isaac's wife; the source also names a necklace absent from that target unit.

Ezekiel 16:12
and put a ring around your nostril, little hoops upon your ears and a crown of boasting upon your head.
  Genesis 24:47 — bridal_adornment: The city's ornaments include hoops upon her ears. The servant's account likewise describes placing earrings on Rebekah while seeking a wife for Isaac; this comparison does not turn those earrings into the source's separately named nostril ring.

Ezekiel 16:13
You were adorned with gold and silver; your coverings were fine linen, hair-woven and patterned. You ate fine flour, oil and honey, and became very beautiful.
  Deuteronomy 32:13-15 — lavish_provision_before_unfaithfulness: Jerusalem receives flour, oil and honey before trusting her beauty and becoming unfaithful. Moses likewise describes honey, oil and rich nourishment followed by the beloved people's abandonment of the God who made them.

Ezekiel 16:14
Your fame went out among the nations because of your beauty, for it was made complete in splendor, in the loveliness I placed upon you, says the Lord.
  Isaiah 60:1-3 — divinely_given_city_radiance: Jerusalem's fame among nations comes from the splendor God placed upon her. Isaiah likewise addresses Jerusalem as radiant with the Lord's glory and describes kings and nations walking by that light.

Ezekiel 16:15
You trusted in your beauty and prostituted yourself because of your fame. You poured out your prostitution upon every passerby—which will not be.
  Ezekiel 28:17 — beauty_becoming_a_ground_of_pride: Jerusalem trusts in her beauty and turns to prostitution. The oracle against Tyre likewise makes beauty the ground of an exalted heart and corrupted knowledge, portraying gifted splendor turned into self-confidence.

Ezekiel 16:16
You took some of your garments, made sewn idols for yourself and prostituted yourself upon them. You certainly will not enter, nor will it come to be.
  Jeremiah 10:9 — fabric_and_manufactured_idols: Jerusalem uses garments to make sewn idols. Jeremiah likewise depicts manufactured gods dressed by their artisans in blue and purple, connecting fine fabric with the making and furnishing of idols.

Ezekiel 16:17
You took your vessels of boasting from My gold and My silver, from what I had given you, made male images for yourself and prostituted yourself among them.
  Hosea 2:10 — divine_gifts_redirected_to_idols: God's own gold and silver become Jerusalem's male images. Hosea likewise says God supplied silver and other gifts, yet the woman made silver and gold objects for Baal.

Ezekiel 16:18
You took your patterned clothing and clothed them; you set My oil and My incense before them.
  Jeremiah 10:9 — clothing_idols: The patterned garments supplied to Jerusalem become clothing for her images. Jeremiah similarly describes artisans dressing manufactured gods in blue and purple; Ezekiel additionally names offerings of God's oil and incense.

Ezekiel 16:19
My loaves that I gave you—fine flour, oil and honey with which I fed you—you set before them as a fragrant aroma. So it happened, says the Lord.
  Jeremiah 7:18 — food_offered_to_other_gods: God's food gifts are set before the images as a fragrant aroma. Jeremiah likewise describes cakes made for heaven's host and drink offerings poured to foreign gods, showing food preparation redirected to illicit worship.

Ezekiel 16:20
You took your sons and daughters whom you had borne and sacrificed them to them for consumption, as though you had prostituted yourself only a little,
  Jeremiah 7:31 — sacrifice_of_children: The city offers its own sons and daughters for consumption. Jeremiah likewise condemns the burning of sons and daughters at Topheth, explicitly denying that God commanded or conceived that practice.

Ezekiel 16:21
and slaughtered your children and gave them over as you used them for averting rites.
  Psalms 105:37-39 — children_sacrificed_and_bloodshed: The accusation joins slaughtered children with religious rites. The psalm likewise describes sons and daughters sacrificed to demons and Canaan's images, joining innocent bloodshed with the land being defiled by the people's works.

Ezekiel 16:22
This was in addition to all your prostitution, and you did not remember the days of your infancy, when you were naked and exposed and lived befouled in your blood.
  Hosea 2:17 — remembering_the_peoples_infancy: Jerusalem forgets her infancy and former exposure while becoming unfaithful. Hosea's restoration of the unfaithful woman likewise recalls the days of her infancy and her ascent from Egypt, using early dependence within the same kind of national marriage image.

Ezekiel 16:24
you built yourself a prostitution chamber and made yourself a display place in every broad street,
  Jeremiah 3:2 — public_prostitution_image: The city's prostitution chambers occupy broad streets. Jeremiah similarly depicts the unfaithful people waiting on the roads and polluting the land with prostitution, placing the metaphor in public ways.

Ezekiel 16:25
and at the head of every road you built your brothels, ruined your beauty, spread your legs to every passerby, and multiplied your prostitution.
  Proverbs 7:10-12 — public_prostitution_image: The city builds brothels at road heads and pursues every passerby. Proverbs' woman with a prostitute's appearance similarly roams outside and lies in wait in squares and at corners; Ezekiel applies such conduct to a city.

Ezekiel 16:26
You prostituted yourself with the sons of Egypt, your neighbors great in flesh, and prostituted yourself in many ways to anger Me.
  Jeremiah 2:18 — unfaithful_attachment_to_egypt: The city's unfaithfulness takes the form of prostitution with Egypt's people. Jeremiah likewise challenges the people's recourse to Egypt's road and waters in a passage about abandoning God, supplying a related foreign-dependence charge.

Ezekiel 16:27
But if I stretch My hand against you, I will remove your ordinances and hand you over to the desires of those who hate you, daughters of foreigners who turn you aside from your way in which you acted impiously.
  Ezekiel 23:28-30 — handed_over_to_hostile_partners: God hands Jerusalem to the desires of those who hate her. The later marriage allegory similarly hands the woman to hostile people who strip and shame her, connecting foreign attachments with the judgment they become.

Ezekiel 16:28
You prostituted yourself with the daughters of Assyria, and not even so were you filled; you prostituted yourself, and were not filled.
  Ezekiel 23:5-7 — assyria_in_the_prostitution_allegory: Jerusalem's unfaithfulness includes Assyria. The later account depicts Oholah's desire for Assyrian lovers and defilement through them; its sons and male officials are preserved separately from this unit's daughters wording.

Ezekiel 16:29
You multiplied your covenants toward the Chaldeans’ land, and not even with these were you filled.
  Ezekiel 23:14-17 — chaldean_attachment_in_the_prostitution_allegory: The city multiplies covenants toward the Chaldeans' land without satisfaction. The later allegory depicts desire for Chaldean figures, messages sent to that land and Babylon's sons entering the woman's bed.

Ezekiel 16:31
among your daughters. You built your brothel at every road head and made your platform in every broad street, and became like a prostitute gathering payments.
  Ezekiel 16:24-25 — same_chapter_repeated_public_construction: The brothel at every road head and platform in every broad street repeat the earlier construction charge. This unit also says she became like a prostitute gathering payments; that statement is preserved rather than negated.

Ezekiel 16:32
The adulterous wife is like you, receiving payments from her husband;
  Hosea 3:1 — adulterous_wife_as_national_image: Ezekiel calls the city an adulterous wife, alongside its unusual statement about payments from her husband. Hosea likewise makes love for an adulterous woman an image of God's relation to Israel's sons who pursue foreign gods.

Ezekiel 16:33
she kept giving payments to all who prostituted her. You too gave payments to all your lovers and loaded them up to come to you from all around in your prostitution.
  Ezekiel 16:34 — same_chapter_reversed_payments: The city pays and loads up her lovers to draw them to her. The following unit expressly describes the distortion compared with other women: she pays additional payments and receives none.

Ezekiel 16:34
With you it became distorted compared with other women in your prostitution. They have prostituted themselves with you as you paid extra payments, and no payments were given to you; with you things became distorted.
  Ezekiel 16:33 — same_chapter_reversed_payments: The statement that Jerusalem pays rather than receives payments explains the preceding account of gifts sent to all her lovers. The particular economic reversal is stated across the two units.

Ezekiel 16:36
Thus says the Lord: Because you poured out your copper, your shame will be exposed in your prostitution with your lovers, in all the reflections of your lawlessness, and in the blood of your children whom you gave to them.
  Ezekiel 16:20-21 — same_chapter_charge_and_judgment_ground: The judgment grounds include the blood of children given to the images. The earlier accusation narrates those sacrifices and the slaughter of the city's children, supplying the conduct now named as a cause of exposure.

Ezekiel 16:37
Therefore behold, I am gathering against you all your lovers with whom you mingled, all whom you loved together with all whom you hated. I will gather them against you from all around, reveal your evils to them, and they will see all your shame.
  Hosea 2:12 — exposure_before_lovers: God gathers Jerusalem's lovers and exposes her evils and shame before them. Hosea likewise announces the uncovering of the unfaithful woman's uncleanness before her lovers, who cannot rescue her.

Ezekiel 16:38
I will exact retribution upon you with the retribution of an adulteress and a woman shedding blood, and place you in blood of wrath and jealousy.
  Leviticus 20:10 — adultery_and_legal_retribution: The city is to receive an adulteress's retribution. Leviticus supplies the legal counterpart by prescribing death for both parties in adultery; Ezekiel applies the marital offense to Jerusalem's allegory.
  Genesis 9:6 — bloodshed_and_retribution: Ezekiel also specifies retribution for a woman shedding blood. Genesis likewise declares that the shedder of human blood will have his own blood shed, grounding judgment in the offense of bloodshed.

Ezekiel 16:39
I will hand you into their hands. They will tear down your brothel, pull down your platform, strip your clothing from you, take your vessels of boasting, and leave you naked and exposed.
  Ezekiel 23:26 — stripping_the_unfaithful_city: Jerusalem's enemies strip her garments and take her vessels of boasting. The later allegory repeats the removal of clothing and objects of boasting as part of the woman's humiliation.

Ezekiel 16:40
They will bring crowds against you, stone you with stones, and slaughter you with their swords.
  Ezekiel 23:46-47 — crowds_stones_and_swords: A crowd stones and strikes the city with swords. The later allegory likewise calls for a multitude that uses stones and swords against the women, repeating this particular combination of judgment actions.

Ezekiel 16:41
They will burn your houses with fire and exact retributions upon you before many women. I will turn you away from your prostitution, and you certainly will give no more payments.
  Jeremiah 52:13 — city_fire_in_allegory_and_narrative: The allegorical sentence includes burning Jerusalem's houses. Jeremiah's account narrates the burning of the Lord's house, the royal house and the city's houses, supplying a concrete narrative counterpart to the threatened fire.

Ezekiel 16:42
I will release My wrath upon you; My jealousy will be removed from you. I will rest and certainly be concerned no longer.
  Ezekiel 5:13 — completion_of_divine_wrath: The release of wrath and removal of jealousy correspond to the earlier announcement that God's wrath and anger would be completed and His jealousy recognized. The earlier passage does not repeat this unit's wording about resting.

Ezekiel 16:43
Because you did not remember the day of your infancy but grieved Me with all these things, behold, I have brought your ways upon your head, says the Lord. Thus you committed impiety in addition to all your lawlessness.
  Deuteronomy 32:18 — forgetting_the_god_of_early_care: Jerusalem forgets her infancy and grieves the God who cared for her. Moses likewise accuses the people of forsaking the God who begat them and forgetting the God who nourishes them.

Ezekiel 16:44
These are all the things they said against you in a proverb, saying, ‘As the mother, so the daughter.’
  Ezekiel 16:3 — same_chapter_family_proverb: The proverb Like mother, like daughter recalls the parentage image introduced with Jerusalem's Hittite mother and Amorite father. The proverb makes family resemblance part of the accusation against the city's conduct.

Ezekiel 16:45
You are the daughter of your mother, who rejected her husband and her children, and the sister of your sisters, who rejected their husbands and children. Your mother was a Hittite, and your father an Amorite.
  Ezekiel 16:3 — same_chapter_repeated_parentage_image: The chapter repeats the Hittite mother and Amorite father while explaining the city's resemblance to relatives who reject husband and children. The connection is the explicit reuse of that parentage within the allegory.

Ezekiel 16:46
Your elder sister is Samaria, she and her daughters, dwelling at your left; your sister younger than you, dwelling at your right, is Sodom and her daughters.
  Ezekiel 23:2-4 — cities_personified_as_related_women: Jerusalem's comparison with Samaria and Sodom as sisters belongs to a pattern repeated in chapter 23, where related women are named as Samaria and Jerusalem. The later pair does not include Sodom.

Ezekiel 16:47
Not even so did you walk in their ways or act according to their lawlessness. Before long, you surpass them in all your ways.
  Jeremiah 3:11 — judah_worse_than_its_comparison: Jerusalem exceeds its sisters' lawlessness. Jeremiah likewise says Israel is justified in comparison with faithless Judah, making Judah's greater guilt the point of a comparison with another unfaithful people.

Ezekiel 16:48
As I live, says the Lord, your sister Sodom, she and her daughters, certainly did not do as you and your daughters have done.
  Matthew 11:23-24 — comparison_unfavorable_even_to_sodom: Jerusalem is accused of doing what even Sodom did not do. Jesus likewise makes Sodom the more favorable comparison when addressing Capernaum; the cities addressed differ, while Sodom functions as the forceful measure of culpability.

Ezekiel 16:49
Yet this was the lawlessness of your sister Sodom: arrogance. In fullness of bread and abundance of wine she and her daughters lived luxuriously. This belonged to her and her daughters, but they did not support the hand of the poor and needy.
  Luke 16:19-21 — abundance_and_neglect_of_the_poor: Sodom's luxury and abundant food coexist with refusal to support the poor. Jesus' story similarly places a richly dressed man enjoying daily splendor beside a poor man longing for food at his gateway, giving a narrative counterpart to neglected need amid abundance.
  Amos 6:4-6 — luxury_and_indifference: Sodom's abundance and luxury accompany failure to help the needy. Amos likewise portrays luxurious couches, rich food, wine and perfumes alongside indifference to Joseph's shattering.

Ezekiel 16:50
They boasted greatly and committed acts of lawlessness before Me, and I removed them as I saw.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — sodoms_removal_and_narrative: God's removal of lawless Sodom corresponds to Genesis's account of the Lord raining fire and sulphur and overthrowing Sodom, Gomorrah and their surroundings. Ezekiel emphasizes the conduct preceding that removal.

Ezekiel 16:51
Samaria did not sin half your sins. You multiplied your lawlessness beyond theirs and justified your sisters by all the lawlessness you committed.
  Jeremiah 3:11 — comparative_justification: Jerusalem's greater lawlessness makes Samaria appear justified by comparison. Jeremiah uses the same comparative force when declaring Israel justified in relation to faithless Judah; neither passage thereby calls the lesser offender sinless.

Ezekiel 16:52
You too, receive your torment, in which you corrupted your sisters by your sins, in which you acted more lawlessly than they and justified them above yourself. You too, be ashamed and take your dishonor, in that you justified your sisters.
  Jeremiah 3:11 — comparative_guilt_and_shame: Jerusalem must accept shame because its worse conduct has justified its sisters above itself. Jeremiah's comparison of Israel with faithless Judah supplies the same rhetorical reversal through greater guilt.

Ezekiel 16:53
I will reverse their reversals—the reversal of Sodom and her daughters; I will reverse the reversal of Samaria and her daughters; and I will reverse your reversal among them,
  Ezekiel 16:55 — same_chapter_restoration_explained: The reversal promised to Sodom, Samaria and Jerusalem is restated as the restoration of each city and its daughters to their earlier condition. The following formulation clarifies the unusual reversal language without changing it.

Ezekiel 16:54
so that you may receive your torment and be dishonored for all you did in provoking Me.
  Ezekiel 36:31-32 — restoration_and_remorse: The reversal leaves Jerusalem bearing dishonor for what it did. The later restoration oracle likewise calls the people to remember their evil practices and be ashamed, explicitly saying that God is not acting because of them.

Ezekiel 16:55
Your sister Sodom and her daughters will be restored as they were from the beginning; Samaria and her daughters will be restored as they were from the beginning; and you and your daughters will be restored as you were from the beginning.
  Ezekiel 16:53 — same_chapter_repeated_restoration: Sodom, Samaria and Jerusalem with their daughters are each promised restoration. The earlier unit names the same three groups in its promise to reverse their reversals, establishing the scope repeated here.

Ezekiel 16:57
before your evils were revealed—as now you are a reproach of Syria’s daughters and all around her, daughters of foreigners who surround you all around?
  Lamentations 2:15-16 — public_reproach_of_jerusalem: Jerusalem has become a reproach among surrounding foreign daughters. Lamentations likewise depicts passersby and enemies mocking Daughter Jerusalem and celebrating her downfall; it does not identify all those mockers with the named Syrian neighbors here.

Ezekiel 16:58
Your impieties and your lawlessness—you have borne them, says the Lord.
  Ezekiel 23:49 — bearing_own_lawlessness: The city bears its own impieties and lawlessness. The later marriage allegory closes with the same consequence: impiety brought upon the women and the sins of their reflections received by them.

Ezekiel 16:59
Thus says the Lord: I will deal with you as you have done, as you dishonored these things by transgressing My covenant.
  Leviticus 26:15-16 — broken_covenant_and_requital: God deals with Jerusalem according to its dishonoring and transgressing the covenant. Leviticus likewise moves from the refusal of God's commands and dissolution of His covenant to the announcement of what He will do in return.

Ezekiel 16:60
I Myself will remember My covenant with you in the days of your infancy and raise up for you an eternal covenant.
  Ezekiel 37:26 — everlasting_covenant_after_judgment: God remembers the early covenant and promises an eternal covenant. The later restoration likewise promises an everlasting covenant of peace and God's holy things among the people forever.

Ezekiel 16:62
I Myself will raise up My covenant with you, and you will recognize that I am the Lord,
  Jeremiah 38:33-34 — renewed_covenant_and_knowledge_of_god: God's establishment of His covenant leads Jerusalem to recognize the Lord. Jeremiah likewise joins the renewed covenant with the promise that all the people will know Him and receive mercy for their injustices.

Ezekiel 16:63
so that you may remember and be ashamed, and no longer have occasion to open your mouth because of your dishonor, when I am propitious to you concerning all that you have done, says the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 36:31-33 — forgiveness_with_remembrance_and_shame: God's propitious dealing leaves Jerusalem remembering, ashamed and with no occasion to open her mouth. The later cleansing oracle similarly joins divine restoration with remembrance of evil ways and shame, explicitly saying that God is not acting because of the people.

Ezekiel 17:2
“Son of man, tell a story and speak a parable to the house of Israel,
  Psalms 77:1-4 — related use of parables to address Israel: Ezekiel is commanded to tell a story and speak a parable to Israel's house. The psalm addresses the people and announces speech in parables and ancient riddles. The literary method corresponds; the psalm recounts ancestral testimony rather than this eagle-and-vine story.

Ezekiel 17:3
and say, ‘Thus says the Lord: The great eagle, with great wings and a broad span, full of talons, which has authority to enter Lebanon, took the cedar’s choice parts;
  Ezekiel 17:12 — same-chapter interpretation of the conquering eagle: The great eagle takes the cedar's choice parts. The explanation asks whether the audience understands the story and describes Babylon's king taking Jerusalem's king and rulers. This follows the chapter's own movement from political imagery to its stated interpretation.

Ezekiel 17:4
he nipped off the tips of its tender growth, carried them to the land of Canaan, and set them in a fortified city.
  Ezekiel 17:12 — same-chapter interpretation of the removed cedar growth: The eagle carries the tender growth to Canaan and a fortified city. The explanation describes Jerusalem's king and rulers being brought to Babylon. The literary correspondence is retained while preserving the parable's actual Canaan wording rather than silently replacing it with Babylon.

Ezekiel 17:5
He took some of the land’s seed and put it in the plain, a plant beside abundant water; he appointed it to be watched over.
  Ezekiel 17:13-14 — same-chapter royal interpretation of the planted seed: The eagle plants seed from the land under supervision. The explanation describes taking royal seed, imposing a covenant and establishing a weak kingdom. The connection concerns the parable's planted seed and the explicitly named royal seed, without importing a personal name.

Ezekiel 17:6
It sprouted and became a weak vine, small in size so that it might appear. Its branches were upon it and its roots beneath it. It became a vine, produced offshoots and extended its climbing growth.
  Ezekiel 17:14 — same-chapter correspondence between the weak vine and weak kingdom: The growing vine is expressly weak and small. The explanation likewise says the kingdom is to remain weak rather than exalt itself, preserving the covenant. This close weakness correspondence helps explain the plant image without asserting that every botanical detail has a separately stated political equivalent.

Ezekiel 17:7
There was another great eagle, with great wings and many talons. Behold, this vine was intertwined toward him, its roots toward him; it sent its branches to him to water it together with the clod of its planting.
  Ezekiel 17:15-17 — same-chapter explanation of turning toward a second protector: The vine directs roots and branches toward another eagle for water. The explanation describes revolt and seeking Egypt's horses and people. The second appeal for support is the particular common movement.

Ezekiel 17:8
It grows fat in a good plain beside abundant water, to produce shoots, bear fruit and become a great vine.
  Psalms 79:9-12 — related flourishing national vine imagery: The parable describes a vine flourishing in a good plain, with shoots and fruit. The psalm depicts Israel through a transplanted vine whose roots fill the land and whose branches spread widely. The psalm does not supply the source's abundant-water or fruit clauses.

Ezekiel 17:9
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: Will it prosper? Will not the roots of its tender growth and its fruit rot, and all its early shoots dry up? No great arm or numerous people will be needed to pull it from its roots.
  Hosea 9:16 — related judgment expressed through ruined roots and lost fruit: The vine's roots and fruit rot and its early shoots dry up. Hosea describes Ephraim drying at the roots and no longer bearing fruit. The plant images both portray national ruin, with rotting in Ezekiel and drying explicitly named in Hosea.

Ezekiel 17:10
Behold, it grows fat—surely it will not prosper? Will it not dry up completely as soon as the scorching wind touches it? It will dry up together with the clod of its growth.’”
  Ezekiel 19:12 — close vine-destruction imagery involving a scorching wind: Both parables describe the scorching wind drying the plant's growth. The source says the vine dries with the clod of its growth; the later lament also describes wrath, being thrown down and consuming fire. Those additional actions are not inserted into the source.

Ezekiel 17:12
“Son of man, say now to the embittering house, ‘Do you not understand what these things were?’ Say, ‘When Babylon’s king comes against Jerusalem, he will take her king and her rulers and bring them to himself in Babylon.
  2 Chronicles 36:9-10 — related removal of a Judean king to Babylon before another appointment: The explanation predicts Babylon's king taking Jerusalem's king and rulers. Chronicles narrates Nebuchadnezzar bringing Jechonias to Babylon and appointing Zedekiah. The source leaves its king unnamed, so the comparison does not turn the target's names into names supplied by Ezekiel.

Ezekiel 17:13
He will take one of the royal seed, make a covenant with him, and bring him under a curse-oath. He will take the land’s leaders,
  2 Chronicles 36:13 — close counterpart for a Babylonian-imposed royal oath: Ezekiel describes Babylon's king binding royal seed by covenant and a curse-oath. Chronicles says Nebuchadnezzar made Zedekiah swear by God and records his breach of obligation. The named narrative is a particular comparison; the source itself does not name its oath-taker.

Ezekiel 17:14
so that it may become a weak kingdom, not exalt itself at all, and keep his covenant and establish it.
  2 Kings 24:14-16 — related weakening of Judah through removal of its powerful people: The source explains the preceding removal of leaders as establishing a weak kingdom. Kings describes deportation of rulers, powerful men, warriors and craftsmen, leaving the poor. The target supplies a narrated loss of strength but does not state this source's covenant condition.

Ezekiel 17:15
He will revolt from him, sending his messengers to Egypt to give him horses and many people. Will he prosper? Will the one doing contrary things be rescued? Will one transgressing a covenant be saved?
  Isaiah 31:1-3 — close warning against reliance on Egyptian military help: The ruler seeks horses and many people from Egypt, and his hope of rescue is challenged. Isaiah rebukes seeking Egypt's horses and chariots instead of God and predicts the helpers' failure. Ezekiel additionally identifies revolt and a broken covenant.

Ezekiel 17:16
As I live, says the Lord, surely in the place of the king who made him king—he who dishonored My curse-oath and transgressed My covenant—with him in the midst of Babylon he will die.
  Jeremiah 52:11 — related death in Babylon of a defeated Judean ruler: Ezekiel predicts that the oath-breaking ruler will die with the king who installed him in Babylon. Jeremiah records Zedekiah being brought to Babylon and held in a mill house until death. The narrative supplies a named counterpart without making Ezekiel's unnamed ruler explicitly named in the source.

Ezekiel 17:17
Pharaoh will not wage war against him with great power or a numerous crowd, amid siegeworks and the building of missile stations to remove lives.
  Jeremiah 44:7-8 — related warnings concerning Pharaoh's military role during Jerusalem's siege: Following the request for Egyptian horses and manpower, Ezekiel says Pharaoh will not wage war against him with great power amid siege operations. Jeremiah explicitly says the Egyptian force that came to help will return home and the Chaldeans will return to capture Jerusalem. The source's ambiguous against him is not resolved into the target's express help-and-withdrawal description.

Ezekiel 17:18
He dishonored a sworn oath by transgressing a covenant; behold, he has given his hand and did all these things to him. Surely he will not be saved?
  Joshua 9:18-20 — contrast between violating and preserving a binding sworn covenant: Ezekiel condemns a dishonored oath and transgressed covenant, denying escape. Joshua's leaders refuse to attack those to whom they swore by the Lord, specifically to avoid wrath for the oath. The parties differ, while the binding force of the sworn undertaking is explicit in both.

Ezekiel 17:19
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: As I live, surely My covenant that he transgressed and My sworn oath that he dishonored—I will bring them upon his head.
  Zechariah 5:3-4 — related divine judgment for dishonoring an oath in God's name: The Lord says He will bring the dishonored oath and broken covenant upon the ruler's head. Zechariah's curse punishes false swearers and enters the house of one who swears falsely by God's name. The target describes a household's destruction rather than the source's royal fate.

Ezekiel 17:20
I will spread My net over him, and he will be caught in his enclosure.
  Ezekiel 12:13 — close divine net-and-enclosure image of capture: Both declarations say God will spread His net over the ruler and that he will be caught in an enclosure. This source says his enclosure; the target says My enclosure and additionally predicts deportation to Babylon. The possessive and added destination are preserved.

Ezekiel 17:21
In all his battle array they will fall by the sword, and I will scatter the survivors to every wind. You will recognize that I, the Lord, have spoken.’
  Ezekiel 12:14 — close sword and scattering of a ruler's supporters: The source predicts death by sword among the ruler's battle array and scattering of survivors to every wind. The target describes his helpers and supporters scattered to every wind with a sword drawn after them. Both connect the ruler's fall with dispersal of those around him.

Ezekiel 17:22
For thus says the Lord: I Myself will take from the cedar’s choice parts, nip off from the top of their heart, and I Myself will plant it upon a high mountain. I will suspend it
  Ezekiel 17:3-4 — same-chapter reversal of the taking of choice cedar growth: The eagle takes the cedar's choice parts and carries its tender growth away. God now takes choice cedar material and plants it on a high mountain. The repeated cedar-taking image supports this reversal without identifying the later plant as a specifically named king or replacing the source's unusual top-of-their-heart wording.

Ezekiel 17:23
upon Israel’s lofty mountain and plant it. It will produce a shoot, bear fruit and become a great cedar. Every beast will rest beneath it, every bird will rest under its shade, and its branches will be restored.
  Mark 4:30-32 — related plant imagery sheltering birds beneath its shade: The planted cedar grows and shelters birds under its shade, as well as beasts beneath it. Jesus' kingdom parable describes a mustard plant with great branches under whose shade birds can shelter. The plant species and the source's additional beasts remain distinct.

Ezekiel 17:24
All the plain’s trees will know that I am the Lord who lowers a high tree, raises a low tree, dries a green tree and makes a dry tree flourish. I, the Lord, have spoken and will act.”
  1 Samuel 2:7-8 — close divine reversal of high and low conditions: God lowers the high tree and raises the low, also reversing green and dry growth. Hannah's song says the Lord humbles and raises high and lifts the poor to sit with rulers. The target supports the reversal of rank without supplying the source's botanical details.

Ezekiel 18:2
“Son of man, what is this proverb you have among Israel’s sons, saying, ‘The fathers ate unripe grapes, and the children’s teeth were set on edge’?
  Jeremiah 38:29-30 — close shared proverb about unripe grapes and children's teeth: Ezekiel quotes the proverb attributing children's affected teeth to their fathers' eating. Jeremiah quotes the same proverb and says it will cease, replacing it with each person's own sin and eating. Both passages challenge this proverb's use to explain responsibility.

Ezekiel 18:3
As I live, says the Lord, this proverb will certainly no longer be spoken in Israel.
  Jeremiah 38:29 — close announced end of the inherited-grape proverb: The Lord swears that the proverb will no longer be spoken in Israel. Jeremiah likewise announces days when people will not say that fathers ate an unripe grape and children's teeth were set on edge. The target states the proverb rather than leaving it in the preceding unit.

Ezekiel 18:4
For all souls are Mine. As the soul of the father, so also the soul of the son—they are Mine. The soul that sins will die.
  Deuteronomy 24:16 — close individual responsibility for the sin that brings death: Ezekiel says father and son belong to God and the soul that sins will die. Deuteronomy says fathers and sons must not die for each other and each dies for his own sin. The law does not include the source's express divine ownership of all souls.

Ezekiel 18:5
The person who is righteous, practicing judgment and righteousness,
  Deuteronomy 16:20 — related practice of justice as the path to life: Ezekiel introduces the righteous person as practicing judgment and righteousness, with life promised at the conclusion of the description. Deuteronomy commands pursuing what is just justly so that the people may live and inherit the land. The source then develops a fuller catalogue of conduct.

Ezekiel 18:6
will not eat upon the mountains, certainly will not raise his eyes to the reflections of the house of Israel, certainly will not defile his neighbor’s wife, and will not approach a woman in her menstrual separation;
  Leviticus 18:19-20 — close prohibitions concerning a neighbor's wife and menstrual separation: Two of Ezekiel's defining prohibitions are defiling a neighbor's wife and approaching a woman in menstrual separation. Leviticus expressly prohibits both acts in adjacent laws. The target does not supply the source's mountain eating or reflections clauses.

Ezekiel 18:7
he certainly will not oppress anyone, will return a debtor’s pledge, takes no plunder, will give his bread to the hungry, and will clothe the naked;
  Deuteronomy 24:12-13 — close duty to return a debtor's pledge: Ezekiel's righteous person returns a debtor's pledge. Deuteronomy requires returning a poor debtor's pledged garment by sunset so that he can sleep in it. The target specifies the poor debtor, garment and timing not given in this source clause.
  Isaiah 58:7 — close paired duties of feeding the hungry and clothing the naked: Ezekiel describes giving bread to the hungry and clothing the naked. Isaiah likewise commands breaking bread for the hungry and clothing the naked, alongside hospitality. This target supports the source's two charitable acts, distinct from its pledge-return requirement.

Ezekiel 18:8
he will not give his silver at interest or take increase, will turn his hand from injustice, and will render a righteous judgment between a man and his neighbor;
  Leviticus 25:36-37 — close prohibition of interest and increase: Ezekiel's righteous person does not lend silver at interest or take increase. Leviticus expressly forbids interest and increase from the impoverished brother and lending him silver at interest. The legal context identifies the borrower more specifically.
  Deuteronomy 1:16 — close requirement of righteous judgment between people: Ezekiel requires righteous judgment between a man and his neighbor. Moses commands judges to judge justly between a man, his brother and the sojourner. This target supports the judicial clause rather than the separate lending prohibition.

Ezekiel 18:9
he has walked in My commands and kept My ordinances to carry them out. This one is righteous; he will surely live, says the Lord.
  Leviticus 18:5 — close life promised through carrying out God's ordinances: Ezekiel's conclusion joins walking in God's commands and doing His ordinances with assured life. Leviticus likewise says the person who keeps and does the ordinances and judgments will live by them. The shared conduct-and-life formulation is explicit.

Ezekiel 18:10
But if he fathers a pestilent son who sheds blood and commits sins,
  2 Kings 21:16 — related example of a blood-shedding son after a righteous father: Ezekiel introduces a blood-shedding son of the righteous man. Kings describes Manasseh's abundant innocent bloodshed; the read surrounding records identify him as Hezekiah's son and describe Hezekiah's fidelity. This is an illustrative narrative comparison, not an identification of Ezekiel's hypothetical son.

Ezekiel 18:11
he did not walk in the way of his righteous father, but even ate upon the mountains and defiled his neighbor’s wife,
  Leviticus 18:20 — close violation of the prohibition against defiling a neighbor's wife: The wicked son defiles his neighbor's wife. Leviticus directly prohibits intercourse with a neighbor's wife and calls it defilement. Ezekiel also names mountain eating and failure to follow the father, which this legal unit does not address.

Ezekiel 18:12
oppressed the poor and needy, seized plunder, did not return a pledge, set his eyes upon idols, and has committed lawlessness,
  Exodus 22:24-26 — related oppression of the poor and failure to restore a pledge: The son oppresses the poor and needy and does not return a pledge. Exodus forbids pressing a poor borrower hard and requires returning a pledged garment before sunset. The source additionally lists plunder and idols, beyond the selected law.

Ezekiel 18:13
gave at interest and took increase—this one certainly will not live. He committed all this lawlessness; he will surely be put to death, and his blood will be upon him.
  Leviticus 25:36-37 — close breach of the interest-and-increase prohibition: The wicked son gives at interest and takes increase. Leviticus forbids those lending practices toward the poor brother. Ezekiel pronounces death and responsibility for the son's whole catalogue of lawlessness; the target itself does not prescribe that penalty for this loan.

Ezekiel 18:14
But if he fathers a son who sees all the sins his father committed, becomes afraid, and does not act according to them,
  2 Chronicles 34:1-3 — related righteous son whose practice differs from his wicked father's: Ezekiel describes a son who sees his father's sins and does not repeat them. Chronicles describes Josiah pursuing God and cleansing Judah after the read account of his father Amon's idolatry. It supplies a concrete generational contrast without asserting that Chronicles narrates Ezekiel's precise seeing-and-fearing sequence.

Ezekiel 18:15
he has not eaten upon the mountains, not set his eyes upon the reflections of the house of Israel, and not defiled his neighbor’s wife,
  Leviticus 20:10 — close rejection of adultery with a neighbor's wife: The righteous son has not defiled his neighbor's wife. Leviticus expressly condemns adultery with that wife and assigns a penalty. The target supports that specific prohibition, not the source's separate mountain and reflections clauses.

Ezekiel 18:16
has not oppressed anyone, not taken a pledge, and not seized plunder; he gave his bread to the hungry and clothed the naked,
  Isaiah 58:7 — close bread-and-clothing pattern of care for the needy: Ezekiel's son gives bread to the hungry and clothes the naked. Isaiah commands the same paired acts. This comparison does not change the source's saying he has not taken a pledge into the different pledge-return wording used elsewhere.

Ezekiel 18:17
and turned his hand from injustice, did not take interest or increase, practiced righteousness and walked in My commands. He will not die in his father’s injustices; he will surely live.
  Deuteronomy 24:16 — close denial that a son dies for his father's injustice: Ezekiel expressly says this obedient son will not die in his father's injustices. Deuteronomy prohibits sons dying for fathers and assigns death to each person's own sin. Ezekiel additionally describes the conduct by which this son lives.

Ezekiel 18:18
But if his father practiced affliction and seized plunder, he did contrary things in the midst of My people and will die in his injustice.
  Deuteronomy 24:16 — close assignment of death to the wrongdoer's own sin: The oppressive father dies in his own injustice while the preceding son lives. Deuteronomy separates fathers' and sons' liability and says each dies for his own sin. The source illustrates that principle through this particular two-generation contrast.

Ezekiel 18:19
You will say, ‘Why did the son not receive his father’s injustice?’ Because the son practiced righteousness and mercy, preserved all My ordinances and carried them out; he will surely live.
  Micah 6:8 — related righteousness and mercy joined with faithful conduct: The son's life is explained by his righteousness, mercy and observance of God's ordinances. Micah identifies doing justice, loving mercy and readiness to walk with God as what He seeks. The target does not address the source's disputed inheritance of a father's injustice.

Ezekiel 18:20
The soul that sins will die. The son will not receive his father’s injustice, nor the father his son’s injustice. The righteous person’s righteousness will be upon him, and the lawless person’s lawlessness upon him.
  Deuteronomy 24:16 — close reciprocal exclusion of inherited liability for sin: Ezekiel says neither son nor father receives the other's injustice and that the sinning soul dies. Deuteronomy likewise denies both directions of substitution and says each dies for his own sin. The source also expressly assigns righteousness to the righteous person.

Ezekiel 18:21
If the lawless person turns from all the lawlessness he committed, keeps all My commandments, and practices righteousness and mercy, he will surely live; he certainly will not die.
  Ezekiel 33:14-16 — close life promised to a wrongdoer who turns and practices righteousness: Both passages describe a lawless person turning, practicing judgment or righteousness and receiving life rather than death. The later passage adds restoration of pledges and seized property. The shared assurance concerns a changed course of conduct.

Ezekiel 18:22
None of the transgressions he committed will be remembered. By the righteousness he practiced, he will live.
  Jeremiah 38:34 — close promise not to remember sins: Ezekiel says the repentant person's transgressions will not be remembered. Jeremiah's new-covenant promise likewise says God will remember sins no more. The source expressly links the person's life to practiced righteousness, while the target places forgiveness within the new covenant.

Ezekiel 18:23
Do I at all desire the death of the lawless, says the Lord, rather than his turning from his evil way and living?
  Ezekiel 33:11 — close rejection of delight in the wrongdoer's death: Both declarations say God desires the wrongdoer to turn from his way and live rather than die. The later passage repeats this as an urgent address to Israel to turn completely.
  2 Peter 3:9 — related divine desire for repentance rather than destruction: Ezekiel contrasts death with turning and living. The letter explains the Lord's patience as not wishing anyone to perish but all to reach repentance. Its setting is the apparent delay of the promised coming, not Ezekiel's dispute over individual responsibility.

Ezekiel 18:24
But when a righteous person turns from his righteousness and practices injustice according to all the lawlessness the lawless person committed, none of the righteous acts he did will be remembered. In the transgression he committed and the sins he sinned, in these he will die.
  Ezekiel 33:12-13 — close warning that former righteousness does not excuse later lawlessness: Both passages say a formerly righteous person who commits lawlessness will not have his earlier righteous deeds remembered as protection. The later passage additionally describes trusting in one's righteousness. The warning does not declare that the earlier acts never happened.

Ezekiel 18:25
You said, ‘The Lord’s way does not run straight.’ Hear now, the whole house of Israel: Surely My way does not fail to run straight? Is it not your way that fails to run straight?
  Deuteronomy 32:4 — related affirmation of God's righteous ways against an accusation of unfairness: Ezekiel challenges the charge that God's way does not run straight. The song in Deuteronomy declares His works true, all His ways judgments and no unrighteousness in Him. The target affirms the divine righteousness questioned in the source without quoting that accusation.

Ezekiel 18:26
When the righteous person turns from his righteousness, commits a transgression and dies, in the transgression he committed—in it he will die.
  Ezekiel 33:18 — close repeated judgment on turning from righteousness to wrongdoing: Both statements describe a righteous person turning from righteousness, committing transgression or lawless deeds and dying in those acts. The correspondence preserves the source's emphasis on the person's changed conduct.

Ezekiel 18:27
When a lawless person turns from the lawlessness he practiced and practices judgment and righteousness, this one has preserved his life
  Ezekiel 33:19 — close preservation of life through turning from lawlessness: Ezekiel says the former wrongdoer who practices judgment and righteousness has preserved his life. The later statement says the sinner who turns and practices those things will live in them. Both connect life with the same reversal of practice.

Ezekiel 18:28
and turned from all the impieties he committed; he will surely live, he certainly will not die.
  Isaiah 55:7 — related mercy and life following abandonment of evil ways: Ezekiel promises certain life to the person who turns from his impieties. Isaiah calls the ungodly to forsake his ways and return to the Lord for mercy and abundant forgiveness. The source speaks in terms of life and death, while Isaiah names forgiveness explicitly.

Ezekiel 18:29
The house of Israel says, ‘The Lord’s way does not go right.’ Surely My way does not fail to go right, house of Israel? Is it not your way that fails to go right?
  Ezekiel 33:17 — close repeated charge and rebuttal concerning God's way: Both passages report the people saying God's way is not straight and answer that their own way is the defective one. The repeated disputation is more specific than a general assertion of divine justice.

Ezekiel 18:30
I will judge you, each according to his way, house of Israel, says the Lord. Turn back and turn away from all your impieties, and they will not become a punishment of injustice for you.
  Romans 2:6 — close individual judgment according to conduct: God says He will judge each Israelite according to his way. Romans says God will repay each according to his works, with the surrounding passage distinguishing good and evil conduct. The source also includes an immediate command to turn from impiety.

Ezekiel 18:31
Cast away from yourselves all your impieties with which you acted impiously against Me, and make yourselves a new heart and a new spirit. Why are you dying, house of Israel?
  Psalms 50:12 — related renewal of heart and spirit with differing forms of appeal: Ezekiel commands the people to make themselves a new heart and spirit. The psalm asks God to create a clean heart and renew an upright spirit within the speaker. The comparison preserves command and petition rather than assigning the same grammatical agency to both.
  Ezekiel 36:26-27 — close new-heart and new-spirit language as command and divine promise: The people are commanded to make themselves a new heart and spirit. The later oracle promises that God will give a new heart and spirit and cause obedience to His ordinances. The common renewal language is explicit, while human command and divine gift remain distinguished.

Ezekiel 18:32
For I do not desire the death of the one who dies, says the Lord.”
  Wisdom of Solomon 1:12-13 — close denial that God desires or delights in death: Ezekiel says God does not desire the death of one who dies. Wisdom warns against pursuing death and says God neither made death nor delights in the living's destruction. The target adds a claim about death's origin that this source does not make.

Ezekiel 19:1
“And you, take up a lament over Israel’s ruler,
  Ezekiel 32:2 — parallel prophetic royal laments using animal imagery: Ezekiel is commanded to lament Israel's ruler, introducing the lion imagery that follows. The later command names Pharaoh and introduces lion and dragon images. The genre and figurative treatment of rulers correspond without identifying the two rulers.

Ezekiel 19:2
and say: What was your mother? A cub! She came to be among lions; among lions she multiplied her cubs.
  Genesis 49:9-10 — related lion-cub imagery in a royal family setting: The mother in the ruler's lament is called a cub and multiplies cubs among lions. Genesis calls Judah a lion's cub and follows it with language of rulers and leaders. The source's mother-as-cub wording is preserved rather than replaced with lioness or identified with the male Judah of the target.

Ezekiel 19:3
One of her cubs sprang away; he became a lion, learned to seize prey, and ate human beings.
  Proverbs 28:15 — related predatory lion imagery for harmful rule: The ruler's cub becomes a lion that seizes prey and eats people. Proverbs compares a poor tyrant over a needy nation to a hungry lion and thirsty wolf. The common predatory rule does not imply identical images or describe Ezekiel's ruler as poor.

Ezekiel 19:4
Nations heard against him; he was caught in their destruction, and they led him with a muzzle to Egypt’s land.
  2 Kings 23:33-34 — related forcible removal of a Judean ruler to Egypt: The lion is captured and led with a muzzle to Egypt. Kings narrates Joachas being removed from rule and brought by Pharaoh to Egypt. This offers a named instance of royal removal to that destination without assigning the unnamed lion a definitive historical identity.

Ezekiel 19:6
He moved among lions, became a lion, learned to seize prey, and ate human beings.
  Ezekiel 22:25 — close predatory lion imagery for rulers devouring lives: The cub becomes a lion, learns to seize prey and eats human beings. The later indictment likens leaders to roaring lions seizing prey and devouring lives. Both use lion predation to portray destructive leadership.

Ezekiel 19:7
He fed in his boldness, desolated their cities, and destroyed the land and its fullness by the sound of his roaring.
  Ezekiel 22:25 — related devastation caused by roaring predatory leaders: The lion desolates cities and destroys the land through its roaring. The later oracle depicts roaring lion-like leaders devouring lives and multiplying widows. The source describes devastated territory, while the target states the harm to people more directly.

Ezekiel 19:8
Nations from the surrounding countries set themselves against him and spread their nets over him; he was caught in their destruction.
  Ezekiel 32:3 — close capture of an animal-figured ruler in the nations' nets: Nations spread nets over the lion and capture him. In the Pharaoh oracle God throws nets of many peoples over the animal-figured ruler. The agents' relation to God and the animal figures differ, but the political net imagery is particular.

Ezekiel 19:9
They put him in a muzzle and a cage. He came to Babylon’s king, and he brought him into prison, so that his voice would not be heard upon Israel’s mountains.
  2 Kings 25:27 — related royal imprisonment under Babylon's king: The muzzled lion is brought to Babylon's king and prison so that its voice is no longer heard in Israel. Kings records a Judean king named Joakim being released from his prison house by Babylon's king. The target establishes a named Babylonian royal imprisonment without resolving which ruler Ezekiel's unnamed lion represents.

Ezekiel 19:10
Your mother was like a vine, like a blossom on a pomegranate tree, planted by water; her fruit and her shoot came from abundant water.
  Psalms 1:3 — related fruitfulness of a plant supplied by water: The mother is compared to a vine and a pomegranate blossom, with fruit and shoot from abundant water. The psalm compares its righteous person to a tree beside waters yielding seasonal fruit. The source's maternal and national setting differs from the psalm's individual moral description.

Ezekiel 19:11
She had a rod of strength for a tribe of leaders; it rose in her size among the trunks, and it saw her size among the multitude of her branches.
  Psalms 109:2 — related rod-of-strength language associated with rule: The vine has a rod of strength for a tribe of leaders. The psalm speaks of the rod of power sent from Zion and commands dominion among enemies. The shared rod-and-rule association does not supply the source's full plant arrangement or identify its ruler with the psalm's addressee.

Ezekiel 19:12
She was broken off in wrath and thrown to the ground. The scorching wind dried her choice parts; her rod of strength was punished and dried up. Fire consumed her.
  Ezekiel 17:10 — close judgment through a scorching wind drying a vine: Both plant parables describe a scorching wind causing drying. The source adds being broken off, thrown to the ground and consumed by fire; the earlier parable specifically says the vine dries with its clod. The different added details are preserved.

Ezekiel 19:13
Now they have planted her in the wilderness, in a waterless land,
  Jeremiah 17:6 — related image of a plant consigned to wilderness: Ezekiel's mother-vine is planted in a waterless wilderness. Jeremiah compares one who trusts human strength with a tamarisk dwelling in wilderness and uninhabited salt land. The environments communicate deprivation, without assigning Jeremiah's exact individual offense to the source's maternal figure.

Ezekiel 19:14
and fire went out from a rod of her choice parts and devoured her, and there was no rod of strength in her. A tribe is for a parable of lament, and it will become a lament.”
  Judges 9:15 — related royal plant parables ending in fire from within the vegetation: Fire comes from a rod of the vine's choice parts and devours it, leaving no rod of strength. The bramble offered kingship threatens fire from itself to consume Lebanon's cedars. The common self-originating destructive fire belongs to different political plant parables and different plant species.

Ezekiel 20:1
In the seventh year, in the fifth month, on the tenth of the month, men from the elders of the house of Israel came to question the Lord and sat before me.
  Ezekiel 14:1 — close scene of Israel's elders sitting before the prophet: Ezekiel records elders coming to inquire of the Lord and sitting before him. The earlier scene likewise has men from Israel's elders coming and sitting before him, followed by God's challenge to their approach. The earlier passage supplies no corresponding date.

Ezekiel 20:3
“Son of man, speak to Israel’s elders and say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: Have you come to question Me? As I live, I will certainly not answer you, says the Lord.’
  Ezekiel 14:3 — parallel divine challenge to the elders' consultation: The Lord challenges the elders' coming to question Him and refuses to answer. The earlier oracle questions whether He should answer men who set thoughts upon their hearts and the punishment of their injustices before their faces. Its following verses describe an answer in their entanglement, so the two responses are not presented as identical.

Ezekiel 20:4
Shall I indeed exact retribution upon them? Son of man, solemnly testify to them about their fathers’ lawlessness,
  Nehemiah 9:16-17 — related historical indictment of the fathers' disobedience: Ezekiel is told to testify to the fathers' lawlessness before the historical recital. The prayer in Nehemiah confesses the fathers' arrogant refusal to hear commandments and their attempted return to Egyptian slavery. It supplies an example of the same ancestral indictment in a penitential prayer rather than this prophetic challenge.

Ezekiel 20:5
and say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: From the day I chose the house of Israel, I became known to the seed of Jacob’s house and was known to them in Egypt’s land. I supported them with My hand, saying, “I am the Lord your God.”
  Exodus 6:6-7 — close declaration of Israel's God in the Egyptian deliverance setting: Ezekiel recalls God's choosing Israel and becoming known to Jacob's house in Egypt as the Lord their God. Exodus promises deliverance from Egypt, taking Israel as God's people and their knowing Him as the Lord their God. The source's supporting hand is not converted into an unstated oath.

Ezekiel 20:6
On that day I supported them with My hand to bring them out of Egypt’s land into the land I prepared for them, a land flowing with milk and honey; it is a honeycomb compared with all the earth.
  Exodus 3:8 — close promise of departure from Egypt into milk-and-honey land: Both passages describe God bringing Israel out of Egypt into a land flowing with milk and honey. Exodus calls it good and spacious and names its peoples; Ezekiel additionally compares it to a honeycomb relative to all the earth.

Ezekiel 20:7
I said to them, “Let each cast away the abominations of his eyes, and do not be defiled by Egypt’s practices. I am the Lord your God.”
  Leviticus 18:3 — close rejection of Egyptian practices: Ezekiel recalls the command to cast away abominations and avoid Egypt's practices. Leviticus forbids following the practices of the land of Egypt where Israel lived, also naming Canaan. This particular rejected model of conduct is explicit in both.

Ezekiel 20:8
They revolted from Me and would not listen to Me. They did not cast away the abominations of their eyes or abandon Egypt’s practices. I said I would pour My wrath upon them and complete My anger in them in the midst of Egypt’s land.
  Psalms 105:6-7 — related ancestral rebellion associated with Egypt: Ezekiel describes disobedience and refusal to abandon Egyptian practices. The psalm confesses ancestral sin and says the fathers in Egypt failed to understand God's wonders and provoked Him at the Red Sea. The episodes share Egyptian setting and rebellion while describing different specific acts.

Ezekiel 20:9
I acted so that My name would not be profaned at all before the nations among whom they are, among whom I was known to them before their eyes by bringing them out of Egypt’s land.
  Psalms 105:8 — close preservation of the people for God's name: Ezekiel says God acted so His name would not be profaned among the nations who witnessed their departure from Egypt. The psalm says He saved them for His name's sake to make His might known. The shared stated motive is more particular than deliverance alone.

Ezekiel 20:10
I brought them out of Egypt’s land and brought them into the wilderness,
  Exodus 13:18 — close historical movement from Egypt into the wilderness: God recalls bringing the people out of Egypt into the wilderness. Exodus narrates His leading them by the wilderness road toward the Red Sea. The target specifies that route without adding it to Ezekiel's shorter recollection.

Ezekiel 20:11
and gave them My commands and made My ordinances known to them, which a person will carry out and by which he will live.
  Leviticus 18:5 — close ordinance-and-life formulation: Ezekiel says God gave ordinances which a person who does them will live by. Leviticus uses the same connection between doing God's ordinances and judgments and living by them. Ezekiel places this lawgiving within the wilderness history.

Ezekiel 20:12
I gave them My sabbaths as a sign between Me and them, so that they might know that I am the Lord who sanctifies them.
  Exodus 31:13 — close Sabbath sign identifying the Lord who sanctifies Israel: Both passages say the Sabbaths are a sign between God and Israel so that they may know He is the Lord who sanctifies them. The explicit purpose of the Sabbath sign is the central correspondence.

Ezekiel 20:13
I said to the house of Israel in the wilderness, “Walk in My commands.” But they did not walk, and they rejected My ordinances, which a person will carry out and by which he will live; they greatly profaned My sabbaths. I said I would pour My wrath upon them in the wilderness to consume them utterly.
  Numbers 15:32-36 — related wilderness Sabbath violation followed by judgment: Ezekiel accuses Israel of greatly profaning Sabbaths in the wilderness and recalls threatened wrath. Numbers narrates a specific man gathering wood on a Sabbath in the wilderness and the sentence carried out against him. The individual case illustrates the type of breach without equating his execution with the source's threatened destruction of the people.

Ezekiel 20:14
I acted so that My name would not be profaned at all before the nations from whom I brought them out before their eyes.
  Exodus 32:11-14 — related preservation of Israel in view of God's reputation before Egypt: Ezekiel gives God's avoiding profanation of His name before the witnessing nations as the reason for His action. Moses appeals to what the Egyptians might say about the Exodus if God destroys Israel, and God is appeased. The target presents an intercessor's appeal rather than Ezekiel's direct divine explanation.

Ezekiel 20:15
I too raised My hand against them in the wilderness, that I would not bring them at all into the land I gave them, a land flowing with milk and honey, a honeycomb compared with all the earth,
  Numbers 14:28-30 — close exclusion of the wilderness rebels from the promised land: Ezekiel recalls God's raised hand against bringing the generation into the good land. Numbers records the sworn sentence that the grumblers' bodies will fall in the wilderness and they will not enter the land. The target names exceptions and specifies those counted from twenty years old.

Ezekiel 20:16
because they rejected My ordinances, did not walk in My commands, and were profaning My sabbaths, and they followed the reflections of their hearts.
  Numbers 15:39-40 — related rejection of inwardly directed infidelity and neglected commandments: Ezekiel connects rejected ordinances and profaned Sabbaths with hearts following their reflections. Numbers commands remembrance of God's laws and forbids turning after one's thoughts and eyes in prostitution. The target provides a corrective to that inward misdirection without supplying Ezekiel's Sabbath accusation.

Ezekiel 20:17
My eye spared them from obliteration, and I did not make a complete end of them in the wilderness.
  Nehemiah 9:19-21 — close divine preservation despite wilderness rebellion: Ezekiel says God's eye spared the people and He did not make a complete end of them in the wilderness. The historical prayer says His compassion did not abandon them there and recounts continued guidance and nourishment. These specified provisions illustrate preservation but are not listed in the source unit.

Ezekiel 20:18
I said to their children in the wilderness, “Do not walk in your fathers’ ordinances, do not keep their judgments, do not mix yourselves with their practices, and do not be defiled.
  Zechariah 1:4 — close warning not to imitate the fathers' evil practices: Ezekiel tells the children not to walk in their fathers' ordinances or become defiled by their practices. Zechariah likewise warns against becoming like the fathers who ignored calls to leave evil ways and practices. The target describes former prophetic warnings rather than the source's wilderness speech to children.

Ezekiel 20:19
I am the Lord your God. Walk in My commands, keep My ordinances and carry them out,
  Leviticus 18:4 — close command to carry out God's own judgments and ordinances: God identifies Himself and commands walking in His commands and doing His ordinances. Leviticus likewise requires doing His judgments and keeping and walking in His ordinances, identifying Him as the Lord their God. This contrasts with following the fathers' practices in the preceding source unit.

Ezekiel 20:20
and sanctify My sabbaths; let it be a sign between Me and you, so that you may know that I am the Lord your God.”
  Exodus 31:16-17 — close Sabbath observance as a sign between God and Israel: Ezekiel commands sanctifying the Sabbaths as a sign between God and the people. Exodus commands their continuing observance and calls the Sabbath an everlasting sign between God and Israel. The target additionally explains the sign through creation and divine rest.

Ezekiel 20:21
Their children too embittered Me. They did not walk in My commands or keep My ordinances to carry them out, which a person will carry out and by which he will live; they were profaning My sabbaths. I said I would pour My wrath upon them in the wilderness and complete My anger upon them.
  Nehemiah 9:29-30 — close disobedience to life-giving ordinances despite divine testimony: Ezekiel says the children failed to keep the ordinances by which the one doing them lives. Nehemiah's prayer repeats that life-giving-law formulation while confessing refusal to listen and recounting God's repeated testimony. It does not separately identify these people as Ezekiel's wilderness children or expressly mention their Sabbath profanation in this range.

Ezekiel 20:22
I acted so that My name would not be profaned at all before the nations from whom I brought them out before their eyes.
  Exodus 32:11-14 — related restraint from destruction in view of the nations' perception: God recalls acting to avoid profaning His name before those who saw the Exodus. Moses' plea similarly invokes the Egyptians' possible account of a destructive Exodus, after which God is appeased concerning the threatened calamity. The source gives the divine motive, while the target narrates the intercession.

Ezekiel 20:23
I raised My hand against them in the wilderness, to scatter them among the nations and disperse them among the countries,
  Psalms 105:26-27 — close raised-hand sentence of scattering among nations: Ezekiel recalls lifting His hand in the wilderness to scatter the people among nations and countries. The psalm likewise says God lifted His hand to cast them down in the wilderness and scatter their offspring among nations and countries. The psalm explicitly distinguishes the original generation from its offspring.

Ezekiel 20:24
because they did not carry out My ordinances, rejected My commands and were profaning My sabbaths, while their eyes followed their fathers’ reflections.
  Numbers 15:39-40 — related turning of the eyes from God's commandments: Ezekiel connects rejection of judgments, commands and Sabbaths with eyes following the fathers' reflections. Numbers commands remembering and doing the commandments rather than turning after one's thoughts and eyes. The target does not name the fathers' practices or mention Sabbaths in these verses.

Ezekiel 20:26
I will defile them through their gifts, when I pass through every one who opens the womb, so that I may destroy them.
  Exodus 13:2 — contrasting statements concerning gifts and womb-opening firstborn: Ezekiel speaks of defilement through gifts when God passes through every womb-opener, with destruction as the stated purpose. Exodus commands consecration of every womb-opening firstborn as God's own. The particular firstborn language is related, without defining Ezekiel's passing-through clause as an explicit statement about burning children.

Ezekiel 20:28
I brought them into the land I raised My hand to give them. They saw every high hill and every shady tree; there they sacrificed to their gods, there they set a fragrant aroma, and there they poured their libations.
  Hosea 4:13 — close sacrifices on heights and beneath shady trees: Ezekiel describes sacrifice, fragrant offerings and libations at high hills and shady trees. Hosea likewise names sacrifices on mountaintops and hills beneath trees that provide shade. The target names particular trees and adds consequences for the daughters and daughters-in-law.

Ezekiel 20:30
