Therefore say to the house of Israel, ‘Thus says the Lord: Are you being defiled in your fathers’ lawlessness and prostituting yourselves after their abominations?
  Judges 2:19 — related renewal of the fathers' corrupt religious practices: Ezekiel accuses the people of being defiled in their fathers' manner and prostituting themselves in their abominations. Judges describes people returning after a judge's death to corruption worse than their fathers', following other gods and retaining their practices. The source does not name the death of a judge as its trigger.

Ezekiel 20:31
In the firstfruits of your gifts, in your separations, you are being defiled with all your reflections to this very day. Shall I answer you, house of Israel? As I live, says the Lord, I will certainly not answer you, nor will this rise upon your spirit.
  Isaiah 1:15 — related refusal to hear worshippers while wrongdoing persists: Ezekiel joins continuing defilement through offerings and reflections with God's refusal to answer inquiry. Isaiah says God will turn His eyes away and not hear multiplied supplication because the people's hands are full of blood. The rejected approaches and specified offenses differ while the incompatibility of persistent wrongdoing and an expected favorable hearing is shared.

Ezekiel 20:32
It will not be as you say: “We will be like the nations and like the tribes of the earth, serving woods and stones.”
  Deuteronomy 4:27-28 — close wood-and-stone worship language with different announced outcomes: Ezekiel rejects the people's plan to become like the nations by serving wood and stone. Deuteronomy warns that dispersion will lead to serving human-made gods of wood and stone. The objects of worship correspond; the source's rejection of their plan is not treated as the target's identical predicted outcome.

Ezekiel 20:33
Therefore, as I live, says the Lord, with a mighty hand, a high arm and outpoured wrath I will reign over you.
  Deuteronomy 4:34 — close mighty-hand and uplifted-arm language for divine action: Ezekiel announces God's reign through a mighty hand, uplifted arm and outpoured wrath. Deuteronomy recalls the mighty hand and uplifted arm by which God took a nation out of Egypt. The source applies this forceful language to His rule over Israel rather than simply repeating the Exodus event.

Ezekiel 20:34
I will bring you out from the peoples and receive you from the countries where you were scattered, with a mighty hand, a high arm and outpoured wrath.
  Ezekiel 36:24 — close gathering from peoples and countries: God promises to bring the people out from the peoples and gather them from countries where they were scattered. The later oracle likewise gathers from nations and countries and brings them into their own land. The source additionally names mighty hand, uplifted arm and wrath.

Ezekiel 20:35
I will bring you into the wilderness of the peoples and contend with you there, face to face.
  Hosea 2:16-17 — related divine encounter after placement in a wilderness: Ezekiel announces being brought to the wilderness of the peoples for face-to-face contention. Hosea's feminine figure is placed in a wilderness where God speaks to her heart, with the Exodus recalled. The wilderness encounter corresponds while contention and the target's promised possessions and understanding are distinguished.

Ezekiel 20:36
As I contended with your fathers in the wilderness of Egypt’s land, so I will judge you, says the Lord.
  Numbers 14:11-12 — related wilderness contention with the rebellious ancestors: Ezekiel says the coming contention will resemble His contention with the fathers in Egypt's wilderness. Numbers records God's challenge to the people's persistent provocation and unbelief and His threatened destruction. The target supplies a concrete wilderness judgment scene without claiming the source uniquely names this episode.

Ezekiel 20:37
I will lead you beneath My rod and bring you in by number,
  Leviticus 27:32 — close passing beneath a staff in a count: Ezekiel says He will lead the people beneath the rod and bring them in by number. Leviticus describes cattle and sheep passing under the staff in the count for the tithe. The legal animal-counting image illuminates the source's wording without replacing it with an unstated bond-of-covenant clause.

Ezekiel 20:38
and select from you the impious and those who have revolted, for I will bring them out of their sojourning, but they will not enter Israel’s land. You will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Numbers 14:28-32 — related exclusion of rebels from entering Israel's land: Ezekiel says the impious will be brought from their sojourning land but will not enter Israel's land. Numbers excludes the grumbling generation from the promised land while distinguishing its children and named faithful exceptions. The sources describe different stages, but departure without eventual entry is the specific shared judgment.

Ezekiel 20:39
And you, house of Israel, thus says the Lord, the Lord: Each of you, remove his practices! After these things, if you are not listening to Me, you will no longer profane My holy name with your gifts and practices.
  Isaiah 1:13-16 — related rejection of corrupt worship and command to remove evil practices: Ezekiel commands removal of practices and says they will no longer profane God's holy name through gifts and practices. Isaiah rejects corrupted offerings and festivals and commands cleansing and removal of evil. Ezekiel's actual command is not rendered as permission to serve idols.

Ezekiel 20:40
For upon My holy mountain, upon a high mountain, says the Lord, the Lord, there the whole house of Israel will serve Me to the end. There I will accept, and there I will attend to your firstfruits and the firstfruits of your separations, with all your holy things.
  Isaiah 56:6-7 — close accepted worship on God's holy mountain: Ezekiel promises that Israel will serve God on His holy mountain, with offerings accepted. Isaiah promises the attached foreigners joy on His holy mountain and acceptance of their sacrifices. The common mountain and acceptance are explicit, while the named worshipping groups differ.

Ezekiel 20:41
As a fragrant aroma I will accept you when I bring you out from the peoples and receive you from the countries where you were scattered. I will be sanctified among you before the eyes of the peoples.
  Ezekiel 36:23-24 — close gathering accompanied by divine sanctification before the nations: Ezekiel joins gathering the scattered people with God's being sanctified in them before the peoples. The later oracle similarly joins sanctification before the nations' eyes with gathering from countries. It does not include this source's fragrant-aroma wording.
  2 Corinthians 2:14-16 — related application of fragrant-aroma imagery to people before God: God says He will accept the gathered people as a fragrant aroma. Paul describes the ministering we as Christ's fragrance to God among both those being saved and those perishing. The human-fragrance imagery corresponds, without identifying the restored people with the apostolic speakers or equating all the outcomes.

Ezekiel 20:42
You will recognize that I am the Lord when I bring you into Israel’s land, into the land for which I raised My hand to give it to your fathers.
  Exodus 6:8 — close promise of the land pledged to the fathers by an extended hand: Ezekiel recalls bringing Israel into the land pledged to the fathers with God's raised hand. Exodus likewise promises the land over which He stretched out His hand, expressly naming Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. The source expressly promises recognition; Exodus concludes I am the Lord, with the preceding 6:7 also promising recognition.

Ezekiel 20:43
There you will remember your ways and your practices by which you were being defiled; you will beat your faces over all your evils.
  Ezekiel 36:31 — close remembrance of defiled ways with differing responses: Both passages say the restored people will remember their evil ways and practices. This source says they will beat their faces over their evils; the later passage says they will feel disgust for their lawless deeds. The bodily action is not replaced with the target's wording of disgust.

Ezekiel 20:44
You will recognize that I am the Lord when I deal thus with you so that My name may not be profaned according to your evil ways and corrupt practices, says the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 36:22 — close divine action explained by concern for the holy name: Ezekiel connects God's dealings with the people and their recognition of Him to avoiding profanation of His name. The later oracle expressly says He acts for His holy name, profaned among the nations, rather than for Israel's sake. The latter explicit contrast clarifies the comparison without replacing the source's own syntax.

Ezekiel 21:3
and say to the Nageb forest, ‘Hear the Lord’s word! Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am kindling a fire within you. It will devour every green tree and every dry tree in you. The kindled flame will not be quenched, and every face from east to north will be burned by it.
  Luke 23:31 — related paired green-and-dry wood imagery in a warning of judgment: Ezekiel describes fire consuming both green and dry trees. Jesus asks what will happen with dry wood if such things are done with green wood. The paired kinds of wood correspond, while Luke does not expressly name a forest fire in this saying.

Ezekiel 21:4
All flesh will recognize that I, the Lord, have kindled it, and it will not be quenched.’”
  Jeremiah 17:27 — close divine fire that will not be extinguished: Ezekiel says all flesh will recognize that God kindled the fire and that it will not be extinguished. Jeremiah likewise announces God kindling a fire that consumes Jerusalem's quarters and is not extinguished. The source's forest imagery differs from the target's gates and quarters.

Ezekiel 21:5
I said, “By no means, Lord, Lord! They say to me, ‘Is this not a parable being spoken?’”
  Ezekiel 17:2 — related description and divine command of prophetic parable speech: The prophet reports people calling his speech a parable. Earlier he was expressly commanded to tell a story and speak a parable to Israel. The connection concerns the stated mode of speech without deciding whether the reported audience meant its remark as a rejection.

Ezekiel 21:7
“Therefore prophesy, son of man; set your face firmly toward Jerusalem, look toward their holy places, and prophesy against Israel’s land,
  Ezekiel 4:1-3 — close face-setting toward Jerusalem in a prophetic siege setting: Ezekiel is commanded to set his face toward Jerusalem and prophesy against Israel's land. The earlier siege sign requires drawing Jerusalem on a brick and preparing his face against it. The target supplies a physical sign that this source does not separately command.

Ezekiel 21:8
and say to Israel’s land, ‘Behold, I am against you. I will draw My dagger from its sheath and destroy from you the unjust and the lawless.
  Deuteronomy 32:41 — related divine sword employed in judgment: God draws His dagger against the unjust and lawless. Deuteronomy describes His sharpened sword, hand taking hold of judgment and repayment to enemies. The target's enemies are not substituted for the source's exact unjust-and-lawless wording.

Ezekiel 21:9
Because I will destroy from you the unjust and the lawless, so My dagger will come out of its sheath against all flesh from east to north.
  Isaiah 66:16 — close divine sword judgment extending to all flesh: Ezekiel says God's dagger goes against all flesh from east to north. Isaiah likewise speaks of the Lord's sword judging all flesh. The target adds fire and the whole earth; neither supplies Ezekiel's particular directional wording.

Ezekiel 21:11
And you, son of man, groan deeply with your loins broken; groan in pains before their eyes.
  Isaiah 21:2-3 — related groaning and afflicted loins in response to a harsh vision: Ezekiel is commanded to groan with broken loins and pain. Isaiah's speaker groans and describes loins filled with weakness and labor pains after a harsh vision. The target's childbirth comparison is not inserted into Ezekiel's instruction.

Ezekiel 21:12
If they say to you, ‘Why are you groaning?’ say, ‘Because of the message, for it is coming. Every heart will be shattered, all hands will be paralyzed, all flesh and every spirit will expire, and all thighs will be defiled with moisture. Behold, it is coming and will happen, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Isaiah 13:7-8 — close bodily collapse at announced judgment: Ezekiel predicts broken hearts and paralyzed hands when the report arrives. Isaiah's Babylon oracle describes slackened hands, cowardly souls and distress. The correspondence concerns disabled strength and fear, not an equivalence between Ezekiel's moist thighs and wording absent from Isaiah.

Ezekiel 21:14
“Son of man, prophesy and say, ‘Thus says the Lord: Say: Sword, sword, be sharpened and enraged,
  Psalms 7:12-13 — related preparation of a divine sword for judgment: The prophetic command calls for the sword to be sharpened and enraged. The psalm describes the righteous divine judge polishing His sword if the addressees do not return. The psalm supplies an express conditional return clause absent from this source unit.

Ezekiel 21:15
so that you may slaughter victims. Be sharpened to become gleaming. Ready for paralysis, slaughter! Disdain; thrust away every tree.
  Deuteronomy 32:41 — close gleaming or lightning-like sword prepared for judgment: Ezekiel commands sharpening the sword to gleam for slaughter. Deuteronomy describes God sharpening His sword like lightning to take judgment in hand. The target does not explain the source's unusual command concerning every tree.

Ezekiel 21:16
He gave it ready for his hand to grasp. The sword has been sharpened; it is ready to be put into the hand of one who pierces.
  Psalms 7:13-14 — related preparation of deadly weapons: Ezekiel says the sharpened sword is ready to be placed in the piercer's hand. The psalm describes the sword, prepared bow and instruments of death. The shared readiness for judgment does not make the sword and bow identical or identify the source's unnamed piercer.

Ezekiel 21:17
Cry aloud and wail, son of man, for it has come among My people, among all Israel’s leaders. They will sojourn upon a sword; it has come among My people. Therefore strike upon your hand.
  Ezekiel 6:11 — close prophetic hand gesture accompanying announced judgment: Ezekiel is commanded to cry, wail and strike his hand because the sword has come among Israel's people and leaders. The earlier command likewise includes clapping the hand while announcing sword, death and famine for Israel's abominations. Its foot-stamping and spoken exclamation are additional actions.

Ezekiel 21:19
And you, son of man, prophesy and strike hand upon hand, and double the sword. The third sword of the wounded is the great sword of the wounded, and it will terrify them,
  Ezekiel 6:11 — close hand-clapping within a prophetic judgment announcement: The prophet is told to prophesy and strike hand upon hand while speaking about the sword. The earlier oracle also commands a hand clap in an announcement of Israel's downfall. This supports the performed gesture without resolving the source's difficult third-sword expression.

Ezekiel 21:22
I too will strike My hand against My hand and release My wrath. I, the Lord, have spoken.’”
  Ezekiel 22:13 — close divine hand-clapping over the people's judgment: God says He will clap His hands and release His wrath. The following oracle speaks conditionally of God clapping His hands over the city's bloodshed. The actor is God in both, distinct from commands elsewhere for the prophet to clap.

Ezekiel 21:26
For Babylon’s king will stand upon the ancient road, at the beginning of the two roads, to divine a divination, toss a rod, question the carved images and inspect a liver at his right.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-14 — contrast between Babylonian royal divination and Israel's prohibition: Babylon's king uses divination at the road junction, including a rod, carved images and a liver. Deuteronomy prohibits divination and says the nations heed it but Israel is not granted that practice. The law does not list the source's three particular implements or imply that the narrative endorses the practice.

Ezekiel 21:27
The oracle came against Jerusalem: to set a siege stake, open the mouth in a shout, raise the voice with a cry, set a siege stake against her gates, cast up a mound and build missile stations.
  Jeremiah 52:4 — related Babylonian preparations for Jerusalem's siege: The oracle selects Jerusalem for siege works, missile stations and shouting. Jeremiah records Babylon's king and force enclosing Jerusalem with a palisade and building around it. The historical counterpart does not narrate the preceding divination or make every siege feature identical.

Ezekiel 21:29
Therefore thus says the Lord: Because you recalled your injustices when your impieties were revealed, so that your sins were seen in all your impieties and practices—because you recalled them, in these you will be captured.
  Hosea 8:13 — related remembrance of wrongdoing followed by punitive consequences: Ezekiel says the people recalled their injustices as their impieties were revealed and announces capture. Hosea says God will remember their injustices and punish their sins, in a passage naming Egypt and Assyria. The remembering subjects and the different movements described are preserved.

Ezekiel 21:30
And you, profane, lawless leader of Israel, whose day has arrived in a season of injustice—an end!—
  Ezekiel 21:34 — same-chapter repeated appointed-day judgment formula: The profane leader of Israel is addressed as one whose day has arrived in a season of injustice, an end. The Ammon oracle repeats that formulation concerning wounded lawless people. The repeated judgment language links the two sections without identifying their persons as the same.

Ezekiel 21:31
thus says the Lord: You removed the tiara and put on the crown. This will not be such. You lowered the high and raised the low.
  Ezekiel 17:24 — related reversal of high and low with distinct grammatical agents: This source says its addressee removed the tiara, put on the crown, lowered the high and raised the low. The cedar oracle says God lowers the high tree and raises the low. The shared reversal does not turn this source into a command to remove the crown or make its second-person acts God's first-person acts.

Ezekiel 21:32
Injustice, injustice I will make it! Nor will this be such until the one comes to whom it belongs, and I will hand it over to him.
  Genesis 49:10 — related expectation of a future rightful recipient in a royal setting: Ezekiel looks toward the one to whom it belongs, to whom God will hand it over. Genesis says the ruler from Judah will not fail until the things reserved for him come, followed by the expectation of nations. The future royal language is related without claiming the passages give an identical name or wording.

Ezekiel 21:33
And you, son of man, prophesy and say, ‘Thus says the Lord concerning the sons of Ammon and their reproach.’ Say, ‘Sword, sword, drawn for victims and drawn for complete destruction, arise so that you may gleam,
  Ezekiel 25:2-7 — close judgment oracles concerning Ammon and its hostile response: Ezekiel's sword oracle expressly concerns Ammon's sons and their reproach. The later oracle condemns their rejoicing at the profanation and devastation of Israel and announces punishment. It specifies a hostile response not fully narrated in this source unit.

Ezekiel 21:34
in your vain vision and your divining false things, to hand you over upon the necks of the wounded lawless, whose day has arrived in a season of injustice—an end!
  Ezekiel 13:6-9 — close false vision and vain divination brought under judgment: The source names vain vision and false divination within the Ammon judgment. The earlier oracle likewise condemns prophets seeing false things and uttering vain divinations and announces God's opposition. The different addressees and penalties are not merged.

Ezekiel 21:36
and pour My anger upon you. I will blow upon you with the fire of My anger and hand you into the hands of barbarian men devising destruction.
  Isaiah 30:27 — related divine anger expressed as consuming fire: God says He will blow upon the addressee with the fire of His anger. Isaiah describes the anger of His wrath consuming like fire. The target supports the fiery-anger image without supplying a breath clause or the source's barbarian destroyers.

Ezekiel 21:37
You will be food in fire; your blood will be in the midst of your land. There certainly will be no remembrance of you, for I, the Lord, have spoken.’”
  Ezekiel 25:7 — close announced destruction of Ammon among the peoples: The Ammon oracle predicts fiery destruction and no further remembrance. The later oracle likewise promises to destroy Ammon from the peoples and countries. Its description of plunder and removal is not replaced with the source's exact fire-and-blood details.

Ezekiel 22:2
“And you, son of man, will you judge the city of bloodshed? Display all her lawless deeds to her,
  Nahum 3:1 — close prophetic indictment of a city of bloodshed: Ezekiel is commanded to judge the city of bloodshed and expose its lawlessness. Nahum similarly addresses a city of bloodshed, adding falsehood and injustice. Nahum's surrounding oracle names Nineveh, so the two cities are not identified with each other.

Ezekiel 22:3
and say, ‘Thus says the Lord, the Lord: O city shedding blood within her so that her time may come, making reflections against herself to defile herself!
  Ezekiel 36:17-19 — related civic or national defilement followed by judgment: The city sheds blood and makes reflections that defile it as its time approaches. The later oracle describes Israel's defiling the land through its ways, idols and uncleannesses, followed by wrath and scattering. That target does not expressly repeat the bloodshed clause in this edition.

Ezekiel 22:4
Through their blood that you shed you have transgressed, and through your reflections that you made you were being defiled. You brought your days near and brought the season of your years. Therefore I have made you a reproach to the nations and a mockery to all the countries
  Deuteronomy 28:36-37 — related public disgrace among the nations: God makes the guilty city a reproach and an object of mockery among nations and countries. Deuteronomy warns that the displaced people will become a riddle, parable and tale among nations. The shared public example of judgment preserves the target's wording rather than replacing it with mockery.

Ezekiel 22:5
near you and far away from you. They will mock you: “Unclean, the famous one, abundant in lawless deeds!”
  Lamentations 1:7-9 — related humiliation of the sinful city before observers: Those near and far mock the city's uncleanness and lawlessness. Lamentations describes Jerusalem's enemies laughing at her deportation, former admirers humbling her and her uncleanness exposed. It does not specify Ezekiel's near-and-far groups.

Ezekiel 22:7
They reviled father and mother in you and dealt unjustly with the sojourner in you. They oppressed the orphan and widow in you.
  Exodus 20:12 — contrast between reviling parents and the command to honor them: Ezekiel accuses the city of reviling father and mother. Exodus commands honoring father and mother, attaching a promise of well-being and long life. This target addresses the parental clause, distinct from the source's treatment of vulnerable outsiders.
  Exodus 22:20-23 — close violations concerning the sojourner, orphan and widow: Ezekiel accuses the people of injustice toward the sojourner and oppression of orphan and widow. Exodus prohibits wronging the foreigner and harming widow or orphan, warning of God's hearing their cry. The three protected groups correspond directly.

Ezekiel 22:8
They despised My holy things and profaned My Sabbaths in you.
  Leviticus 19:30 — close inverse of the paired Sabbath-and-holy-things command: Ezekiel accuses the people of despising God's holy things and profaning His Sabbaths. Leviticus pairs keeping His Sabbaths with revering His holy things. The accusation directly reverses both duties in that command.

Ezekiel 22:9
Robbers are in you in order to shed blood in you. They ate upon the mountains in you; they committed unholy acts within you.
  Proverbs 1:10-16 — related association of robbery with bloodshed: Ezekiel identifies robbers in the city who shed blood. Proverbs depicts an invitation to bloodshed and seizure of costly property and warns against those paths. The target supports that violent robbery clause, not Ezekiel's separate accusation of mountain eating.

Ezekiel 22:10
They uncovered a father’s shame in you, and they violated a woman sitting apart in uncleanness in you.
  Leviticus 18:7-8 — close violation concerning the father's nakedness: Ezekiel says the father's shame is uncovered in the city. Leviticus expressly prohibits uncovering the father's nakedness and explains the prohibition regarding his wife. The source does not separately name the particular woman in this clause.
  Leviticus 18:19 — close prohibition concerning a woman in menstrual separation: Ezekiel accuses the people of violating a woman during her menstrual separation. Leviticus forbids approaching a woman in that separation to uncover her nakedness. This is distinct from the source's preceding paternal-shame accusation.

Ezekiel 22:11
Each acted lawlessly with his neighbor’s wife; each defiled his daughter-in-law with impiety; each violated his sister, his father’s daughter, in you.
  Leviticus 18:20 — close prohibition against adultery with a neighbor's wife: Ezekiel accuses one person of unlawful conduct with his neighbor's wife. Leviticus expressly prohibits intercourse with that wife and consequent defilement. The source goes on to list separate kinship offenses.
  Leviticus 18:9-15 — close named kinship prohibitions involving sister and daughter-in-law: Ezekiel names defilement of a daughter-in-law and violation of a sister who is the father's daughter. The selected laws explicitly prohibit uncovering a paternal sister and a daughter-in-law, among other relatives. The target's additional kinship categories are not assigned to this source unit.

Ezekiel 22:12
They took gifts in you in order to shed blood; they took interest and increase in you. You brought your evil in oppression to its full completion, but you forgot Me, says the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 27:25 — close prohibition against accepting payment for bloodshed: Ezekiel condemns taking gifts in order to shed blood. Deuteronomy pronounces a curse on taking a bribe to kill an innocent life. The target explicitly specifies innocence; the source also lists lending abuses and forgetting God.
  Leviticus 25:36-37 — close violation of the interest-and-increase prohibition: Ezekiel names taking interest and increase among the city's offenses. Leviticus prohibits those practices toward the impoverished brother. This legal connection addresses the lending clause rather than the distinct bribe-for-bloodshed charge.

Ezekiel 22:13
But if I strike My hand against My hand over what you have completed, what you did, and over your bloodshed that occurred within you,
  Ezekiel 21:22 — close divine hand-clapping in judgment: God speaks conditionally of clapping His hands over the source city's deeds and bloodshed. The preceding sword oracle likewise has God clap His hands and release His wrath. The divine actor and judgment gesture are shared without inserting the target's sword into this source unit.

Ezekiel 22:14
will your heart hold out? Will your hands prevail in the days when I act within you? I, the Lord, have spoken and will act.
  Nahum 1:6 — close rhetorical challenge about enduring divine anger: Ezekiel asks whether the people's heart and hands can remain strong in the days when God acts. Nahum asks who can stand before His anger or resist His wrath. The target adds rulers melting and rocks being crushed rather than the source's heart-and-hands formulation.

Ezekiel 22:15
I will scatter you among the nations and disperse you among the countries, and your uncleanness will cease from you.
  Ezekiel 36:19-25 — related scattering and eventual removal of uncleanness: Ezekiel joins scattering among nations with the ending of uncleanness. The later passage recalls scattering, then promises gathering and cleansing by clean water. The comparison preserves those distinct narrated stages rather than asserting that the cleansing mechanism is identical.

Ezekiel 22:18
“Son of man, behold, Israel’s house has become mixed to Me, all of them with copper, iron, tin and lead; it is mixed amid silver.
  Isaiah 1:21-22 — related compromised-metal imagery for a corrupt community: Israel's house is described as a mixture of copper, iron, tin, lead and silver. Isaiah addresses the corrupt city Zion and calls her silver unapproved. The source does not expressly call its mixture dross, and the target does not list all its metals.

Ezekiel 22:19
Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: Because all of you became one mixture, therefore I am receiving you into Jerusalem.
  Jeremiah 6:27-28 — related metal imagery for the corrupt people facing divine assessment: God calls the people a mixture and announces gathering them into Jerusalem, following the list of metals. Jeremiah's testing oracle describes the people as bronze and iron and calls them corrupted. The target supports the characterization but does not describe the source's gathering into Jerusalem.

Ezekiel 22:20
As silver, copper, iron, tin and lead are received into a furnace to blow fire into it to melt it, so I will receive you in My anger, gather you and melt you,
  Proverbs 17:3 — related furnace imagery applied to God's dealings with people: God compares gathering the people in wrath with metals placed in a furnace and melted. Proverbs compares silver and gold tested in a furnace with hearts chosen before the Lord. The source's destructive melting is not simply renamed the target's testing or choosing.

Ezekiel 22:21
and blow upon you with the fire of My anger, and you will be melted within her.
  Psalms 17:16 — related blast associated with divine anger: Ezekiel says God will blow upon the gathered people with the fire of His wrath and melt them. The psalm describes exposed foundations and springs at the blast of the spirit of God's anger. The shared angry blast produces different explicitly described effects.

Ezekiel 22:22
As silver is melted within a furnace, so you will be melted within her. You will recognize that I, the Lord, have poured My wrath upon you.’”
  Psalms 65:10-12 — related divine treatment of people through fire-and-silver imagery: Ezekiel compares the people melting under wrath with silver melting in a furnace. The psalm says God tried His people by fire as silver and brought them through affliction to refreshment. The target's stated testing and refreshing outcome are not imposed on Ezekiel's wrath oracle.

Ezekiel 22:24
“Son of man, say to her, ‘You are a land not wetted, nor did rain come upon you on the day of wrath.
  Deuteronomy 28:23-24 — related absence of sustaining rain under divine judgment: Ezekiel calls the land unwetted and without rain on the day of wrath. Deuteronomy's curses describe bronze heaven and dust given instead of rain. The target supplies different atmospheric imagery for the same loss of life-sustaining rainfall.

Ezekiel 22:25
Her leaders within her are like roaring lions seizing prey, devouring lives with power, taking payments unjustly, and your widows have multiplied within you.
  Zephaniah 3:3 — close comparison of rulers with roaring lions: Ezekiel's leaders are like a roaring lion seizing prey and devouring lives. Zephaniah likewise calls the city's rulers roaring lions. The source says leaders, not prophets, and adds unjust payments and multiplied widows.

Ezekiel 22:26
Her priests set aside My law and profaned My holy things. They did not distinguish between holy and profane, nor distinguish between unclean and clean. They covered their eyes from My Sabbaths, and I was profaned among them.
  Leviticus 10:10-11 — close neglect of priestly distinctions prescribed in the law: Ezekiel accuses priests of failing to distinguish holy from profane and unclean from clean. Leviticus commands precisely these distinctions and instruction of Israel in God's statutes. The source additionally names Sabbath neglect.
  Ezekiel 44:23-24 — close restoration of the priestly duties neglected in the indictment: The indicted priests fail to teach holy/profane and clean/unclean distinctions and disregard Sabbaths. The later priestly instruction explicitly requires teaching both distinctions and sanctifying Sabbaths. The latter describes prescribed future duties rather than asserting that these indicted priests already fulfilled them.

Ezekiel 22:27
Her rulers within her are like wolves seizing prey to shed blood, so that in greed they may gain more.
  Zephaniah 3:3 — close predatory-wolf imagery for civic authorities: Ezekiel compares rulers who shed blood for greedy gain with wolves seizing prey. Zephaniah compares judges with Arabian wolves leaving nothing for morning. The offices and the target's qualifier differ, while the predatory civic image corresponds.

Ezekiel 22:28
Her prophets, coating them, will fall, seeing vain things, divining falsehoods, saying, “Thus says the Lord,” when the Lord has not spoken,
  Ezekiel 13:6-11 — close conjunction of false oracles, coating and an announced fall: The source says her prophets, coating them, will fall and condemns their vain visions and claims that God spoke when He did not. The earlier oracle combines false claims of divine speech with a coated wall that will fall. The falling prophets and falling wall remain distinct; no unmentioned mortar recipe is supplied.

Ezekiel 22:29
oppressing the people of the land unjustly and seizing plunder, oppressing the poor and needy and not dealing justly with the sojourner.
  Zechariah 7:9-10 — close prohibition against oppressing the poor and outsider: The source charges oppression of the poor and needy and injustice to the sojourner. Zechariah commands righteous judgment and forbids oppressing the poor and newcomer, also naming widow and orphan. The source's particular exploited groups are directly covered.

Ezekiel 22:30
I sought among them a man conducting himself rightly and standing wholly before Me at the land’s time, so that I would not utterly wipe her out, but I found none.
  Jeremiah 5:1 — close search for a just person in order to spare the community: God says He sought someone conducting himself rightly and standing wholly before Him so that He would not destroy the land, but found none. Jeremiah commands a search through Jerusalem for one doing justice and seeking faithfulness, with mercy promised if found. The target states the condition rather than the source's explicit failed search.
  Psalms 105:23 — contrast between no suitable person found and Moses' successful intervention: Ezekiel says no person was found standing wholly before God to prevent destruction. The psalm names Moses standing in the breach to turn God's anger away from destruction. The breach belongs to the target; the source is not rewritten with hedge-or-gap wording.

Ezekiel 22:31
I poured My wrath upon her in the fire of My anger to bring an end. I brought their ways upon their own heads, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 9:10 — close bringing of the people's own ways upon their heads: Ezekiel's conclusion joins divine wrath with returning their ways upon their heads. The earlier Jerusalem judgment likewise says God has brought their ways upon their heads and will not spare. The source additionally expresses wrath through consuming fire.

Ezekiel 23:2
“Son of man, there were two women, daughters of one mother,
  Ezekiel 16:44-45 — related female family imagery for the cities: The source introduces two women from one mother, identified as cities in the following explanation. The earlier Jerusalem oracle uses mother, daughter and sister language to expose related wrongdoing. The comparison concerns the familial allegory rather than literal human genealogy.

Ezekiel 23:3
and they prostituted themselves in Egypt in their youth. There their breasts fell; there they were deflowered.
  Ezekiel 20:7-8 — related recollection of unfaithfulness in Egypt: The two women's youthful prostitution is placed in Egypt. The preceding historical indictment recalls refusal to abandon Egyptian practices and abominations. The source's sexualized portrayal of the cities differs from the historical statement about Jacob's house.

Ezekiel 23:4
Their names were Oholah the elder and Oholibah her sister. They became Mine and bore sons and daughters. Their names: Samaria is Oholah, and Jerusalem is Oholibah.
  Ezekiel 16:46 — close personification of Samaria as Jerusalem's elder sister: The source explicitly identifies Oholah with Samaria and Oholibah with Jerusalem. The earlier oracle addressed to Jerusalem calls Samaria the elder sister, also naming Sodom as another sister. That additional figure is not inserted into this source's two-woman story.

Ezekiel 23:5
Oholah prostituted herself away from Me and set her desire upon her lovers, the Assyrians approaching her,
  Hosea 8:8-10 — related turning toward Assyria within an indictment of Israel: Oholah, identified as Samaria, desires her Assyrian lovers. Hosea describes Israel among the nations and going up to the Assyrians, also naming Ephraim and love of gifts. The target does not use the source's lovers wording or identify a literal woman.

Ezekiel 23:6
clothed in blue-purple, leaders and generals; all choice young men, horsemen riding horses.
  Ezekiel 23:12 — same-chapter parallel descriptions of desired Assyrian horsemen: Oholah's Assyrian objects of desire are leaders, generals, choice young men and mounted horsemen. The description of Oholibah's desired Assyrians repeats those categories. The clothing descriptions differ and are not made identical.

Ezekiel 23:7
She bestowed her prostitution upon them, all choice sons of Assyrians; and with all upon whom she set her desire, in all her reflections she was defiled.
  Ezekiel 16:28 — related Assyrian prostitution in distinct city personifications: Samaria's female figure bestows prostitution on Assyrians' sons and becomes defiled. The earlier Jerusalem oracle describes prostitution with Assyria's daughters and continued dissatisfaction. The different personified cities and the sons-versus-daughters wording are preserved.

Ezekiel 23:8
She did not abandon her prostitution from Egypt, for they lay with her in her youth; they deflowered her and poured their prostitution upon her.
  Ezekiel 20:8 — related failure to abandon prior Egyptian practices: Oholah does not abandon the prostitution associated with her youth in Egypt. The earlier historical recital likewise says the people failed to abandon Egypt's practices despite God's command. The target uses the language of rebellion and practices rather than this sexual allegory.

Ezekiel 23:9
Therefore I handed her into her lovers’ hands, into the hands of Assyrians’ sons, upon whom she had set her desire.
  2 Kings 17:5-6 — related handing of Samaria into Assyrian power: God hands Oholah, explicitly Samaria, into the Assyrians' hands. Kings narrates the Assyrian siege and capture of Samaria and deportation of Israel. The narrative does not describe the Assyrians as her lovers, which belongs to Ezekiel's interpretation.

Ezekiel 23:10
They uncovered her shame, took her sons and daughters, and killed her with the sword. She became a subject of talk among women, and they inflicted punishments upon her, upon the daughters.
  Hosea 2:5-6 — related exposure of an unfaithful mother and threatened treatment of her children: Ezekiel describes uncovering the woman's shame, taking her children and killing her. Hosea threatens stripping the mother naked and withholding mercy from her children. The targets of the explicit killing, taking and withheld mercy remain distinct.

Ezekiel 23:11
Her sister Oholibah saw this and corrupted her desire beyond hers, and her prostitution beyond her sister’s prostitution.
  Jeremiah 3:8-11 — close failure of Judah to learn from Israel's prior unfaithfulness: Oholibah sees her sister's fate and exceeds her corruption. Jeremiah recounts Israel's dismissal and Judah's failure to fear and concludes that Israel is justified in comparison with faithless Judah. The comparison follows the source's named city identities without importing an additional literal family history.

Ezekiel 23:12
She set her desire upon Assyrians’ sons, leaders and generals near her, clothed in finely bordered garments, horsemen riding horses; all choice young men.
  Ezekiel 23:6 — same-chapter repetition of attraction to Assyrian officers and cavalry: Oholibah desires Assyrian leaders, generals and choice young horsemen, echoing the earlier description of Oholah's lovers. The repetition makes the shared course explicit, while finely bordered garments here are not renamed the earlier blue-purple clothing.

Ezekiel 23:13
I saw that she was defiled; one way for the two.
  Ezekiel 16:51 — related comparison of Jerusalem's and Samaria's corrupt ways: God says the two women share one defiled way. The earlier city oracle compares Jerusalem and Samaria, saying Jerusalem's multiplied lawlessness exceeds Samaria's. The target states a difference of degree rather than denying their common wrongdoing.

Ezekiel 23:16
She set her desire upon them at the sight of her eyes and sent messengers to them in the Chaldeans’ land.
  Isaiah 57:8-9 — close conjunction of prostitution and distant embassies: Oholibah desires the depicted Chaldeans and sends messengers to their land. Isaiah likewise joins multiplied prostitution and lovers with ambassadors sent beyond the boundaries. The target does not name the Chaldeans or describe their portraits.

Ezekiel 23:17
Babylon’s sons came to her, to a bed of lodgers, and defiled her in her prostitution. She was defiled by them, and her soul withdrew from them.
  Ezekiel 16:29 — related unfaithful involvement with Chaldea: Babylon's sons come to the woman and defile her, after her message to Chaldea. The earlier Jerusalem oracle describes multiplied covenants toward the Chaldeans' land. It supplies covenant language rather than the source's bed-of-lodgers image or subsequent withdrawal.

Ezekiel 23:18
She uncovered her prostitution and uncovered her shame. My soul withdrew from her, as My soul had withdrawn from her sister.
  Leviticus 26:30 — related divine rejection expressed in terms of God's soul: God says His soul withdrew from the woman as from her sister. Leviticus warns that His soul will be indignant with the disobedient people amid destruction of their idols. Withdrawal and indignation are related responses, not identical verbs or stages.

Ezekiel 23:19
You multiplied your prostitution to recall the days of your youth, in which you prostituted yourself in Egypt,
  Ezekiel 16:26 — related prostitution with Egypt in Jerusalem's allegory: Oholibah recalls youthful prostitution in Egypt. The earlier Jerusalem oracle likewise names prostitution with Egypt's sons. The target describes them as neighbors and does not explicitly place that act in infancy or youth.

Ezekiel 23:20
and you set your desire upon the Chaldeans, whose flesh was like donkeys’ and whose genitals were horses’ genitals.
  Ezekiel 16:26 — related sexualized descriptions of foreign involvement with different nations: The source compares the desired Chaldeans' flesh and genitals with animals. The earlier oracle describes Egypt's sons as great in flesh. The bodily imagery is related, while the source's Chaldeans are expressly not renamed Egyptians.

Ezekiel 23:21
You revisited the lawlessness of your youth, what you did in Egypt at your lodging, where your youthful breasts were.
  Ezekiel 23:8 — same-chapter parallel return to the sisters' Egyptian past: Oholibah revisits the lawlessness of her youth in Egypt. Earlier Oholah is said not to have abandoned her Egyptian prostitution. The parallel concerns the two sisters' persistent past, not an assertion that the same named human woman appears in both units.

Ezekiel 23:22
Therefore, Oholibah, thus says the Lord: Behold, I am rousing your lovers against you, those from whom your soul withdrew. I will bring them against you all around:
  Ezekiel 16:37 — close reversal in which former lovers are gathered against Jerusalem: God rouses Oholibah's former lovers and brings them against her from all around. The earlier Jerusalem oracle likewise gathers lovers, both loved and hated, against the city. The former objects of desire becoming instruments of exposure is the particular common reversal.

Ezekiel 23:24
All will come against you from the north: chariots and wheels with a multitude of peoples, large shields and small shields; they will set a guard against you all around. I will set judgment before them, and they will punish you by their judgments.
  Jeremiah 6:22-23 — close northern army with mounted forces directed against Zion: The source describes a northern force with chariots and peoples surrounding the woman identified as Jerusalem. Jeremiah likewise announces a northern people advancing with horses and chariots against Zion. The target does not reproduce the source's shields or delegated judgments.

Ezekiel 23:25
I will set My jealousy within you, and they will deal with you in fierce anger. They will remove your nose and ears and strike down your remaining people with the sword. They will take your sons and daughters, and fire will consume your remaining people.
  Ezekiel 16:38 — close jealousy and wrath in judgment of the unfaithful city: God announces jealousy and fierce anger followed by mutilation, captivity and killing. The earlier Jerusalem oracle likewise associates retribution for adultery and bloodshed with wrath and jealousy. Its selected unit does not specify removal of nose and ears or the taking of children.

Ezekiel 23:26
They will strip you of your clothing and take the objects of your boasting.
  Ezekiel 16:39 — close stripping of clothing and objects of boasting: Both city judgments say the adversaries will strip the woman's clothing and take her objects or vessels of boasting. The earlier oracle additionally names demolition of the brothel and platform and exposure of nakedness.

Ezekiel 23:27
I will turn your impieties away from you and your prostitution from Egypt’s land. You certainly will not lift your eyes toward them, and you certainly will no longer remember Egypt.
  Ezekiel 16:41 — close announced ending of the city's prostitution: God says He will turn away the woman's impieties and Egyptian prostitution. The earlier Jerusalem oracle likewise promises to turn her away from prostitution and stop her payments. The specific end of Egyptian remembrance belongs to this source rather than that target.

Ezekiel 23:28
For thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am handing you into the hands of those you hate, those from whom your soul withdrew.
  Ezekiel 16:37 — related former objects of attachment now hated and brought against the city: God hands the woman to those she hates and from whom her soul withdrew. The earlier oracle gathers against Jerusalem those she loved together with those she hated. The target includes both groups, while the source names the hated group.

Ezekiel 23:29
They will deal with you in hatred and take all the fruit of your pain and toil. You will be naked and exposed to shame, and the shame of your prostitution and your impiety will be uncovered. Your prostitution
  Ezekiel 16:39 — close plundering and naked exposure of personified Jerusalem: The hostile agents take the fruit of toil and leave the woman naked with her shame exposed. The earlier oracle likewise takes clothing and prized vessels and leaves Jerusalem naked and exposed. The descriptions of seized property differ.

Ezekiel 23:30
did these things to you when you prostituted yourself after nations and were defiled in their reflections.
  Jeremiah 2:17-19 — close attribution of harm to the people's own abandonment of God: The source attributes the woman's treatment to her prostitution after nations. Jeremiah asks whether abandoning God brought such things upon the people and says their apostasy will discipline them, in a passage naming Egypt and Assyria. The target does not describe the same attackers or every feature of the woman's punishment.

Ezekiel 23:31
You walked in your sister’s way; I will put her cup into your hands.
  Isaiah 51:17 — close judgment-cup imagery applied to Jerusalem: God places the sister's cup in Jerusalem's hands because she followed that sister's way. Isaiah addresses Jerusalem as having drunk the cup of anger from the Lord's hand. The prior transfer from Samaria belongs to Ezekiel, not the target.

Ezekiel 23:32
Thus says the Lord: You will drink your sister’s cup, deep and wide, abounding to bring about
  Jeremiah 32:15-16 — related drinking from a divinely assigned judgment cup: The source announces drinking the sister's deep, wide cup, whose effect continues into the following unit. Jeremiah is given a cup of unmixed wine for the nations, bringing vomiting and madness before the sword. The source's stated dimensions and the target's explicit wine are not made identical.

Ezekiel 23:33

  Isaiah 51:17-21 — close collapse and drunkenness associated with Jerusalem's judgment cup: The source names drunkenness, collapse and Samaria's cup of annihilation. Isaiah describes Jerusalem's cup of falling and anger and calls her drunk but not from wine. The target does not identify the cup as Samaria's.

Ezekiel 23:34
you will drink it. I will turn away her festivals and her new moons, for I have spoken, says the Lord.
  Hosea 2:13 — close removal of festivals and new moons from the unfaithful female figure: Ezekiel says God will turn away her festivals and new moons. Hosea likewise removes the woman's rejoicings, festivals, new moons, Sabbaths and assemblies. This follows the actual festival clause rather than substituting a different edition's cup-shard imagery.

Ezekiel 23:35

  Jeremiah 2:32 — related forgetting of God within feminine imagery of unfaithfulness: Ezekiel charges forgetting God and casting Him behind the body. Jeremiah contrasts the expected remembrance of bridal adornment with God's people forgetting Him for countless days. The target supports the forgetting charge but does not repeat the source's body clause.

Ezekiel 23:36
The Lord said to me, “Son of man, will you not judge Oholah and Oholibah? Declare their lawless deeds to them,
  Ezekiel 16:2 — parallel commission to expose the cities' lawlessness: Ezekiel is commanded to declare Oholah's and Oholibah's lawless deeds. The earlier oracle begins with a command to testify to Jerusalem's lawlessness before its female personification. The source addresses two named city figures rather than Jerusalem alone.

Ezekiel 23:37
for they committed adultery, and blood is on their hands. They committed adultery with their reflections, and made their children whom they bore to Me pass through fire offerings for them.
  Jeremiah 7:30-31 — close condemnation of children offered through fire: Ezekiel accuses the women of making children borne to God pass through fire offerings. Jeremiah condemns Judah's burning sons and daughters at Topheth, an act God did not command. The target names the location; the source explicitly stresses that the children were borne to God.

Ezekiel 23:38
They even did these things to Me: they defiled My holy things and profaned My Sabbaths.
  Leviticus 19:30 — close inverse of reverence for holy things and Sabbath observance: Ezekiel accuses the women of defiling holy things and profaning Sabbaths. Leviticus commands keeping God's Sabbaths and revering His holy things. Both named duties are violated in the source.

Ezekiel 23:39
When they slaughtered their children for their idols, they entered My holy places to profane them. Indeed, they acted this way within My house.
  Jeremiah 7:9-11 — close conjunction of grave offenses with entry into God's house: The source describes slaughtering children for idols and then entering God's holy places. Jeremiah likewise lists murder, adultery and foreign worship followed by standing in the house bearing God's name. The shared sequence does not make Jeremiah's murder clause explicitly child sacrifice.

Ezekiel 23:40

  Jeremiah 4:30 — close adornment with ornaments and antimony eye paint in a city oracle: The woman bathes, paints her eyes with antimony and adorns herself for distant visitors. Jeremiah describes scarlet, golden ornaments and antimony eye paint as futile when lovers reject the addressed city. Bathing and messengers are not supplied by the target.

Ezekiel 23:41
and sat upon a spread couch with an adorned table before it. My incense and My oil—they delighted in them.
  Ezekiel 16:18 — close misuse of the oil and incense claimed by God: The source identifies the incense and oil as God's and says they delighted in them beside the adorned table and couch. The earlier oracle likewise calls the oil and incense God's and says the woman set them before her images. The source's delight is not rewritten as the target's precise placing action.

Ezekiel 23:42
They struck up a harmonious sound. With men arriving from the mass of people out of the wilderness, they gave bracelets for their hands and a crown of boasting upon their heads.
  Ezekiel 16:11-12 — close bracelets-and-crown adornment language with differing participants: The source describes bracelets for hands and a crown of boasting for heads amid arriving men and celebration. In the earlier oracle God adorns Jerusalem with bracelets and a crown of boasting. The shared objects and body locations do not resolve the source's pronouns into the target's named giver and recipient.

Ezekiel 23:45
Righteous men will punish them with the punishment of an adulteress and the punishment of bloodshed, for they are adulteresses, and blood is on their hands.
  Leviticus 20:10 — related legal category of punishment for adultery: The city figures are to receive the punishment of an adulteress from righteous men. Leviticus prescribes punishment for adultery with another man's wife. The source applies this legal category within its city allegory rather than narrating that individual legal case.
  Genesis 9:5-6 — related accountability for human bloodshed: The source says the women have blood on their hands and will receive punishment for bloodshed. Genesis declares an accounting for human life and reciprocal bloodshed for one who sheds human blood. It supports this distinct bloodshed charge rather than the source's adultery category.

Ezekiel 23:46

  Ezekiel 16:40 — close assembling of a crowd against the personified city: God commands a multitude against the women with turmoil and plunder. The earlier Jerusalem judgment likewise brings crowds against her, followed by stones and swords. The selected target does not expressly repeat the source's plunder word.

Ezekiel 23:47
and stone them with the multitude’s stones and pierce them with their swords. They will kill their sons and daughters and burn their houses.
  Ezekiel 16:40-41 — close cluster of stones, swords and burned houses in the city judgment: Both descriptions combine the crowd's stones, swords and burning houses. This source additionally says sons and daughters will be killed. That explicit fate is not assigned to the target's children merely because children occur elsewhere in its oracle.

Ezekiel 23:48
I will turn impiety away from the land. All women will be disciplined and certainly will not act according to their impieties.
  Deuteronomy 13:11-12 — related punitive example intended to prevent repetition of wrongdoing: Ezekiel says the women will be disciplined after the removal of impiety and will not repeat the acts. Deuteronomy gives a punishment for enticing others to foreign gods and says Israel will hear, fear and not repeat such evil. The offenses, subjects and stated responses are distinct, while deterrence is explicit in both.

Ezekiel 23:49
Your impiety will be brought upon you, and you will receive the sins of your reflections. You will know that I am the Lord.”
  Ezekiel 16:43 — close return of the city figure's own wrongdoing upon herself: The source says the women's impiety will be brought upon them and they will receive their sins. The earlier oracle likewise says God has brought Jerusalem's ways upon her head. The source adds recognition of the Lord rather than repeating the target's infancy-memory accusation.

Ezekiel 24:1
The Lord’s word came to me in the ninth year, in the tenth month, on the tenth of the month, saying,
  Jeremiah 52:4 — close matching date at the beginning of Jerusalem's siege: Ezekiel dates this word to the ninth year, tenth month and tenth day. Jeremiah records Babylon's arrival against Jerusalem on that same numbered year, month and day. The source's next unit explicitly connects its date with the siege.

Ezekiel 24:2
“Son of man, write for yourself a record of the day, from this day on which Babylon’s king pressed against Jerusalem, from this present day,
  2 Kings 25:1 — related onset of Babylon's pressure against Jerusalem: Ezekiel is told to record the day Babylon's king presses against Jerusalem. Kings records Babylon's king coming with his force and constructing an enclosing wall. Kings gives the ninth year and tenth month here but does not give the tenth day supplied by Ezekiel and Jeremiah.

Ezekiel 24:3
and speak a parable against the provoking house. Say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: Set on the cauldron and pour water into it,
  Ezekiel 11:3-7 — close cauldron-and-meat imagery concerning Jerusalem: The commanded parable begins by setting on a cauldron, developed as the city in the following units. The earlier oracle quotes the city's leaders calling it a cauldron and themselves its meat, then identifies their dead as the meat. The different speakers and interpretations remain explicit.

Ezekiel 24:4
and put the cut pieces into it, every good cut, leg and shoulder stripped from the bones,
  Micah 3:2-3 — related flesh-and-bones cooking imagery for a community's harm: Ezekiel puts selected cuts, stripped from bones, into the cauldron. Micah depicts oppressive authorities taking flesh from bones and dividing the people like meat for a cooking pot. The source's divine judgment sign differs from the target's accusation against exploitative rulers.

Ezekiel 24:5
from choice animals taken. Burn the bones beneath them. It boiled, it boiled, and her bones have been cooked within her.
  Micah 3:3 — related bones and meat within a cooking-pot image: The source burns bones beneath the choice pieces and speaks of bones cooked within. Micah depicts crushed bones and divided flesh as meat for a pot. The target does not say the bones are fuel beneath it, and its actors are the oppressive authorities.

Ezekiel 24:6
Therefore thus says the Lord: O city of bloodshed! A cauldron with rust in it, whose rust has not come out of her! He brought it out limb by limb; no lot fell upon her.
  Jeremiah 6:29-30 — related failed removal of corruption through metalworking imagery: The city is a cauldron whose rust has not come out. Jeremiah describes vain silverworking in which wickedness was not melted away and the people are rejected silver. The rusted vessel and the unsuccessfully worked silver are different images of persistent corruption.

Ezekiel 24:7
For her blood is within her. I have set it upon smooth rock; I have not poured it upon the earth to cover it with earth.
  Job 16:18 — related blood remaining uncovered on the earth: God says He has set the city's blood on smooth rock rather than covering it with earth. Job asks the earth not to cover the blood of his flesh. The speakers, ownership of the blood and smooth-rock detail differ; Job's plea is not made the city's action.

Ezekiel 24:8
To make wrath rise for vengeance to be exacted, I have set her blood upon smooth rock so that it will not be covered.
  Isaiah 26:20-21 — close uncovered blood associated with divine wrath: God leaves the blood uncovered to bring wrath and vengeance. Isaiah likewise joins the Lord's wrath with the earth uncovering blood and not covering the killed. The source expressly assigns placement on the rock to God, while Isaiah describes the earth uncovering it.

Ezekiel 24:10
and multiply the wood and kindle the fire, so that the flesh melts and the broth is reduced,
  Jeremiah 1:13-16 — related heated-cauldron imagery for coming judgment: The parable calls for more wood and fire until flesh melts and broth is reduced. Jeremiah sees a cauldron heated from beneath and is told of northern calamities and judgment. The target's vision does not supply Ezekiel's meat, bones or reduced broth.

Ezekiel 24:11
and it stands upon the coals, so that its copper is scorched and heated and melts amid its uncleanness, and its rust ceases,
  Malachi 3:2-3 — related heated metal and removal of uncleanness: The cauldron's copper is heated and melts amid uncleanness so that its rust ceases. Malachi describes smelting and purifying silver and gold as an image for cleansing the sons of Levi. The metals and groups differ, and the following source unit complicates the anticipated removal by saying rust persists.

Ezekiel 24:12
yet her abundant rust certainly will not leave her; her rust will be put to shame,
  Jeremiah 6:29 — close failure to remove corruption despite the heated process: Ezekiel says the abundant rust will not leave the cauldron. Jeremiah describes failed bellows and lead, with silver worked in vain because wickedness was not melted away. Both preserve the failure rather than claiming successful purification.

Ezekiel 24:13
because you were being defiled. And what if you are not cleansed any more until I fill up My wrath?
  Isaiah 4:4 — related cleansing linked with divine judgment: Ezekiel asks about not being cleansed until God's wrath is filled up. Isaiah promises cleansing of Zion's filth and blood through a spirit of judgment and burning. The question and threatened delay in the source are distinguished from the target's positive promise.

Ezekiel 24:14
I, the Lord, have spoken, and it will come, and I will act. I will not draw back, nor will I show mercy. According to your ways and your reflections I will judge you, says the Lord. Therefore I will judge you according to your bloodshed, and according to your reflections I will judge you, the unclean one, the famous one, abundant in provoking.’”
  Ezekiel 7:5-8 — close judgment according to conduct without sparing: God says He will act, not draw back or show mercy, and judge the city according to its ways and bloodshed. The earlier oracle likewise combines wrath, judgment according to ways and refusal to spare or show mercy. Both assign the consequences to the people's own acts.

Ezekiel 24:16
“Son of man, behold, I am taking the desires of your eyes from you in battle array. You certainly will not beat your breast, nor be wept over.
  Jeremiah 16:5 — related prophetic prohibition of customary mourning during announced judgment: God says He will take the desires of Ezekiel's eyes in battle array and forbids breast-beating or being wept over. Jeremiah is likewise forbidden to join the people's mourning because God removed His peace. The source's battle-array wording and passive being-wept-over wording are preserved.

Ezekiel 24:17
It is a groaning of blood, of loins, of mourning. Your hair will not be plaited upon you, nor will your footwear be on your feet. You certainly will not be comforted by their lips or eat the bread of men.
  Ezekiel 24:23 — same-chapter difference in footwear within mourning directions: This unit says the prophet's footwear will not be on his feet. The later directions to the people say their footwear will be on their feet. The reference records that actual textual difference within the sign and its explanation rather than harmonizing the two instructions.

Ezekiel 24:18
I spoke to the people in the morning as He commanded me, and my wife died in the evening. In the morning I did as I was ordered.
  Ezekiel 24:21-24 — same-chapter explanation of the prophet's bereavement as a sign: The prophet's wife dies and he acts as commanded the next morning. The following explanation announces loss of the people's cherished holy things and children, tells them they will act as he did and calls Ezekiel a portent. The footwear difference within that explanation is not silently removed.

Ezekiel 24:19
The people said to me, “Will you not tell us what these things are that you are doing?”
  Ezekiel 12:9-11 — close audience question about a prophetic sign-act: The people ask what the prophet's actions mean. The earlier deportation sign likewise draws the question What are you doing, followed by an interpretation that what the prophet did will happen to the people. The sign's concrete event differs.

Ezekiel 24:21
‘Say to Israel’s house, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am profaning My holy things, the boast of your strength, the desires of your eyes, those for whom your souls care. Your sons and daughters whom you left behind will fall by the sword.
  Lamentations 2:7 — related divine rejection of His own sanctuary: God announces profaning His holy things, the people's cherished boast. Lamentations says the Lord rejected His altar and shook off His sanctuary. The target supports the divine agency in sanctuary loss without adding the source's children to that selected unit.

Ezekiel 24:22
