  Jeremiah 16:7 — close absence of bread and consolation in mourning: Ezekiel says the people will not be comforted from others' mouth or eat the bread of men. Jeremiah says bread will not be broken in mourning for consolation and no consolation cup will be given. The source does not mention the target's cup.

Ezekiel 24:23
with your hair upon your heads and your footwear on your feet. You certainly will neither beat your breasts nor weep; you will waste away in your injustices and each will comfort his brother.
  Leviticus 26:39 — close wasting away because of wrongdoing: Ezekiel says the people will waste away in their injustices. Leviticus likewise predicts that survivors will be ruined because of their sins and waste away in their enemies' land. That location is specified in the target, not this source unit.

Ezekiel 24:24
Ezekiel will be a portent for you; according to everything he has done, you will do when these things come. You will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Isaiah 20:2-4 — parallel prophets' commanded actions serving as portents: Ezekiel is expressly called a portent whose actions the people will repeat. Isaiah's commanded naked and barefoot walking becomes signs and wonders for the Egyptians and Ethiopians. The prophets' actions and the affected groups differ while the sign-act function is explicit.

Ezekiel 24:25
And you, son of man, will it not be on the day when I take their strength from them, the exaltation of their boasting, the desires of their eyes, the exaltation of their soul, their sons and daughters—
  Ezekiel 24:21 — same-chapter reprise of the cherished things about to be lost: The timing clause repeats strength, boasting, desires of the eyes and sons and daughters from the earlier announcement. That earlier unit names profaned holy things and children falling by the sword; this source enumerates what is to be taken before the survivor's report. The distinctive repeated list supports the connection without equating every item or supplying the earlier unit with the later timing clause.

Ezekiel 24:26
on that day the survivor will come to you to announce it into your ears?
  Ezekiel 33:21 — close predicted and narrated arrival of a survivor from Jerusalem: The source predicts a survivor bringing the announcement. The later record reports the survivor from Jerusalem arriving and saying the city has been captured. The latter names the city and gives the later arrival's date.

Ezekiel 24:27
On that day your mouth will be opened toward the survivor; you will speak and certainly will no longer be struck mute. You will be a portent for them, and they will recognize that I am the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 33:22 — close promise and later account of the prophet's opened mouth: God promises Ezekiel an opened mouth toward the survivor and an end to muteness on that day. The later account describes the Lord's hand in the preceding evening and the opening of the mouth until the survivor arrived in the morning. The connection preserves those temporal formulations rather than collapsing them into one precise instant.

Ezekiel 25:2
“Son of man, set your face firmly against the sons of Ammon and prophesy against them,
  Ezekiel 21:33 — repeated commission to prophesy against Ammon: Ezekiel is directed to face Ammon's sons and prophesy against them. The earlier commission likewise explicitly names Ammon and its reproach, there introducing the drawn sword; the shared national addressee is stated in both passages.

Ezekiel 25:3
and say to Ammon’s sons, ‘Hear the Lord’s word! Thus says the Lord: Because you rejoiced over My holy things when they were profaned, over Israel’s land when it was devastated, and over Judah’s house when they went into captivity,
  Obadiah 1:11-14 — parallel condemnation of rejoicing over Judah's disaster: Ammon rejoices at Israel's devastation and Judah's captivity. Obadiah similarly forbids rejoicing over Judah's destruction or joining the attack on Jerusalem, addressing Idumea rather than Ammon and also forbidding interference with escape routes.
  Psalms 78:1-4 — related profanation of holy places accompanied by neighbors' scorn: Ammon's offense includes rejoicing when God's holy things were profaned. The psalm describes nations defiling God's temple and Jerusalem's ruin, followed by neighbors' sneering and ridicule, supplying the particular sacred-place and hostile-response combination.

Ezekiel 25:4
therefore behold, I am handing you to the sons of Kedem as an inheritance. They will camp with their equipment in you and set their dwellings in you. They will eat your fruits and drink your richness.
  Ezekiel 25:10 — same-chapter repeated grant to Kedem's sons: God gives Ammon to Kedem's sons as an inheritance, with their camps and consumption of its produce. Verse 10 repeats the grant to Kedem's sons against Ammon and adds the end of Ammon's remembrance; it does not repeat the food details.

Ezekiel 25:5
I will make Ammon’s city pastures for camels, and Ammon’s sons a pasture for sheep. You will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Zephaniah 2:6-7 — related conversion of inhabited territory into animal pasture: Ammon's city becomes camel pasture and Ammon's sons a pasture for sheep. Zephaniah likewise describes Crete becoming pasture and sheep enclosure, with grazing in Ashkelon's houses; the territory differs and the target does not mention camels.

Ezekiel 25:6
For thus says the Lord: Because you clapped your hand and made noise with your foot, and rejoiced from your soul over Israel’s land,
  Lamentations 2:15-17 — parallel clapping and hostile rejoicing over Israel's calamity: Ammon claps, makes noise with the foot and rejoices over Israel's land. Lamentations likewise reports passersby clapping at Jerusalem and enemies rejoicing over her downfall, without repeating Ezekiel's foot gesture.

Ezekiel 25:7
therefore I will stretch My hand against you, give you to plunder among the nations, utterly destroy you from the peoples and make you perish from the countries with destruction. You will recognize that I am the Lord.’
  Zephaniah 2:8-10 — parallel plunder of Ammon following hostility to God's people: Ezekiel threatens Ammon with plunder among nations and destruction after its rejoicing over Israel. Zephaniah also names Ammon's hostility and promises plunder and inheritance, there specifically by the remnant of God's people.

Ezekiel 25:8
Thus says the Lord: Because Moab said, ‘Behold, Israel’s and Judah’s house is like all the nations,’
  Deuteronomy 4:6-8 — contrast with the claim that Israel is merely like other nations: Moab dismisses Israel and Judah as like all nations. Deuteronomy instead distinguishes Israel before the nations through its wisdom, God's nearness and its righteous laws, supplying a specific contrast to that dismissal rather than a prediction of Moab's punishment.

Ezekiel 25:10
To Kedem’s sons, against Ammon’s sons, I have given them as an inheritance, so that no remembrance of Ammon’s sons may remain.
  Ezekiel 21:33-37 — repeated Ammon oracle ending with no remembrance: Ezekiel 25 ends the grant against Ammon with the loss of its remembrance. The earlier oracle explicitly names Ammon and likewise ends with no remembrance after judgment in its land, while describing fire and blood rather than the grant to Kedem.

Ezekiel 25:11
I will exact vengeance upon Moab, and they will recognize that I am the Lord.
  Amos 2:1-3 — parallel divine judgment upon Moab with different stated offenses: Ezekiel announces vengeance upon Moab following its dismissive statement about Israel and Judah. Amos likewise announces Moab's judgment, there for burning Idumea's king's bones and with the destruction of rulers; the charges and particular penalties differ.

Ezekiel 25:12
Thus says the Lord: Because Idumea acted in vengeance against Judah’s house, and they bore a grudge and exacted punishment,
  Obadiah 1:10-14 — parallel indictment of Idumea for violence toward Judah: Idumea's vengeance and grudge against Judah provoke judgment. Obadiah likewise condemns Idumea's slaughter and impiety toward brother Jacob, then identifies Jerusalem and Judah's disaster and forbids harm to fugitives.

Ezekiel 25:13
therefore thus says the Lord: I will stretch My hand against Idumea and utterly destroy human and animal from her. I will make her a wilderness, and those pursued from Thaiman will fall by the sword.
  Jeremiah 30:11-14 — parallel desolation of Idumea and its named region: Ezekiel threatens Idumea with wilderness and sword, naming those pursued from Thaiman. Jeremiah likewise announces uninhabitable Idumea and a destructive purpose against Teman's inhabitants, preserving the different name forms and the target's own images.

Ezekiel 25:14
I will place My vengeance upon Idumea by the hand of My people Israel. They will act in Idumea according to My anger and My wrath, and they will recognize My vengeance, says the Lord.
  Obadiah 1:18-21 — parallel judgment of Esau's land through Israel's people: Ezekiel makes God's people Israel the instrument of vengeance upon Idumea. Obadiah depicts Jacob and Joseph consuming Esau like fire and men from Zion executing judgment upon Esau's mountain, connecting the divine judgment with those named human agents.

Ezekiel 25:15
Therefore thus says the Lord: Because the foreigners acted in vengeance and stirred up vengeance, rejoicing from their soul to wipe out for ever,
  Ezekiel 25:16-17 — same-chapter human vengeance answered by divine vengeance: The foreigners' vengeance and rejoicing in destruction form the charge. The following therefore introduces God's action against the coastal inhabitants and His great vengeance, making the repetition a stated retributive answer rather than identifying an unstated victim in this unit.

Ezekiel 25:16
therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will stretch My hand against the foreigners, utterly destroy Cretans and make the remaining inhabitants of the coast perish.
  Zephaniah 2:5-6 — parallel judgment of Cretans and coastal inhabitants: Ezekiel names Cretans and the remaining inhabitants of the coast in the announced destruction. Zephaniah likewise addresses the seacoast's inhabitants and sojourners of Cretans, threatening destruction and then depicting Crete as pasture; the shared names are retained as supplied.

Ezekiel 25:17
I will execute great acts of vengeance upon them. They will recognize that I am the Lord when I bring My vengeance upon them.”
  Ezekiel 25:15-16 — same-chapter divine vengeance answering the foreigners' vengeance: The announced great vengeance concludes the indictment of foreigners who themselves acted in vengeance. Verses 15–16 supply that charge and the therefore introducing God's response, identifying the people addressed by this unit's them.

Ezekiel 26:2
“Son of man, because Tyre said of Jerusalem, ‘Well done! She is shattered; the nations have perished. She has turned to me; the full one has been laid waste,’
  Ezekiel 25:3 — parallel neighboring nations rejoicing at Judah's downfall: Tyre greets Jerusalem's shattering with approval and speaks of the full one's devastation. The preceding Ammon oracle likewise condemns rejoicing over profaned holy things, Israel's devastation and Judah's captivity, with different words for the neighboring nation's response.

Ezekiel 26:3
therefore thus says the Lord: I am against you, Tyre, and I will bring many nations against you, as the sea rises with its waves.
  Isaiah 17:12-13 — shared comparison of many nations with surging sea water: God brings many nations against Tyre as the sea rises with its waves. Isaiah likewise compares many nations with a surging sea and abundant rushing water, without identifying Tyre as the target of its own comparison.

Ezekiel 26:4
They will throw down Tyre’s walls and throw down your towers. I will winnow her dust from her and make her a smooth rock.
  Ezekiel 26:14 — same-chapter repeated reduction of Tyre to smooth rock: The removal of Tyre's walls, towers and dust ends with smooth rock. Verse 14 repeats that smooth-rock outcome and adds a net-drying place and the refusal of rebuilding, developing the city's predicted stripped condition.

Ezekiel 26:5
She will be a drying place for nets in the midst of the sea, for I have spoken, says the Lord. She will become plunder for the nations,
  Ezekiel 47:9-10 — contrasting net-drying places in ruin and restored life: Tyre becomes a drying place for nets and plunder in the sea. Ezekiel 47 also names a drying place for nets, there amid healed waters and abundant living fish; the repeated fishing image has a different surrounding purpose.

Ezekiel 26:6
and her daughters in the plain will be killed by the sword. They will know that I am the Lord.
  Ezekiel 26:8 — same-chapter repeated sword judgment upon Tyre's daughters in the plain: Both units predict the killing of Tyre's daughters in the plain by the sword. Verse 8 places that action within the king's campaign and siege works, making the agency more concrete than the earlier announcement.

Ezekiel 26:7
For thus says the Lord: I am bringing against you, Tyre, Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon from the north—he is king of kings—with horses, chariots, horsemen, and an assembly of very many nations.
  Daniel 2:36-38 — shared king-of-kings title for Babylon's ruler: Ezekiel identifies Nebuchadnezzar as king of Babylon and king of kings. Daniel's interpretation likewise addresses the king as king of kings and attributes his authority to the Lord of heaven, rather than describing the attack on Tyre.
  Ezekiel 29:18 — later report of Nebuchadnezzar's force serving against Tyre: Ezekiel 26 announces Nebuchadnezzar's force coming against Tyre. Ezekiel 29 explicitly reports his force's great servitude against Tyre and its lack of wages, supporting the named campaign without asserting an outcome beyond that report.

Ezekiel 26:8
He will kill your daughters in the plain with the sword, set an advance guard against you, build around you, and make a palisade and a ring of weapons around you; he will set his spears opposite you.
  2 Kings 25:1-2 — related Babylonian siege works against different cities: The campaign against Tyre includes building around it and making a palisade and a ring of weapons. Kings describes Nabouchodonosor's force camping against Jerusalem and building an enclosing wall; the comparison concerns siege operations, preserving the different cities and native name forms.

Ezekiel 26:9
He will throw down your walls and your towers with his swords.
  Ezekiel 26:4 — same-chapter repeated destruction of walls and towers: The king will throw down Tyre's walls and towers with his swords. Verse 4 had announced the fall of those same structures under the coming nations, linking the specific agent and weapon with the preceding prediction.

Ezekiel 26:10
The dust of his multitude of horses will cover you, and your walls will shake at the sound of his horsemen and his chariots’ wheels when he enters your gates like someone entering a city from the plain.
  Jeremiah 8:16 — parallel horse noise accompanied by shaking: Tyre's walls shake at the sound of horsemen and chariot wheels as the force enters. Jeremiah likewise describes the sound of horses and the earth shaking at their neighing, retaining the different sound details and scale of the shaking.

Ezekiel 26:11
With his horses’ hoofs they will trample all your squares. He will kill your people with the sword and bring the support of your strength down to the ground.
  Nahum 3:2-3 — parallel mounted assault and sword slaughter in a city: Ezekiel describes horses' hoofs trampling Tyre's squares and people killed by the sword. Nahum likewise combines a pursuing horse, chariot, mounted warrior and flashing sword with a multitude of slain, in its account concerning Nineveh rather than Tyre.

Ezekiel 26:12
He will plunder your strength, strip your possessions, throw down your walls, and demolish your desirable houses. He will throw your stones, timber, and dust into the sea.
  Ezekiel 26:4 — same-chapter demolition and removal of Tyre's material: The destruction includes walls and houses, with stones, timber and dust thrown into the sea. Verse 4 had promised broken walls and towers and the removal of Tyre's dust; the later detail adds the debris and its destination.

Ezekiel 26:13
He will put an end to the multitude of your musicians, and the sound of your psalteries will certainly no longer be heard.
  Isaiah 24:8 — parallel cessation of music in judgment: Tyre's musicians are brought to an end and its psalteries are no longer heard. Isaiah likewise announces ceased tambourine joy and a ceased lyre sound, preserving the named instruments rather than making every instrument identical.

Ezekiel 26:14
I will make you a smooth rock, a drying place for nets. You will certainly not be rebuilt, for I have spoken, says the Lord.
  Ezekiel 26:4-5 — same-chapter combination of smooth rock and net-drying place: The smooth rock and drying place for nets repeat the two earlier descriptions of stripped Tyre. This unit brings them together and adds that the city will not be rebuilt, an additional statement not supplied by the earlier pair alone.
  Isaiah 23:15-17 — contrast between Tyre not rebuilt and Tyre restored: Ezekiel declares that Tyre will certainly not be rebuilt. Isaiah's Tyre oracle instead describes restoration to its former state after seventy years and renewed trade; the differing prophetic statements are preserved without inventing a harmonizing timetable.

Ezekiel 26:15
For thus says the Lord, the Lord, to Tyre: Will not the islands shake at the sound of your fall, when the wounded groan and a sword is drawn within you?
  Ezekiel 27:35 — related island response to Tyre's fall: Ezekiel 26 asks whether the islands will shake at Tyre's fall amid groaning and the sword. Ezekiel 27 likewise portrays the island inhabitants becoming gloomy and their kings astonished and tearful, describing further reactions to the same city's downfall.

Ezekiel 26:16
All the rulers from the nations of the sea will step down from their thrones, remove the turbans from their heads, and strip off their patterned clothing. They will be utterly astonished, sit on the ground, fear their own destruction, and groan over you.
  Jonah 3:6 — related royal departure from throne and changed dress in crisis: Sea-nation rulers step down from thrones, remove headwear and patterned clothes, and sit on the ground in fear over Tyre. Nineveh's king likewise rises from his throne, removes his robe and sits in ashes wearing sackcloth, there in response to Jonah's warning and as part of repentance.

Ezekiel 26:17
They will raise a lament over you and say to you: ‘How you were destroyed from the sea, praised city that spread her fear to all who live in her!
  Ezekiel 27:2-3 — parallel lament over Tyre's maritime prominence: The sea rulers raise a lament over Tyre as a praised city destroyed from the sea. Ezekiel 27 likewise commands a lament for Tyre and describes its place at the sea's entrance and its trade with many islands, preserving the different lamenting voices.

Ezekiel 26:18
The islands will be afraid from the day of your fall.’
  Isaiah 23:5-6 — related distress among maritime neighbors over Tyre: Ezekiel predicts the islands' fear from the day Tyre falls. Isaiah's Tyre oracle likewise describes pain reaching Egypt and orders island residents to wail, with different emotions and named surrounding places.

Ezekiel 26:19
For thus says the Lord, the Lord: When I make you a desolated city like cities that will not be inhabited, when I bring the abyss up over you and much water covers you,
  Jeremiah 28:42-43 — shared sea covering and uninhabitable city imagery: Tyre becomes uninhabited as the abyss and much water cover it. Jeremiah likewise depicts the sea rising over Babylon and covering her, followed by cities without inhabitants; the different cities and Jeremiah's ensuing waterless-land image remain distinct.

Ezekiel 26:20
I will bring you down to those descending into the pit, to the people of old. I will settle you in the depths of the earth like an everlasting wilderness, with those descending into the pit, so that you are not inhabited and do not rise in the land of life.
  Isaiah 14:9-15 — related descent among the ancient dead: Tyre is brought down among people of old in the earth's depths and those descending to a pit. Isaiah likewise portrays Babylon's king meeting prior earthly rulers in Hades and descending to the earth's foundations; the city and royal figure are different subjects of the descent imagery.

Ezekiel 26:21
I will bring you to ruin, and you will no longer exist for ever, says the Lord, the Lord.”
  Ezekiel 27:36 — repeated permanent-ruin refrain for Tyre: Ezekiel 26 ends by announcing Tyre's ruin and that it will no longer exist for ever. The following Tyre lament ends with the same ruin-and-no-more refrain, there also reporting the traders' hissing.

Ezekiel 27:2
“Son of man, take up a lament over Tyre,
  Ezekiel 26:17 — repeated lament directed toward Tyre: Ezekiel is commanded to take up a lament for Tyre. The preceding oracle depicts sea rulers raising a lament over the same city, identifying both the shared genre and the different voices who deliver it.

Ezekiel 27:3
and say to Tyre, who dwells at the entrance of the sea, the trading place of the peoples from many islands: Thus says the Lord to Tyre: You said, ‘I have clothed myself with my beauty.’
  Ezekiel 28:12-13 — related beauty of Tyre and adornment of its ruler: Tyre says it clothed itself with beauty. The lament over its ruler calls him a crown of beauty and enumerates his precious stones, transferring the beauty motif to a royal addressee without making every detail of city and ruler identical.

Ezekiel 27:4
In the heart of the sea, at Beelim, your sons clothed you with beauty.
  Ezekiel 27:3 — same-chapter repeated clothing with beauty: The account of Tyre's place at Beelim and in the sea says its sons clothed it with beauty. The preceding unit has Tyre itself say that it clothed itself with beauty, retaining the different stated agents of the repeated image.

Ezekiel 27:5
Cedar from Senir was built for you; strips of cypress planks were taken from Lebanon to make fir masts for you.
  1 Kings 5:20-24 — related Tyrian use of Lebanon timber with maritime handling: Tyre's materials include cypress from Lebanon for its masts. Hiram of Tyre supplies cedar and pine timber brought from Lebanon to the sea as rafts; the comparison concerns Tyrian access to Lebanon timber and its maritime use, preserving the different timber descriptions.

Ezekiel 27:6
From Bashan they made your oars; they made your sacred places from ivory, wooded houses from the islands of Kittim.
  Amos 3:13-15 — related ivory structures within judgment upon costly buildings: Ezekiel names Tyre's sacred places of ivory and wooded houses from Kittim's islands. Amos likewise mentions ivory houses that will perish amid judgment on Israel's buildings; the material and vulnerable structures correspond, while their stated sacred or domestic character differs.

Ezekiel 27:7
Fine linen with embroidery from Egypt became your bedding, to clothe you with glory and wrap you in blue and purple from the islands of Elishah; it became your coverings.
  Proverbs 7:16 — shared Egyptian textiles furnishing a bed: Tyre's bedding is embroidered fine linen from Egypt. The woman in Proverbs likewise describes Egyptian textiles spread on her bed, there double-sided rugs; the shared origin and furnishing use do not make the fabrics or narrative settings identical.

Ezekiel 27:8
Your rulers who inhabit Sidon, and the Arvadites, became your rowers; your wise men, Tyre, who were in you, were your pilots.
  Ezekiel 27:28-29 — same-chapter rowers and pilots in service and calamity: The maritime portrait names Tyre's rowers and wise pilots. Later their occupational groups recur as pilots become afraid and rowers leave ships, contrasting the earlier organized service with the lament's response to disaster without identifying every later rower individually.

Ezekiel 27:9
The elders of Byblos and their wise men were in you; they strengthened your counsel. All the ships of the sea and their rowers came to you toward the farthest west.
  Ezekiel 27:27 — same-chapter counselors included in Tyre's loss: Byblos's elders and wise people strengthen Tyre's counsel. The catastrophe later includes counselors among the people and resources falling in the sea; the shared role does not identify every later counselor as a member of the earlier Byblos group.

Ezekiel 27:10
Persians, Lydians, and Libyans were in your force; your warrior men hung small shields and helmets in you; they gave you your glory.
  Ezekiel 30:5 — overlapping named foreign peoples in military judgment lists: Persians, Lydians and Libyans appear in Tyre's force, with shields and helmets contributing to its glory. The Egypt oracle also names these three peoples among those falling by the sword, with additional peoples and without repeating all the equipment.

Ezekiel 27:11
Sons of Arvadites and your force were on your walls; guards were in your towers. They hung their quivers around your harbors; they completed your beauty.
  Ezekiel 26:4 — contrast between Tyre's guarded fortifications and their destruction: Sons of Arvadites are on Tyre's walls and guards in its towers, with quivers around its harbors. The preceding oracle predicts destruction of those walls and towers; the shared structures connect the display of defense with their announced fall.

Ezekiel 27:12
Carthaginians were your traders because of the abundance of all your strength; they gave silver, gold, iron, tin, and lead for your market.
  Isaiah 23:10 — related Carthaginian commerce in Tyre's rise and loss: Carthaginians are named as Tyre's trading partners in metals. Isaiah's Tyre oracle instead announces that ships no longer come from Carthage, connecting the named commercial relationship with its cessation without importing that oracle's statement into Ezekiel's metal list.

Ezekiel 27:13
Greece, all of it, and the adjoining regions traded with you in human lives, and gave bronze vessels for your commerce.
  Joel 4:4-6 — related trade in human lives involving Tyre and Greeks: Ezekiel names Greece and adjoining places in Tyre's trade in human lives and bronze. Joel directly addresses Tyre and Sidon for selling Judah's and Jerusalem's sons to Greeks; the particular human commerce is shared, while Joel specifies victims and the direction of sale.

Ezekiel 27:14
From the house of Togarmah they gave horses and horsemen for your market.
  1 Kings 10:28-29 — parallel international horse trade: Horses and horsemen from the house of Togarmah are given for Tyre's market. Kings likewise describes horses from Egypt and Thekoue, merchants obtaining them from Thekoue, and Egyptian export prices for Hittite and Syrian royal recipients. The particular horse trade corresponds without equating the partners or adding horsemen to the target's goods.

Ezekiel 27:17
Judah and the sons of Israel were your traders in the sale of grain, perfumes, and cassia; they gave choice honey, oil, and resin to your mixed company.
  1 Kings 5:25-26 — related wheat and oil supplied to Tyre from Israel: Judah and Israel trade grain and oil among other goods with Tyre. Kings describes Solomon supplying Hiram with wheat and beaten oil each year within their covenant relationship, providing a concrete counterpart for those two goods rather than all items in Ezekiel's catalog.

Ezekiel 27:21
Arabia and all the rulers of Kedar were your traders through your hand, with camels, rams, and lambs in which they traded with you.
  Isaiah 60:6-7 — related Kedar livestock directed to different destinations: Ezekiel lists Arabia and Kedar in trade involving camels, rams and lambs. Isaiah likewise brings Kedar's sheep, alongside Nabaioth's rams, to the restored addressee and God's altar; the named livestock connection is shared while trade and acceptable offering differ.

Ezekiel 27:22
Traders of Sheba and Raamah were your traders with choice spices and good stones, and they gave gold for your market.
  1 Kings 10:10 — shared Sheba association with spices, precious stones and gold: Sheba and Raamah trade spices, good stones and gold with Tyre. Kings likewise names Sheba's queen giving Solomon spices, precious stones and gold, matching the three goods while distinguishing a royal gift from Tyre's commerce.

Ezekiel 27:25
ships: in them Carthaginians were your traders in abundance among your mixed company. You were filled and became very heavy in the heart of the sea.
  Isaiah 23:14 — related Carthaginian ships in Tyre's commerce and downfall: The continuation mentioning ships names Carthaginian trade and Tyre's abundance in the sea. Isaiah addresses ships of Carthage with a command to wail because their fortress has perished, connecting that shipping world with Tyre's loss rather than repeating the full cargo list.

Ezekiel 27:26
Your rowers led you in much water; the south wind shattered you in the heart of the sea.
  Psalms 47:8 — shared wind shattering ships: A south wind shatters Tyre in the heart of the sea within the ship portrait. The psalm likewise speaks of a violent wind shattering Tarshish's ships; it supplies no compass direction and should not be used to replace Ezekiel's south wind.

Ezekiel 27:27
There were your forces, your payment and that of your mixed companies, your rowers and pilots, your counselors and your mixed companies from your mixed companies, all your warrior men in you, and your whole assembly within you; they will fall in the heart of the sea on the day of your fall.
  Ezekiel 27:34 — same-chapter repeated loss of assembly and crew in the sea: Tyre's forces, payments, crews, counselors and assembly fall in the sea on the day of its fall. Verse 34 repeats the shattering in the sea's depth and the fall of the mixed company, assembly and rowers, concentrating the same collective loss.

Ezekiel 27:28
At the sound of your cry, your pilots will be greatly afraid,
  Ezekiel 26:15 — related sound and fear at Tyre's downfall: The sound of crying frightens the pilots in this lament. The preceding Tyre oracle likewise links the sound of the city's fall and wounded people's groans with the islands' shaking, preserving the different frightened groups and sounds.

Ezekiel 27:29
and all your rowers will come down from the ships; the passengers and the lookouts of the sea will stand on the land.
  Ezekiel 27:8 — same-chapter rowers in Tyre's maritime portrait and lament: All the rowers leave the ships as passengers and lookouts stand on land. Verse 8 had named rulers inhabiting Sidon and the Arvadites as rowers serving Tyre; the repeated occupation links the maritime service with the later response without asserting that every person in the two groups is identical.

Ezekiel 27:30
They will raise a cry over you with their voice and cry bitterly; they will put earth on their head and spread ashes beneath them.
  Joshua 7:5-9 — shared dust-on-head gesture after communal calamity: Those mourning Tyre put earth on their heads and spread ashes beneath them. Joshua and Israel's elders likewise put dust on their heads after the loss at Ai, matching the head gesture while preserving the different disasters and Ezekiel's additional ashes.

Ezekiel 27:32
Their sons will take up a lament over you and a lamentation for you:
  Ezekiel 26:17 — repeated lament over Tyre's destruction: The sons raise a lament for Tyre, whose destruction in the sea is described in the following units. The preceding oracle likewise gives a lament over the praised maritime city, there raised by sea rulers; the subject and loss recur with different lamenting groups.

Ezekiel 27:33
‘What a payment you found from the sea! You filled nations from your abundance, and from your mixed company you enriched all the kings of the earth.
  Isaiah 23:8-9 — related Tyrian commerce and royal prominence: Tyre finds payment from the sea, fills nations from its abundance and enriches kings from its mixed company. Isaiah likewise portrays Tyre's merchants as glorious rulers of the earth before announcing their humiliation; it emphasizes status rather than repeating Ezekiel's specific statement that Tyre enriched kings.

Ezekiel 27:34
Now you were shattered in the sea, in the depth of water; your mixed company and all your assembly fell within you—all your rowers.
  Ezekiel 27:27 — same-chapter repeated sinking of Tyre's company and rowers: The city's shattering in deep water brings its mixed company, assembly and rowers down. Verse 27 had already gathered the force, payments and crews into a collective fall in the sea, making the later line a specific repetition of their shared loss.

Ezekiel 27:35
All who inhabit the islands became gloomy over you; their kings were utterly astonished, and their faces shed tears.
  Ezekiel 26:18 — repeated island response to Tyre's fall: Island inhabitants become gloomy, and their kings are astonished with tears on their faces. The preceding oracle had announced the islands' fear from Tyre's fall; the shared island audience is retained while the later lament adds particular reactions.

Ezekiel 27:36
Traders from nations hissed at you; you came to ruin, and you will be no more for ever.’”
  Ezekiel 26:21 — repeated permanent-ruin refrain for Tyre: The lament ends with traders hissing and Tyre becoming ruin and no longer existing for ever. Ezekiel 26 had ended with the same ruin-and-no-more declaration, supplying the particular repeated conclusion rather than merely a general judgment theme.

Ezekiel 28:2
“And you, son of man, say to the ruler of Tyre: Thus says the Lord: Because your heart was exalted and you said, ‘I am a god; I have inhabited a god’s dwelling in the heart of the sea’—yet you are a human, and not a god, and you made your heart like a god’s heart—
  2 Thessalonians 2:3-4 — related divine claims and occupation of a purported divine place: Tyre's ruler claims to be a god and to occupy a god's dwelling in the sea, although he is human. The man of lawlessness likewise takes a seat in God's sanctuary displaying himself as God; the comparison concerns the divine claims and occupation of a divine place, without identifying the figures or locations as the same.

Ezekiel 28:3
Surely you are not wiser than Daniel? Did wise men not instruct you in their knowledge?
  Daniel 2:27-30 — Daniel's divine disclosure beside the challenge to Tyre's wisdom: The ruler is challenged with the question whether he is wiser than Daniel. Daniel describes God as the revealer of mysteries and expressly denies that the disclosure came through wisdom exceeding all people, illuminating the named comparison without adding a secrets-hidden clause to Ezekiel.

Ezekiel 28:4
Surely it was not by your knowledge or understanding that you built up strength for yourself, and gold and silver in your treasuries?
  Deuteronomy 8:17-18 — related attribution of strength and wealth: Ezekiel asks whether the ruler's knowledge and understanding produced his strength, gold and silver. Deuteronomy warns against claiming that one's own strength gained wealth and identifies God as the giver of that ability, preserving Ezekiel's question and Deuteronomy's explicit instruction.

Ezekiel 28:5
By your abundant knowledge and your trade you increased your strength; your heart was exalted in your strength.
  Deuteronomy 8:12-14 — parallel abundance associated with a lifted-up heart: The ruler's great knowledge and commerce increase his strength, and his heart is lifted up in that strength. Deuteronomy similarly warns that multiplied possessions, silver and gold may accompany a lifted-up heart and forgetting God; the target gives a warning rather than Tyre's reported condition.

Ezekiel 28:6
Therefore thus says the Lord: Since you made your heart like a god’s heart,
  Ezekiel 28:2 — same-chapter repeated charge of a godlike heart: The announced judgment is grounded in the ruler's setting his heart like a god's heart. Verse 2 had already joined that charge to his divine claim and the correction that he is human, supplying the stated offense behind the response.

Ezekiel 28:7
therefore, behold, I am bringing foreigners against you, scourges from nations. They will draw their swords against you and against the beauty of your knowledge, and lay your beauty out for destruction,
  Ezekiel 28:17 — same-chapter beauty and knowledge corrupted and judged: Foreigners strike the beauty of the ruler's knowledge and destroy his beauty. Verse 17 likewise joins beauty and knowledge, there explaining pride and corruption before the ruler is cast down as a spectacle, preserving the different means and explanation.

  Psalms 81:6-7 — related god-language confronted with human death: The ruler's claim to be a god is challenged before those killing him, since he is human.   Ezekiel 28:13 — related divine-paradise imagery for foreign royal splendor: The cypress's beauty is unmatched by the trees of God's paradise.
