Ezekiel 31:9
because of the multitude of its branches, and the trees of God’s paradise of delight envied it.
  Ezekiel 31:8 — same-chapter unrivaled beauty followed by the other trees' envy: The trees of God's paradise of delight envy the great tree with its many branches. Verse 8 had explained that no paradise tree resembled it in beauty, supplying the stated comparative distinction behind the ensuing envy.

Ezekiel 31:10
Therefore thus says the Lord: Because you became great in size and set your crown amid clouds, and I saw it being lifted high,
  Daniel 4:22 — related towering royal-tree height with different explanations: Ezekiel cites the tree's great size, crown amid clouds and God's seeing it lifted high. Daniel likewise interprets heavenly height and cloud-reaching mass as royal elevation, explicitly adding pride of heart against the Holy One; that heart charge is not stated in this Ezekiel unit.

Ezekiel 31:11
I handed it into the hands of a ruler of nations, and he brought about its destruction.
  Daniel 4:14-17 — parallel divine judgment removing an imperial tree: God hands the tree to a ruler of nations who destroys it. Daniel likewise has an angel command the imperial tree's removal at the Most High's order, there preserving a root and describing seven years; the means and the added preservation detail differ.

Ezekiel 31:12
Foreigners, scourges from nations, utterly destroyed it and cast it upon the mountains. Its branches fell in all the ravines; its trunks were shattered in every plain of the land. All the nations’ peoples went down from their shelter and dashed it to the ground.
  Daniel 4:17a — parallel destruction and scattering of a great tree's branches: Foreigners destroy the tree, its branches fall through ravines and its trunks are shattered on the plains. Daniel's dream likewise has the tree cut down, its branches given to every wind and the tree dragged and thrown down, with different agents and destinations.

Ezekiel 31:13
All the sky’s birds rested upon its fallen trunk, and all the field’s beasts were upon its trunks,
  Ezekiel 31:6 — same-chapter birds and beasts before and after the tree's fall: Birds now rest on the fallen trunk and beasts are upon its trunks. Verse 6 had placed nesting birds in its offshoots and beasts giving birth below its branches, making the repeated animal groups and changed condition of the tree explicit.

Ezekiel 31:14
so that none of the trees in the water may rise high in their size. They did not set their crowns amid clouds, and none of those drinking water stood toward them in their height. All were given to death, to the depth of the earth, among human beings, with those descending to a pit.
  Isaiah 14:13-15 — related high crowns and clouds reversed by descent below earth: The watered trees are prevented from rising high and setting crowns among clouds, and are given to death and the earth's depth. Isaiah similarly contrasts the Babylonian ruler's ambition above clouds with descent to Hades and the earth's foundations, using a royal speech rather than the same tree picture.

Ezekiel 31:15
Thus says the Lord, the Lord: On the day it descended to Hades, the abyss mourned it. I stopped its rivers and restrained the multitude of water. Lebanon grew dark over it, and all the plain’s trees collapsed over it.
  Isaiah 14:8 — contrasting woodland scenes around a royal fall: Lebanon grows dark and all the plain’s trees collapse over the great tree's descent. Isaiah instead has Lebanon's trees and cedar rejoice because the one cutting them has not come up since the ruler fell asleep, giving opposed woodland responses within royal downfall imagery.

Ezekiel 31:16
At the sound of its fall the nations shook when I brought it down to Hades with those descending to a pit. In the earth all the trees of delight and the choice trees of Lebanon, all drinking water, comforted it.
  Isaiah 14:9-10 — related reception of a fallen ruler among the underworld's inhabitants: The fallen tree is brought to Hades, and trees of delight and Lebanon comfort it in the earth. Isaiah likewise has Hades's giants rise and address the fallen Babylonian king, saying he has been captured and numbered with them; the responses are distinct rather than both explicitly called comfort.

Ezekiel 31:17
For they too went down with it to Hades among those wounded by the sword; its offspring, those living under its shelter, perished in the midst of their life.
  Ezekiel 31:16 — same-chapter others accompanying the tree's descent to Hades: Others also go down with the tree to Hades among those wounded by the sword, and its sheltered offspring perish. Verse 16 had announced the tree's descent to Hades and the pit, supplying the movement that this unit extends to others.

Ezekiel 31:18
Whom were you likened to? Descend and be brought down with the trees of delight into the earth’s depth. Among the uncircumcised you will lie with those wounded by the sword. So it is with Pharaoh and the multitude of his strength, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 31:2 — same-chapter royal addressee and comparison restated at the conclusion: The conclusion names Pharaoh and his strength after the command to descend with the trees of delight. The opening had addressed Pharaoh and his multitude with a question about likeness in height, connecting the repeated comparison to the same stated addressee.

Ezekiel 32:2
“Son of man, take up a lament over Pharaoh, Egypt’s king, and say to him, ‘You were likened to a lion of nations, and you are like the dragon in the sea. You butted in your rivers, troubled water with your feet and trampled your rivers.
  Ezekiel 29:3 — repeated Pharaoh portrayed as a dragon among waters: The lament likens Pharaoh to a lion and a dragon in the sea, troubling his rivers with his feet. The earlier Egypt oracle likewise calls Pharaoh a great dragon seated among rivers; it does not repeat the lion comparison or the trampling detail.

Ezekiel 32:3
Thus says the Lord: I will throw nets of many peoples over you and haul you up with My hook,
  Ezekiel 29:4 — repeated capture and removal of Pharaoh's dragon figure: God throws peoples' nets over the dragon and hauls it up with His hook. The earlier oracle likewise removes the dragon from its river, there with traps in its jaws and fish on its fins; nets and hook are not silently substituted into that earlier wording.

Ezekiel 32:4
and stretch you out upon the earth; plains will be filled with you. I will seat all the sky’s birds upon you and fill all the beasts of the whole earth with you.
  Ezekiel 29:5 — repeated Pharaoh's body given to birds and beasts on the plain: The dragon is stretched on the earth, filling plains, with birds seated on it and beasts filled with it. Ezekiel 29 similarly casts it on the plain and gives it as food to beasts and birds, making the animal-feeding consequence explicit.

Ezekiel 32:5
I will lay your flesh upon the mountains and fill them with your blood,
  Isaiah 34:3 — shared discarded bodies and mountains filled with blood: God lays the ruler's flesh upon mountains and fills them with his blood. Isaiah likewise describes discarded wounded and dead people and mountains soaked with their blood, using plural victims in its judgment on nations rather than the single royal dragon image.

Ezekiel 32:6
and the earth will be watered from your discharges, from your multitude upon the mountains. I will fill ravines with you.
  Ezekiel 32:5 — same-chapter the ruler's body pictured filling the terrain: The earth is watered from the addressee's discharges and the ravines are filled with him. Verse 5 had laid his flesh on mountains and filled them with blood; the adjacent territorial body images correspond without replacing this unit's discharges with a repeated blood clause.

Ezekiel 32:7
When you are extinguished, I will cover heaven and darken its stars. I will cover the sun with a cloud, and the moon certainly will not show its light.
  Isaiah 13:10 — parallel darkening of stars, sun and moon in national judgment: God promises to darken heaven's stars, cover the sun with cloud and withhold moonlight at the ruler's extinguishing. Isaiah's Babylon oracle likewise describes absent stellar light, the rising sun darkened and no moonlight, with a different national setting and no identical cloud detail.

Ezekiel 32:8
All the things shining light in heaven will grow dark over you, and I will put darkness upon your land, says the Lord, the Lord.
  Exodus 10:21-23 — repeated divine darkness upon Egypt's land: God places darkness upon the addressee's land as heaven's lights darken. Exodus likewise brings darkness over Egypt at God's command, there for three days while Israel has light; the shared country and divine darkness do not make the episodes or stated durations identical.

Ezekiel 32:9
I will provoke the heart of many peoples when I bring your captivity among the nations, into a land you have not known.
  Deuteronomy 28:36 — related deportation into an unknown land: Ezekiel announces captivity among nations into a land the addressee has not known. Deuteronomy likewise threatens removal of the people and their rulers to an unknown nation, with a further clause about serving other gods absent from Ezekiel's unit.

Ezekiel 32:10
Many nations will grow gloomy over you; their kings will be utterly astounded when My sword flies before their faces, awaiting their own fall from the day of your fall.
  Ezekiel 26:16 — related foreign rulers fearing their own downfall: Kings are astonished as God's sword flies before them and await their own fall from Egypt's fall. The Tyre oracle likewise depicts sea rulers fearing their own destruction at another power's disaster, adding changed clothing and sitting on the ground rather than the flying sword.

Ezekiel 32:11
For thus says the Lord: Babylon’s king’s sword will come upon you
  Jeremiah 50:10-11 — parallel Babylonian royal attack upon Egypt: The Egypt lament announces the sword of Babylon's king. Jeremiah likewise names Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon entering and striking Egypt and assigning people to the sword among other fates, adding the king's personal name to the shared imperial identification.

Ezekiel 32:12
with giants’ swords, and I will strike down your strength. All are scourges from nations; they will destroy Egypt’s arrogance, and all her strength will be shattered.
  Ezekiel 30:10-11 — repeated nations' scourges destroying Egypt by swords: Giants' swords accompany the attack, God strikes its strength, and nations' scourges destroy Egypt's arrogance. Ezekiel 30 likewise names Nebuchadnezzar's people as scourges from nations who draw swords against Egypt, without repeating the giants designation in those units.

Ezekiel 32:13
I will destroy all her livestock beside the abundant water. No human foot will ever trouble it again, nor will livestock’s tread trample it.
  Ezekiel 34:18-19 — related water disturbed by feet, with different purposes: Egypt's livestock are destroyed and neither human nor livestock feet will trouble its water again. The flock oracle likewise describes water troubled by feet, there condemning sheep that spoil what other sheep drink; it does not make Egypt's depopulation a promise of pastoral prosperity.

Ezekiel 32:14
Thus their waters will then become still, and their rivers will flow like oil, says the Lord.
  Ezekiel 32:13 — same-chapter still water following the end of trampling: The waters become still and rivers flow like oil. The preceding unit had removed livestock and ended human and animal disturbance by feet, giving the explicit sequence for the stillness without adding a restoration or healing promise to the Egypt lament.

Ezekiel 32:15
When I give Egypt to destruction and the land is made desolate with its fullness, when I scatter all who dwell in her, they will know that I am the Lord.
  Ezekiel 29:12 — repeated Egyptian desolation and scattering of inhabitants: God makes Egypt desolate and scatters its inhabitants. Ezekiel 29 likewise combines desolation of the land and cities with scattering Egyptians among nations, there also specifying forty years; this unit emphasizes recognition of God.

Ezekiel 32:16
It is a lament, and you will lament it, and the nations’ daughters will lament it. Over Egypt and all her strength they will lament her, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Ezekiel 32:2 — same-chapter framing of the Egyptian royal lament: The closing declaration identifies the lament over Egypt and its strength and includes the nations' daughters as lamenters. Verse 2 had commanded a lament over Pharaoh king of Egypt, linking the genre's opening and closing with additional lamenters named at the close.

Ezekiel 32:18
“Son of man, lament over Egypt’s strength. The nations will bring her daughters down dead into the earth’s depth, with those descending to a pit.
  Ezekiel 26:20 — related national descent among the dead into a pit: The nations bring Egypt's daughters down dead into the earth's depth with those descending to a pit. The Tyre oracle likewise brings the city down among ancient people and pit-descenders, preserving the different subjects and stated agents of the descent.

Ezekiel 32:20
They will fall with him among those wounded by the sword, and all his strength will lie down.
  Ezekiel 31:17 — related companions falling with a defeated figure among sword-wounded people: Others fall with him among those wounded by the sword and all his strength lies down. The tree oracle similarly describes others descending with it to Hades among the sword-wounded, preserving the supplied pronouns rather than inventing an additional named companion.

Ezekiel 32:21
The giants will say to you, ‘Be in the depth of a pit! Whom do you surpass? Descend and lie with the uncircumcised among those wounded by the sword.’
  Isaiah 14:9-10 — shared underworld giants addressing the fallen addressee: Giants speak to the addressee in the pit and command descent among the sword-wounded. Isaiah likewise depicts underworld giants addressing the fallen Babylonian king and naming his shared captivity, with different words in the dead speakers' address.

Ezekiel 32:22
There Assyria and all his assembly, all wounded, were placed. Their burial is in the depth of a pit, and his assembly was around his tomb, all the wounded who fell by the sword,
  Ezekiel 31:3-6 — contrast between Assyria's imperial tree and its grave assembly: Assyria and its assembly are placed wounded in the pit and around a tomb. Ezekiel 31 had pictured Assyria as a high cypress with nations in its shade, providing a named-power contrast between impressive shelter and the grave assembly.

Ezekiel 32:23
who spread their fear upon the land of life.
  Ezekiel 32:24 — same-chapter repeated fear upon the land of life within the grave catalog: The continuation after Assyria's burial describes spreading fear upon the land of life. The Elam entry repeats that same fear description while placing its force among the dead below earth, contrasting their living terror with their grave setting.

Ezekiel 32:24
There is Elam and all his force around his tomb, all the wounded who fell by the sword and those descending uncircumcised into the earth’s depth, who spread their fear upon the land of life and received their torment with those descending to a pit,
  Jeremiah 25:15-17 — related sword judgment upon Elam's military strength: Elam and its force lie sword-wounded around the tomb. Jeremiah likewise announces Elam's shattered bow, dispersal and a pursuing sword until they are consumed, sharing the named military power's judgment while using different grave and exile imagery.

Ezekiel 32:26
There were placed Mosoch and Thobel and all their strength around his tomb, all his wounded, all uncircumcised, wounded by the sword, who spread their fear upon the land of life.
  Ezekiel 38:2-4 — related Mosoch and Thobel in military portraits: Mosoch and Thobel and their strength appear sword-wounded around the tomb. The Gog oracle also names Mosoch and Thobel within an armed political configuration, with horses and weapons; the shared names and military setting do not establish that the two scenes are the same event.

Ezekiel 32:28
You too will lie among the uncircumcised with those wounded by the sword.
  Ezekiel 28:10 — parallel royal death associated with the uncircumcised: The addressee will lie among the uncircumcised with those wounded by the sword. The Tyre ruler's oracle likewise associates uncircumcised people with perishing by foreign hands, preserving the different royal settings and specific death descriptions.

Ezekiel 32:29
There were placed Assyria’s rulers, who gave his strength to a sword wound. These lay with the wounded, with those descending to a pit.
  Ezekiel 32:22 — same-chapter repeated Assyrian burial group: Assyria's rulers are placed with the wounded and those descending to a pit. Verse 22 had named Assyria and its entire assembly around the tomb, linking the rulers with the same named country's grave description without changing them into Edom's rulers.

Ezekiel 32:30
There are the north’s rulers, all Assyria’s generals, who descended wounded. With their fear and their strength they lay uncircumcised with those wounded by the sword and carried away their torment with those descending to a pit.
  Ezekiel 32:22-23 — same-chapter Assyrian military figures among the wounded dead: The north's rulers and all Assyria's generals descend wounded and lie uncircumcised with the sword-wounded. The earlier Assyrian assembly likewise lies wounded around the tomb and is associated with fear in the land of life; the additional northern rulers are not thereby identified as the same group.

Ezekiel 32:31
King Pharaoh will see them and be comforted over all their strength, says the Lord, the Lord.
  Ezekiel 31:16 — related comfort amid the underworld defeat of great powers: Pharaoh sees them and is comforted over all their strength. The earlier tree lament likewise has the trees of delight and Lebanon comfort the fallen tree in the earth after its descent to Hades, preserving the different agents and the source's their strength rather than substituting Pharaoh's own force.

Ezekiel 32:32
For I have set his fear upon the land of life, and he will lie among the uncircumcised with those wounded by the sword—Pharaoh and all his multitude—says the Lord, the Lord.”
  Ezekiel 32:28 — same-chapter repeated final place among the uncircumcised and sword-wounded: The closing unit expressly names Pharaoh and his multitude lying among the uncircumcised and those wounded by the sword. Verse 28 had announced that same placement to its addressee, providing the particular burial description repeated at the conclusion.

Ezekiel 33:2
“Son of man, speak to your people’s sons and say to them, ‘If I bring a sword upon a land, and the land’s people take one man from among themselves and appoint him as their watchman,
  Ezekiel 3:17 — watchman image and its prophetic application: The land's people appoint a watchman in the source's illustration. In the earlier commission God appoints Ezekiel as Israel's watchman to hear and convey His word; the appointing authority differs.

Ezekiel 33:3
and he sees the sword coming upon the land, sounds the trumpet and signals to the people,
  Jeremiah 6:17-19 — parallel trumpet warning of approaching judgment: The watchman sounds a trumpet when the sword approaches. Jeremiah's appointed watchers also call for hearing the trumpet, and the people's refusal is followed by threatened evils.

Ezekiel 33:4
and a hearer hears the trumpet’s sound but does not take care, and the sword comes upon him and overtakes him, his blood will be upon his own head.
  Jeremiah 6:17-19 — parallel accountability for refusing a trumpet warning: The hearer who neglects the trumpet is overtaken by the sword and bears responsibility for his blood. Jeremiah likewise connects refusal to hear the watchers and God's words with ensuing judgment.

Ezekiel 33:5
Because he heard the trumpet’s sound and did not take care, his blood will be upon himself. But this one, because he took care, rescued his life.
  Ezekiel 3:21 — related preservation of life through heeding warning: The source contrasts the negligent hearer's responsibility with one who takes care and rescues his life. The earlier commission likewise says a warned righteous person who does not sin will live.

Ezekiel 33:6
If the watchman sees the sword coming but does not signal with the trumpet, and the people do not take care, and the sword comes and takes a life from them, that life was taken because of its own lawlessness, but I will require the blood from the watchman’s hand.’
  Ezekiel 3:18 — watchman's blood responsibility applied to prophetic warning: A watchman who fails to signal must answer for the victim's blood even though the victim dies in his own lawlessness. The earlier commission assigns Ezekiel the same responsibility when he withholds the warning.
  Acts 20:26-27 — contrast between withheld warning and discharge of responsibility: The negligent watchman must answer for another's blood. Paul declares himself clean from everyone's blood because he did not hold back God's whole purpose, presenting the corresponding positive claim about faithfully delivering a message.

Ezekiel 33:7
And you, son of man, I have appointed you a watchman for Israel’s house, and you will hear a word from My mouth.
  Ezekiel 3:17 — repeated appointment as Israel's watchman: Both passages appoint Ezekiel as watchman for Israel's house and require him to hear God's word. The earlier passage explicitly adds conveying God's warning.

Ezekiel 33:8
When I say to the sinner, ‘You will surely be put to death,’ and you do not speak so that the impious man may guard himself from his way, that lawless man will die in his lawlessness, but I will require his blood from your hand.
  Ezekiel 3:18 — repeated warning and blood accountability: Both passages announce death to the lawless person and require his blood from the prophet's hand if the prophet fails to warn him to turn from his way.

Ezekiel 33:9
But if you forewarn the impious man of his way so that he may turn from it and he does not turn from his way, this man will die in his impiety, and you have rescued your own life.
  Ezekiel 3:19 — repeated distinction between rejected warning and the prophet's responsibility: Both passages say that a warned person who refuses to turn dies in his own wrongdoing, while the prophet who delivered the warning rescues his own life.

Ezekiel 33:10
And you, son of man, say to Israel’s house, ‘You spoke this way: Our wanderings and lawless deeds are upon us, and we waste away in them. How shall we live?’
  Leviticus 26:39-40 — covenant warning and confession of wasting away in sin: Israel says its lawless deeds lie upon it and asks how it can live while wasting away. Leviticus predicts that survivors waste away because of their sins and then confess their own and their fathers' sins.

Ezekiel 33:11
Say to them, ‘I live, thus says the Lord: I do not desire the impious man’s death, but rather that he turn from his way and live. Turn back completely from your way! Why are you dying, house of Israel?’
  Ezekiel 18:23 — repeated preference for repentance and life: Both passages deny that God desires the lawless person's death and instead call for turning from the evil way and living. The source directly addresses Israel with an urgent call to turn.

Ezekiel 33:12
Say to your people’s sons, ‘A righteous man’s righteousness certainly will not rescue him on the day he wanders, and an impious man’s lawlessness certainly will not harm him on the day he turns from it. A righteous man certainly will not be able to be saved.
  Ezekiel 18:21-24 — parallel reversal of standing through present conduct: Both passages contrast turning from lawlessness with turning away from righteousness. The source says former lawlessness will not harm the person who turns, while Ezekiel 18 explicitly promises life; former righteousness does not protect the person who now acts lawlessly.

Ezekiel 33:13
When I speak to the righteous man—this one has trusted in his righteousness, yet commits lawlessness—none of his righteous deeds will ever be remembered. In the injustice he committed, in it he will die.
  Ezekiel 18:24 — repeated warning that former righteous deeds do not excuse new wrongdoing: The source describes a person trusting in prior righteousness while acting lawlessly. Both passages say that the former righteous acts will not be remembered and that the person dies in the new wrongdoing.

Ezekiel 33:14
When I say to the impious man, “You will surely be put to death,” and he turns from his sin and practices judgment and righteousness,
  Ezekiel 18:21 — parallel turning from lawlessness toward righteous conduct: Both passages describe a lawless person turning from wrongdoing and practicing righteousness. The source's sentence continues into the following unit's concrete acts and assurance of life.

Ezekiel 33:15
and returns a pledge, repays what he seized and walks in the ordinances of life without doing wrong, he will surely live and certainly will not die.
  Deuteronomy 24:12-13 — particular legal counterpart for returning a pledge: The source includes returning a pledge among the acts that accompany a changed life. Deuteronomy specifically requires returning a poor debtor's garment at sunset and calls that act mercy before God.

Ezekiel 33:16
None of the sins he committed will ever be remembered, because he practiced judgment and righteousness; in these he will live.
  Ezekiel 18:22 — repeated non-remembrance of former sins after repentance: Both passages say that the former transgressions of the person who now practices righteousness will not be remembered, and connect that righteous conduct with life.

Ezekiel 33:17
Your people’s sons will say, “The Lord’s way is not straight.” Yet this way of theirs is not straight.
  Ezekiel 18:25 — repeated dispute over whose way is crooked: Israel's complaint that the Lord's way is not straight is answered by identifying the people's own way as the crooked one. The earlier oracle presents the same accusation and response.

Ezekiel 33:18
When a righteous man turns from his righteousness and commits lawless deeds, he will die in them.
  Ezekiel 18:26 — repeated consequence of turning from righteousness: Both passages say that a righteous person who turns from righteousness to transgression dies in the wrongdoing he committed.

Ezekiel 33:19
When the sinner turns from his lawlessness and practices judgment and righteousness, in these he will live.
  Ezekiel 18:27-28 — repeated preservation of life through turning from lawlessness: Both passages describe the lawless person turning to judgment and righteousness and living. The earlier passage explicitly states that this person has preserved his life.

Ezekiel 33:20
This is what you said: “The Lord’s way is not straight.” I will judge you, each in his ways, house of Israel.’”
  Ezekiel 18:29-30 — repeated complaint followed by individual judgment: Both passages repeat Israel's objection to God's way and announce judgment on each person according to that person's way. The earlier passage additionally calls for turning from impiety.

Ezekiel 33:21
In the twelfth year of our captivity, in the twelfth month, on the fifth of the month, the survivor from Jerusalem came to me, saying, “The city has been captured!”
  Ezekiel 24:25-27 — narrative arrival corresponding to the promised survivor's report: The source records a survivor from Jerusalem announcing that the city has been captured. The earlier oracle anticipates a survivor coming to announce the loss and the prophet's mouth opening toward him.

Ezekiel 33:22
The Lord’s hand came upon me in the evening before he arrived, and He opened my mouth until he came to me in the morning. Once my mouth was opened, it was no longer restrained.
  Ezekiel 24:27 — narrative counterpart to the promised opening of the prophet's mouth: The earlier oracle promises that Ezekiel will speak to the survivor and no longer be mute. The source records the opening of his mouth around the survivor's arrival and the ending of its restraint.

Ezekiel 33:24
“Son of man, those dwelling in the devastated places upon Israel’s land say, ‘Abraham was one, yet he possessed the land. We are more; the land has been given to us as a possession.’
  Isaiah 51:2 — different appeals to the single ancestor Abraham: The inhabitants argue from Abraham being one while they are many to claim the land. Isaiah also recalls Abraham as one whom God called and multiplied, but locates the increase in God's blessing.
  Matthew 3:8-10 — contrast to security claimed through Abraham: The inhabitants appeal to Abraham and their greater numbers as grounds for possession. John's warning refuses ancestry in Abraham as a substitute for repentance; Ezekiel's surrounding oracle likewise announces judgment for their abominations.

Ezekiel 33:27
I live: surely those in the devastated places will fall by the sword. Those in the open plain will be given as food to the field’s beasts, and those in fortified places and caves I will kill with death.
  Leviticus 26:22-25 — particular covenant judgments of beasts, sword and death: The source assigns sword, wild beasts and death to people in different locations. Leviticus likewise threatens consuming beasts, a covenant-avenging sword and death within cities; its sequence and locations differ.

Ezekiel 33:28
I will make the land a wilderness; the arrogance of her strength will perish, and Israel’s mountains will be desolate because no one passes through.
  Leviticus 26:31-33 — covenant desolation reflected in the land's announced ruin: Both passages announce desolate land. Ezekiel particularly describes empty mountains and the collapse of arrogant strength, while Leviticus includes scattering among nations.

Ezekiel 33:29
They will know that I am the Lord. I will make their land a wilderness, and it will be desolate because of all the abominations they committed.’
  Leviticus 26:30-33 — related judgment connecting polluted worship with a desolate land: The source attributes the land's devastation to the people's abominations. Leviticus places ruined images, desolate holy places, cities and land within the consequences of covenant disobedience.

Ezekiel 33:31
They come to you as people assemble and sit before you. They hear your words but certainly will not do them, because falsehood is in their mouth, and their heart follows defilements.
  James 1:22-25 — explicit contrast between hearing the word and doing it: The assembled people hear Ezekiel's words but do not do them. James warns against being hearers only and distinguishes the forgetful hearer from the person who acts.
  Matthew 7:26-27 — parallel warning about hearing without obedience: Ezekiel's listeners hear his words without doing them. Jesus likens hearing His words without practicing them to a house founded on sand that falls under the storm.

Ezekiel 33:32
You become to them like the sound of a sweet-voiced, well-tuned psaltery. They will hear your words but certainly will not do them.
  James 1:22-25 — related exposure of hearing without action: Ezekiel's listeners enjoy his words like a finely tuned instrument but do not act. James contrasts mere hearing with doing; he does not supply Ezekiel's musical comparison.

Ezekiel 33:33
When it comes, they will say, ‘Behold, it has arrived,’ and know that a prophet was among them.”
  Deuteronomy 18:21-22 — recognition of prophetic speech through its outcome: The coming event will make the people recognize that a prophet was among them. Deuteronomy likewise tests a purported divine word by whether it happens, describing the negative case of an unfulfilled prediction.

Ezekiel 34:2
“Son of man, prophesy against Israel’s shepherds. Prophesy and say to the shepherds, ‘Thus says the Lord, the Lord: O shepherds of Israel, surely shepherds do not feed themselves? Do shepherds not feed the sheep?
  Jude 1:12 — shared criticism of shepherds feeding themselves: The source contrasts shepherds feeding themselves with their duty to feed the sheep. Jude also describes the criticized people as shepherding themselves at communal feasts, in a different setting.

Ezekiel 34:3
Behold, you consume the milk, clothe yourselves with the wool and slaughter the fattened animal, but you do not feed My sheep.
  Zechariah 11:4-5 — parallel exploitation of sheep by those responsible for them: Ezekiel's shepherds consume milk, wear wool and slaughter animals without feeding the flock. Zechariah describes sheep destined for slaughter, profitable sellers and unfeeling shepherds; the specific benefits differ.

Ezekiel 34:4
You have not strengthened the weak, restored the sick to health, bound up the broken, brought back the wandering or sought the lost. You have worn down the strong with toil.
  Zechariah 11:16 — close parallel lists of failed shepherding duties: Both passages fault a shepherd for failing to seek the scattered or lost and heal or bind the injured. Ezekiel also says the strong were worn down with toil, while Zechariah describes devouring flesh and wrenching ankle bones.

Ezekiel 34:5
My sheep scattered because there were no shepherds and became food for all the field’s beasts.
  John 10:12-13 — parallel scattering and exposure of an uncared-for flock: The source's sheep scatter for lack of shepherds and become food for beasts. In John the hired worker abandons the sheep and the wolf seizes and scatters them, because the worker does not care.

Ezekiel 34:6
My sheep scattered on every mountain and upon every high hill, and scattered over the whole face of the earth. There was no one seeking them out or turning them back.
  Jeremiah 23:1-3 — parallel scattered flock neglected by its shepherds: Both oracles describe God's scattered sheep. Ezekiel says no one seeks or turns them back, while Jeremiah faults shepherds who did not visit them and announces God's gathering of the remnant.

Ezekiel 34:8
I live, says the Lord, the Lord: surely because My sheep became plunder and My sheep became food for all the plain’s beasts for lack of shepherds, and the shepherds did not seek out My sheep but fed themselves and did not feed My sheep—
  Jeremiah 23:1-2 — parallel indictment of shepherds for the flock's loss: Ezekiel charges the shepherds with feeding themselves while the sheep become plunder and prey. Jeremiah likewise holds shepherds accountable for scattering and destroying the flock without visiting it.

Ezekiel 34:10
thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am against the shepherds. I will require My sheep from their hands and turn them aside from shepherding My sheep. The shepherds will no longer feed them. I will rescue My sheep from their mouth, and they will no longer be food for them.
  Jeremiah 23:2-3 — parallel divine accountability of shepherds and recovery of the flock: God requires the sheep from negligent shepherds and rescues them in Ezekiel. Jeremiah similarly announces judgment on the shepherds and God's own receiving and establishing of the remnant.

Ezekiel 34:11
For thus says the Lord: Behold, I will seek out My sheep and visit them.
  Luke 15:4-6 — related image of seeking and recovering a lost sheep: God promises personally to seek His sheep. Jesus' parable describes a shepherd going after a lost sheep until he finds it and then rejoicing; the source concerns the scattered people as a flock.

Ezekiel 34:12
As a shepherd seeks his flock on a day when there is gloom and cloud among separated sheep, so I will seek out My sheep and drive them away from every place where they scattered on a day of cloud and gloom.
  Luke 15:4-6 — related shepherd's search through the flock's separation: Ezekiel compares God's search for scattered people with a shepherd seeking separated sheep amid gloom. The parable likewise depicts a shepherd actively going after the lost sheep, without Ezekiel's cloud-and-gloom setting.

Ezekiel 34:13
I will bring them out from the nations, gather them from the countries and bring them into their own land. I will feed them upon Israel’s mountains, in the ravines and in every dwelling place of the land.
  Jeremiah 23:3-8 — parallel gathering from countries and restoration to pasture and land: Both passages gather Israel from the lands of dispersion and restore the people to their own land and pasture. Jeremiah additionally promises new shepherds and a righteous Davidic ruler.

Ezekiel 34:14
I will feed them in good pasture, and their folds will be upon Israel’s high mountain. There they will lie down, there rest in good delight, and graze in rich pasture upon Israel’s mountains.
  Psalms 22:1-2 — shared divine shepherding into nourishing pasture and rest: The source promises good pasture and a place where the sheep rest on Israel's mountain. The psalm describes the Lord shepherding the speaker into green growth and beside water of rest.

Ezekiel 34:15
I Myself will feed My sheep and give them rest, and they will know that I am the Lord. Thus says the Lord, the Lord:
  Psalms 22:1-2 — shared personal care and rest under the divine shepherd: God Himself promises to feed His sheep and give them rest. The psalm likewise names the Lord as the shepherd who nourishes the speaker and provides a resting place.

Ezekiel 34:16
I will seek the lost, bring back the wandering, bind up the broken, strengthen the failing and guard the strong. I will feed them with judgment.
  Zechariah 11:16 — contrast between promised care and failed shepherding: God promises to seek, restore, bind and strengthen the flock, guarding the strong. Zechariah's shepherd fails to attend, seek, heal or guide and instead consumes the flock. Ezekiel's guarding of the strong is preserved.
  Luke 15:4-7 — related recovery of the lost sheep: The source's first promise is to seek the lost and bring back the wandering. The parable depicts a shepherd finding a lost sheep and relates the rejoicing to a sinner's repentance; its scope is narrower than Ezekiel's whole list of care.

Ezekiel 34:17
And you, sheep, thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I will distinguish between sheep and sheep, rams and male goats.
  Matthew 25:32-33 — related divine distinction within a flock representing people: Ezekiel distinguishes among sheep, rams and male goats. Matthew depicts the gathered nations being separated as a shepherd separates sheep from goats; the target develops a judgment scene with right and left positions not stated in Ezekiel.

Ezekiel 34:18
Was it not enough for you that you grazed the good pasture, yet trampled the rest of your pasture with your feet, drank the settled water and troubled the rest with your feet?
  Ezekiel 34:19 — same-chapter accusation recast as its consequence for the flock: The source accuses the well-fed sheep of trampling pasture and muddying water. The next unit repeats those acts from the dependent sheep's perspective: they ate the trampled pasture and drank the disturbed water.

Ezekiel 34:19
My sheep grazed what your feet had trampled and drank the water troubled by your feet.
  Ezekiel 34:18 — same-chapter contrast between privileged consumption and the flock's remainder: The source describes the sheep eating trampled pasture and drinking disturbed water. The preceding accusation identifies the privileged animals that enjoyed the good pasture and settled water before spoiling what remained.

Ezekiel 34:20
Therefore thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I will distinguish between the strong sheep and the weak sheep.
  Matthew 25:32-33 — related judgment portrayed through distinguishing among flock members: Ezekiel distinguishes strong sheep from weak sheep amid the indictment of abusive flock members. Matthew also represents judgment of people through a shepherd separating a flock, but distinguishes sheep from goats rather than strong from weak sheep.

Ezekiel 34:21
You shove with your sides and shoulders; you butted with your horns and forced out every failing one.
  Ezekiel 34:4 — same-chapter extension of blame from shepherds to abusive flock members: The source accuses animals of using shoulders and horns to force out failing sheep. Earlier the shepherds were faulted for not strengthening the weak and wearing down the strong; both expose failures to care for vulnerable members, with different actors.

Ezekiel 34:22
I will save My sheep, and they certainly will no longer be plunder. I will judge between ram and ram.
  Jeremiah 23:3-4 — parallel restoration of a flock to safety: Ezekiel promises to save the sheep from plunder and judge within the flock. Jeremiah promises restored pasture and shepherds under whom the sheep will no longer fear. Jeremiah does not include the source's judgment between rams.

Ezekiel 34:23
I will raise up one shepherd over them, My servant David, and he will shepherd them and be their shepherd.
  Ezekiel 37:24 — repeated promise of David and one shepherd: Both passages designate God's servant David as ruler or shepherd and place the people under one shepherd. The later passage also connects this leadership with keeping God's ordinances.
  John 10:16 — related promise of unity under one shepherd: Ezekiel promises one shepherd, called God's servant David. Jesus says the sheep He brings will become one flock with one shepherd. This links the shared shepherd-and-unity language without treating the passages as identical wording.

Ezekiel 34:24
I, the Lord, will be their God, and David a ruler among them. I, the Lord, have spoken.
  Ezekiel 37:24-27 — repeated combination of Davidic rule and covenant belonging: Both passages place David among the people as ruler and declare that the Lord is their God. The later oracle expands this with an everlasting covenant and God's dwelling among them.

Ezekiel 34:25
I will make a covenant of peace with David and remove harmful beasts from the land. They will dwell in the wilderness and sleep in the woods.
  Leviticus 26:6 — shared promise of peace and removal of harmful beasts: Ezekiel promises a covenant of peace with David and removal of harmful beasts so people may rest safely. Leviticus likewise promises peace, safe lying down and removal of harmful animals, without naming David.

Ezekiel 34:26
I will place them around My mountain and give you rain, rain of blessing.
  Leviticus 26:4 — shared promise of beneficial rain: Ezekiel promises rain of blessing around God's mountain. Leviticus promises rain in its season and resulting produce; the source does not itself specify that seasonal wording.

Ezekiel 34:27
The trees in the plain will give their fruit, and the land will give its strength. They will dwell upon their land in hope of peace and know that I am the Lord when I shatter their yoke. I will rescue them from the hand of those who enslaved them.
  Leviticus 26:4-5 — related fruitfulness and peaceful settlement: Both passages promise fruitful trees and productive land. Ezekiel joins this with dwelling in hope of peace, while Leviticus promises secure dwelling. Ezekiel also announces deliverance from enslavers, which is not contained in this selected target.
  Leviticus 26:13 — close parallel breaking the enslaving yoke: Ezekiel promises to shatter the people's yoke and rescue them from enslavers. Leviticus recalls God's release of Israel from Egyptian slavery and the shattering of their yoke.

Ezekiel 34:28
They will no longer be plunder for the nations, and the land’s beasts certainly will no longer eat them. They will dwell in hope, and no one will terrify them.
  Leviticus 26:6 — close parallel security from beasts and terror: Both passages promise that harmful beasts will no longer threaten the people and that they will dwell or lie down without anyone frightening them.

Ezekiel 34:29
I will raise up a plant of peace for them. They will no longer perish by famine upon the land or bear the nations’ reproach.
  Joel 2:19-26 — parallel relief from famine and national reproach: Ezekiel promises a plant of peace, no longer perishing by famine and relief from the nations' reproach. Joel promises grain, wine and oil, abundant eating and an end to reproach; it does not identify Ezekiel's plant.

Ezekiel 34:30
They will know that I am the Lord their God, and they, Israel’s house, are My people, says the Lord.
  Leviticus 26:12 — shared covenant declaration of God and His people: Both passages join God's being their God with their being His people. Ezekiel explicitly identifies this people as Israel's house.

Ezekiel 34:31
You are My sheep and the sheep of My flock, and I am the Lord your God, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Psalms 99:3 — close identification of God's people as His sheep: Ezekiel calls the addressed people God's sheep and flock. The psalm similarly identifies the people God made as His people and the sheep of His pasture.

Ezekiel 35:2
“Son of man, turn your face toward Mount Seir and prophesy against it,
  Genesis 36:8-9 — geographical and ancestral counterpart for Mount Seir: The oracle is directed against Mount Seir. Genesis locates Esau, identified as Edom, in that mountain; Ezekiel 35:15 itself also pairs Mount Seir with Idumea.

Ezekiel 35:3
and say, ‘Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am against you, Mount Seir. I will stretch My hand against you and make you a wilderness, and you will be laid waste.
  Malachi 1:3-4 — parallel desolation of Esau's territory and Idumea: Ezekiel announces that Mount Seir will become a wilderness. Malachi likewise assigns Esau's territories to desolation and threatens to overthrow Idumea's rebuilding; Genesis 36:8-9 supplies the Seir-Esau connection.

Ezekiel 35:4
I will make your cities desolate, and you will be a wilderness. You will know that I am the Lord.
  Malachi 1:4 — parallel desolation of settlements in Idumea: Ezekiel announces desolate cities and wilderness. Malachi reports Idumea's plan to rebuild deserted places and God's declaration that He will overthrow what they build.

Ezekiel 35:5
Because you became an everlasting enemy and ambushed Israel’s house treacherously, by enemies’ hand, with a sword at the final time of injustice,
  Obadiah 1:10-14 — parallel condemnation of hostility during Israel's disaster: Ezekiel charges Seir with lasting enmity and treacherous attack during Israel's final time of injustice. Obadiah records violence against Jacob and participation in Jerusalem's calamity, then forbids destroying those escaping and shutting in fugitives.

Ezekiel 35:6
therefore, I live, says the Lord, the Lord: surely you sinned unto blood, and blood will pursue you.
  Genesis 9:5-6 — related requital for shedding blood: The source announces that blood will pursue the people who sinned unto blood. Genesis requires an accounting for human blood and describes the shedding of the blood-shedder's own blood in return.

Ezekiel 35:7
I will make Mount Seir a wilderness and lay it waste, and destroy human beings and livestock from it,
  Ezekiel 25:13 — close parallel judgment on Mount Seir and Idumea: God announces Mount Seir's desolation and the destruction of human beings and livestock. The earlier Idumea oracle likewise announces destruction of human and animal and the land becoming wilderness; Ezekiel 35:15 explicitly joins Mount Seir and all Idumea. The target additionally mentions Thaiman and the sword.

Ezekiel 35:8
and fill the hills and your ravines with your wounded. In all your plains, those wounded by the sword will fall within you.
  Isaiah 34:3-7 — related judgment filling Idumea's terrain with the wounded: Ezekiel fills hills, ravines and plains with the sword-wounded. Isaiah describes wounded and dead, blood-soaked mountains and a sword descending upon Idumea; the locations and imagery are not identical.

Ezekiel 35:9
I will make you an everlasting wilderness, and your cities certainly will not be inhabited again. You will know that I am the Lord.
  Isaiah 34:9-13 — parallel enduring desolation of Idumea: Ezekiel calls Seir an everlasting wilderness with uninhabited cities. Isaiah likewise describes Idumea's land burning and deserted for generations, with vegetation and wild creatures replacing settled cities.

Ezekiel 35:10
Because you said, “The two nations and the two countries will be mine, and I will inherit them,” and the Lord is there,
  Ezekiel 36:2-5 — adjacent-oracle parallel claim to possess God's land: Seir claims two nations and countries as its inheritance even though the Lord is there. The following oracle again condemns enemies, explicitly including Idumea, for assigning God's land to themselves; it does not name the source's two nations separately.

Ezekiel 35:11
therefore, I live, says the Lord: I will deal with you according to your hostility and make Myself known to you when I judge you.
  Obadiah 1:15 — parallel repayment according to the aggressor's own conduct: Ezekiel announces judgment according to Seir's hostility. Obadiah likewise says that what the aggressor did will be repaid upon the aggressor's own head.

Ezekiel 35:12
You will know that I am the Lord. I heard the voice of your blasphemies, because you said, “Israel’s mountains are desolate; they have been given to us for food.”
  Ezekiel 36:2-5 — adjacent-oracle repetition of predatory claims over Israel's land: The source quotes the boast that Israel's desolate mountains were given for the enemy's food. The following oracle repeats the enemy's possession claim and condemns Idumea's rejoicing and plunder.

Ezekiel 35:13
You spoke grandly against Me with your mouth. I heard.
  2 Kings 19:22-28 — related divine response to an enemy's arrogant speech: Ezekiel says the Lord heard Seir's grand speech against Him. The Kings oracle likewise identifies Assyria's raised voice against Israel's Holy One and says its arrogance reached God's ears; the enemies and occasions differ.

Ezekiel 35:15
You will be a wilderness, Mount Seir, and all Idumea will be utterly consumed. You will know that I am the Lord their God.’”
  Obadiah 1:18 — parallel announced consumption of Idumea's associated house: Ezekiel announces that Mount Seir and all Idumea will be consumed. Obadiah, addressed to Idumea, depicts Esau's house as stubble consumed by Jacob's fire and Joseph's flame. Its particular wording about no bearer of fire is preserved.

Ezekiel 36:1
“And you, son of man, prophesy concerning Israel’s mountains and say to Israel’s mountains, ‘Hear the Lord’s word:
  Ezekiel 6:2-3 — repeated address to Israel's mountains in contrasting oracles: Both directives direct Ezekiel to prophesy to Israel's mountains and call them to hear God's word. The earlier address announces a sword, whereas the present address introduces the restoration of the same land.

Ezekiel 36:2
Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Because the enemy said over you, “Well done! Everlasting wildernesses have become our possession,”
  Ezekiel 35:10-12 — adjacent-oracle repetition of the enemy's possession claim: The enemy celebrates gaining the desolate land as a possession. The preceding Seir oracle quotes the same kind of claim over the countries and Israel's devastated mountains; its two nations are not separately named here.

Ezekiel 36:3
therefore prophesy and say, Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Because you were dishonored and hated by those around you, so that you became a possession for the remaining nations, and you became a topic on people’s tongues and a reproach to nations,
  Psalms 78:1-4 — parallel national humiliation after hostile occupation: Ezekiel describes Israel's land becoming the nations' possession and a reproach on people's tongues. The psalm likewise joins invasion of God's inheritance and Jerusalem's devastation with neighbors' sneering and ridicule.

Ezekiel 36:4
therefore, mountains of Israel, hear the Lord’s word! Thus says the Lord to the mountains, hills, ravines, torrents, devastated and destroyed places, and abandoned cities, which became plunder and a trampling place for the remaining nations all around:
  Ezekiel 6:3-6 — repeated geographical address with reversal of the land's condition: Both oracles address Israel's mountains, hills and ravines and speak of devastated settlements. The earlier oracle announces their ruin; the present oracle addresses the ruined places before promising their recovery.

Ezekiel 36:5
Therefore thus says the Lord, the Lord: Surely in the fire of My wrath I have spoken against the remaining nations and all Idumea, because they gave My land to themselves as a possession with rejoicing, dishonoring lives so as to destroy through plunder.
  Obadiah 1:11-15 — parallel judgment on Idumea's conduct toward Judah's calamity: Ezekiel condemns Idumea for claiming God's land with rejoicing and destructive plunder. Obadiah describes Idumea's involvement in Jerusalem's calamity, forbids rejoicing and joining the attacks, and announces repayment according to its conduct.

Ezekiel 36:6
Therefore prophesy over Israel’s land and say to the mountains, hills, ravines and valleys, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I have spoken in My jealousy and My wrath because you bore the nations’ reproaches.
  Psalms 78:4-5 — related reproach of Israel and appeal to divine jealousy: Ezekiel's Lord speaks in jealousy and wrath because the land bore the nations' reproaches. The psalm places the neighbors' ridicule beside its question about God's anger and jealousy; it is a plea, whereas Ezekiel announces God's response.

Ezekiel 36:7
Therefore I will raise My hand against the nations around you; these will receive their own dishonor.
  Psalms 78:12 — parallel requital of the neighbors' insult: Ezekiel says the surrounding nations will receive their own dishonor. The psalm asks God to repay neighboring peoples for the insult they directed against Him; its sevenfold measure is not stated in Ezekiel.

Ezekiel 36:8
But your grapes and fruit, mountains of Israel, My people will consume, for they are drawing near.
  Amos 9:14 — parallel restored Israel consuming its land's fruit: Ezekiel promises that the approaching people will consume the mountains' grapes and fruit. Amos likewise joins the reversal of Israel's captivity with planting vineyards and gardens and enjoying their wine and fruit.

Ezekiel 36:9
For behold, I am toward you; I will look upon you, and you will be cultivated and sown.
  Amos 9:14 — parallel renewal of cultivation in restored land: Ezekiel promises that God's favorable attention will result in the land being cultivated and sown. Amos describes restored Israelites planting vineyards and gardens as they rebuild and inhabit the ruined land.

Ezekiel 36:10
I will multiply people upon you, all Israel’s house completely. The cities will be inhabited and the devastated land rebuilt.
  Isaiah 61:4 — parallel rebuilding of devastated settlements: Ezekiel promises inhabited cities and rebuilding of devastated land. Isaiah likewise promises renewal of deserted cities and places desolated for generations; it does not specify Ezekiel's multiplication of all Israel's house.

Ezekiel 36:11
I will multiply humans and livestock upon you, settle you as at your beginning and do you good as in your former times. You will know that I am the Lord.
  Jeremiah 38:27-28 — parallel restoration of human and animal populations: Ezekiel promises to multiply humans and livestock and restore former settlement. Jeremiah similarly promises to sow Israel and Judah with human and livestock seed, followed by God's watching to build and plant.

Ezekiel 36:12
I will cause people, My people Israel, to be born upon you. They will inherit you, and you will be their possession. You certainly will no longer be bereaved of them.
  Amos 9:15 — parallel secure possession of restored land: Ezekiel promises Israel possession of the land and says the land will no longer be bereaved of the people. Amos promises to plant the people on their given land without their being uprooted again; the images express security differently.

Ezekiel 36:13
Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Because they said to you, “You devour human beings and became bereaved by your nation,”
  Numbers 13:32 — repeated description of the land as devouring inhabitants: Ezekiel quotes the accusation that the land devours human beings. The scouts' dismaying report in Numbers uses the same description of a land that devours its inhabitants; the speakers and occasions differ.

Ezekiel 36:14
therefore you will no longer eat human beings or bereave your nation, says the Lord, the Lord.
  Numbers 13:32 — reversal of the land-devouring accusation: Ezekiel promises that the land will no longer eat human beings or bereave its nation. Numbers preserves the scouts' earlier accusation that the land devours its inhabitants; Ezekiel's promise negates that characterization.

Ezekiel 36:15
The nations’ dishonor will no longer be heard against you, and you certainly will not bear the peoples’ reproaches, says the Lord, the Lord.’”
  Joel 2:19 — parallel removal of the nations' reproach: Ezekiel promises that the land will no longer bear the peoples' reproaches. Joel likewise promises that God will no longer give His people over as a reproach among the nations, alongside renewed food supplies.

Ezekiel 36:17
“Son of man, Israel’s house lived upon their land and defiled it by their way, their idols and their uncleannesses. Their way before Me became like the uncleanness of a woman sitting apart.
  Leviticus 15:19-24 — ritual-uncleanness imagery applied to Israel's conduct: Ezekiel compares Israel's defiling conduct and idols with the uncleanness of a woman sitting apart. Leviticus supplies concrete regulations for menstrual uncleanness of that kind; it does not itself equate menstruation with Israel's wrongdoing.

Ezekiel 36:19
and scattered them among the nations and winnowed them into the countries. I judged them according to their way and their sin.
  Leviticus 26:33 — parallel covenant judgment of international scattering: Ezekiel describes God scattering Israel among nations as judgment for its conduct. Leviticus threatens scattering among the nations together with ruined land and cities as a consequence of covenant disobedience.

Ezekiel 36:20
They entered the nations where they entered, and profaned My holy name when it was said of them, ‘These are the Lord’s people, and they have left His land.’
  Romans 2:23-24 — parallel dishonor of God's name among the nations through His people: Ezekiel describes the holy name being profaned when nations see the Lord's people outside His land. Romans likewise says God's name is blasphemed among nations because of the addressed people's conduct, specifically their transgression of law.

Ezekiel 36:21
I spared them because of My holy name, which Israel’s house profaned among the nations where they entered.
  Ezekiel 20:9 — repeated divine action for the sake of the profaned name: God spares Israel because of His holy name, profaned among nations. The earlier oracle similarly says God acted so His name would not be profaned at all before nations during Israel's departure from Egypt.

Ezekiel 36:22
Therefore say to Israel’s house, ‘Thus says the Lord: I am not acting for you, house of Israel, but because of My holy name, which you profaned among the nations where you entered.
  Ezekiel 20:14 — repeated distinction between Israel's conduct and God's concern for His name: Ezekiel says the coming action is for God's holy name rather than for Israel. The earlier wilderness account also attributes God's restraining action to protecting His name before the nations, despite the people's rebellion.

Ezekiel 36:23
I will sanctify My great name, profaned among the nations, which you profaned among them. The nations will know that I am the Lord when I am sanctified in you before their eyes.
  Ezekiel 20:41 — close parallel public sanctification before the nations: Both promises say God will be sanctified in the people before the nations' eyes. The earlier promise places this beside gathering them from dispersion, while the source emphasizes His profaned great name.

Ezekiel 36:24
I will take you out of the nations, gather you out of all the lands and bring you into your own land.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — parallel gathering of dispersed Israel into its ancestral land: Both passages promise God's gathering of Israel from nations and bringing the people into their land. Deuteronomy describes that land as the fathers' inheritance and joins gathering with mercy and increase.

Ezekiel 36:25
I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you will be cleansed from all your uncleannesses and all your idols. I will cleanse you.
  Numbers 19:17-19 — related sprinkling of water for purification: Ezekiel promises sprinkling clean water to cleanse the people from uncleannesses and idols. Numbers prescribes sprinkling purification water on persons and objects defiled by contact with death; the kinds of impurity and the water's preparation differ.
  Hebrews 10:22 — related combination of sprinkling, inward cleansing and pure water: Ezekiel's clean-water sprinkling removes uncleannesses and idols. Hebrews exhorts people to approach with hearts sprinkled clean from an evil conscience and bodies washed with pure water, combining cleansing imagery in its exhortation about access to God.

Ezekiel 36:26
And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.
  Ezekiel 11:19 — close parallel replacement of the stone heart: Both promises give a new spirit and replace the heart of stone with a heart of flesh. The earlier oracle says another heart, while the source says a new heart.
  2 Corinthians 3:3 — related contrast of stone and hearts of flesh with the Spirit: Ezekiel replaces a stone heart with a heart of flesh and gives a new spirit. Paul describes Christ's letter inscribed by the living God's Spirit on hearts of flesh rather than stone tablets; the writing metaphor differs from Ezekiel's replacement of a heart.

Ezekiel 36:27
I will put My Spirit within you and cause you to walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.
  Ezekiel 11:19-20 — close parallel inner renewal enabling obedience: Both passages connect the gift of spirit within the people with walking in God's ordinances and doing His judgments or commands. Ezekiel 11 additionally states the reciprocal God-and-people relationship.
  Romans 8:3-4 — related Spirit-directed obedience to God's requirements: Ezekiel promises God's Spirit within the people so they keep His judgments. Romans describes God's action through His Son so the law's righteous requirement is fulfilled in those walking according to Spirit; it does not explicitly identify itself as this oracle's fulfillment.

Ezekiel 36:28
You will dwell upon the land I gave your fathers. You will be My people, and I will be your God.
  Jeremiah 38:33 — shared restored covenant relationship: Both promises declare that the people will be God's people and He their God. Ezekiel locates them on the land given their fathers; Jeremiah joins the relationship with God's law written within their understanding and hearts.

Ezekiel 36:29
I will save you from all your uncleannesses, summon the grain and multiply it, and will not bring famine upon you.
  Joel 2:19 — parallel provision of grain after divine restoration: Ezekiel promises multiplied grain and relief from famine along with cleansing. Joel promises grain, wine and oil to satisfy the people and remove national reproach; the selected verse does not repeat Ezekiel's cleansing language.

Ezekiel 36:30
I will multiply the tree’s fruit and the field’s produce, so that you do not bear the reproach of famine among the nations.
  Joel 2:22-26 — parallel fruitful fields and trees ending food-related shame: Ezekiel multiplies tree fruit and field produce to remove the reproach of famine. Joel likewise promises fruitful trees, filled threshing floors and abundant eating, followed by the promise that God's people will not be put to shame.

Ezekiel 36:31
You will remember your evil ways and practices that were not good, and feel disgust before them at your lawless deeds and abominations.
  Ezekiel 20:43 — close parallel remembrance of former evil with shame: Both promises describe remembering the ways by which Israel acted wickedly. The source expresses disgust at lawless deeds and abominations; the earlier oracle describes beating their faces because of their wickedness, without identical wording.

Ezekiel 36:32
I am not acting because of you, says the Lord, the Lord; this will be known to you. Be ashamed and abashed because of your ways, house of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 9:4-6 — related denial that land possession rewards Israel's merit: Ezekiel says God is not acting because of Israel and calls the people to shame over their ways. Deuteronomy likewise denies that possession of the good land comes from Israel's righteous deeds or holy heart, calling the people stiff-necked.

Ezekiel 36:33
Thus says the Lord: On the day I cleanse you from all your lawless deeds, I will cause the cities to be inhabited, and the wilderness places will be rebuilt.
  Jeremiah 40:7-8 — parallel restoration and cleansing of national wrongdoing: Ezekiel joins cleansing from lawless deeds with inhabited cities and rebuilding. Jeremiah similarly joins restoration and rebuilding of Judah and Israel with cleansing of the injustices committed against God.

Ezekiel 36:34
The devastated land will be worked, instead of lying devastated before every passerby’s eyes.
  Amos 9:14 — parallel cultivation replacing ruined land: Ezekiel says devastated land will again be worked. Amos describes restored Israelites rebuilding ruined cities and planting vineyards and gardens, giving concrete forms of renewed cultivation.

Ezekiel 36:35
They will say, “That devastated land has become like a garden of delight, and the deserted, devastated, razed cities stand fortified.”
  Isaiah 51:3 — parallel transformation of devastated land into a garden: Ezekiel's devastated land becomes like a garden of delight. Isaiah likewise promises comfort to Zion's deserted places by making them like the Lord's paradise; neither comparison requires altering the source's garden wording.

Ezekiel 36:36
The nations remaining around you will know that I, the Lord, have rebuilt the razed places and planted the devastated places. I, the Lord, have spoken and will act.
  Jeremiah 38:28 — parallel reversal from destruction to building and planting: Ezekiel identifies God as the One rebuilding razed places and planting devastated places. Jeremiah likewise contrasts His former tearing down and harming with His renewed watching to build and plant.

Ezekiel 36:37
Thus says the Lord: I will also be sought by Israel’s house to do this for them: I will multiply them, human beings like sheep.
  Ezekiel 34:31 — related explicit identification of human people with God's flock: Ezekiel promises to multiply human beings like sheep. The earlier shepherd oracle explicitly addresses the people as God's sheep and flock, supplying the same human-flock identification without the source's multiplication promise.

Ezekiel 36:38
Like holy sheep, like Jerusalem’s sheep at her festivals, so the deserted cities will be filled with sheep who are human beings. They will know that I am the Lord.’”
  2 Chronicles 35:7-9 — concrete festival-flock counterpart for the population comparison: Ezekiel compares cities full of people with Jerusalem's festival sheep. Chronicles describes many thousands of sheep, lambs and kids provided for Josiah's Passover; it illustrates large festival flocks without equating its numbers with Ezekiel's promised population.

Ezekiel 37:1
The Lord’s hand came upon me. The Lord brought me out in spirit and set me in the middle of the plain, which was full of human bones.
  Ezekiel 3:22-24 — repeated visionary setting involving the Lord's hand, a plain and spirit: The source places Ezekiel under the Lord's hand and brings him in spirit to a plain. The earlier commission likewise has the Lord's hand upon him, sends him to the plain and describes a spirit coming upon him; the visions there differ.

Ezekiel 37:2
He led me all around them, and behold, there were very many upon the plain’s surface, very dry.
  Ezekiel 37:11 — same-chapter explicit interpretation of the dry bones: The vision emphasizes the multitude and dryness of the bones. Its later interpretation identifies them as all Israel's house, whose saying about dry bones expresses lost hope and exhaustion.

Ezekiel 37:3
He said to me, ‘Son of man, will these bones live?’ I said, ‘Lord, Thou knowest these things.’
  Isaiah 26:19 — question about renewed life alongside an assurance of the dead rising: God asks whether the bones will live, and Ezekiel leaves the answer to God's knowledge. Isaiah explicitly announces that the dead will rise and those in tombs awaken; Ezekiel's surrounding vision applies its bones to Israel's house.

Ezekiel 37:4
He said to me, ‘Prophesy over these bones and say to them, Dry bones, hear the Lord’s word:
  John 5:25 — related portrayal of the dead hearing a life-giving divine word: Ezekiel must tell dry bones to hear the Lord's word. Jesus says the dead will hear the Son of God's voice and live; Ezekiel's vision explains the bones as Israel's house rather than giving John's identical setting.

Ezekiel 37:5
Thus says the Lord to these bones: Behold, I am bringing the breath of life into you,
  Genesis 2:7 — shared gift of the breath of life: God promises breath of life to the bones. Genesis describes God breathing the breath of life into the formed human so that he becomes a living soul; Ezekiel depicts restoration rather than the first human's formation.

Ezekiel 37:6
and I will put sinews upon you, bring flesh upon you, stretch skin over you and put My Spirit into you. You will live and know that I am the Lord.’
  Job 10:11-12 — shared description of bodily formation and divinely given life: Ezekiel names sinews, flesh and skin before the gift of Spirit and life. Job similarly recalls God clothing him in skin and flesh, joining bones and sinews, and granting life and care; the occasions differ.

Ezekiel 37:7
I prophesied as He commanded me. As I prophesied, behold, there was a shaking, and the bones came together, each to its joint.
  Ezekiel 37:5-6 — same-chapter narrated commencement of the promised reconstruction: Ezekiel's obedient prophecy is followed by bones joining at their joints. This begins the earlier promised reconstitution of the bones with sinews, flesh and skin, while the complete gift of life remains later in the vision.

Ezekiel 37:8
I saw, and behold, sinews and flesh grew upon them, and skin came up over them, but there was no breath in them.
  Job 10:11-12 — shared bodily materials with a significant difference about life: The vision shows sinews, flesh and skin on the bones but still no breath. Job recalls the same bodily materials together with God's gift of life and care, highlighting the distinction between bodily formation and living animation in Ezekiel's sequence.

Ezekiel 37:9
He said to me, ‘Prophesy, son of man! Prophesy to the breath and say to the breath, Thus says the Lord: Come from the four winds and breathe into these dead, and let them live!’
  Genesis 2:7 — related breathing of life into a formed body: Ezekiel calls the breath from the four winds to breathe into the dead so they live. Genesis describes God breathing life into the human formed from dust; Genesis does not supply the four winds or the multitude of bodies.
  John 20:21-22 — related breathing and reception of Spirit: The source commands breath to enter the dead and make them live. John describes Jesus breathing and telling the disciples to receive the Holy Spirit; this is a comparison of breathing and the divine gift of Spirit, not an identification of the disciples with Ezekiel's dead bodies.

Ezekiel 37:10
I prophesied as He commanded me. The breath entered them, and they lived and stood upon their feet, an exceedingly great assembly.
  Ezekiel 37:5-6 — same-chapter realization of the promised breath and life: After prophecy, breath enters the bodies, they live and stand. This realizes the earlier promise that God would put breath and Spirit into the bones so that they live.

Ezekiel 37:11
The Lord spoke to me, saying, ‘Son of man, these bones are all Israel’s house, and they say, Our bones have become dry, our hope has perished; we are utterly spent.
  Proverbs 17:22 — related use of dried bones to express inward distress: Israel's saying that its bones are dry is explicitly joined with lost hope and exhaustion. Proverbs likewise says that a sorrowful person's bones dry up, using the bodily image for distress in an individual rather than the national vision.

Ezekiel 37:12
Therefore prophesy and say, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I am opening your tombs and will bring you up from your tombs and into Israel’s land,
  Isaiah 26:19 — parallel imagery of bringing life from tombs: Ezekiel promises to open the people's tombs and bring them into Israel's land. Isaiah announces that the dead will rise and those in tombs awaken; the selected Isaiah verse does not specify Ezekiel's return to Israel's land.

Ezekiel 37:13
and you will know that I am the Lord when I open your graves to bring My people up from the graves.
  John 5:28-29 — related image of people brought out of graves: Ezekiel links recognition of the Lord with His opening graves and bringing His people up. Jesus describes those in tombs hearing His voice and coming out to resurrection of life or judgment; Ezekiel's surrounding explanation concerns restoration of Israel's house.

Ezekiel 37:14
I will put My Spirit into you, and you will live. I will place you upon your land, and you will know that I, the Lord, have spoken and will act, says the Lord.’
  Romans 8:11 — related promise of life through God's indwelling Spirit: Ezekiel promises Spirit within the people, life and placement on their land. Romans promises that the One who raised Christ will give life to believers' mortal bodies through His indwelling Spirit; it does not include Ezekiel's land promise.

Ezekiel 37:16

  Numbers 17:17-20 — parallel use of inscribed tribal staffs as visible divine signs: Ezekiel writes Judah and Joseph-Ephraim on two staffs to depict joining. Numbers prescribes named staffs for ancestral houses, with Aaron's name on Levi's staff, for a sign of divine choosing; the signs and number of staffs differ.

Ezekiel 37:17

  Zechariah 11:14 — contrasting actions with staffs concerning Judah and Israel: Ezekiel binds staffs into one in the sign of reunion. Zechariah throws away the second staff to dissolve the possession between Judah and Israel; the source's joining contrasts with the target's throwing away without replacing its word possession.

Ezekiel 37:18
When your people’s sons say to you, Will you not tell us what these things mean to you?
  Ezekiel 24:19-24 — parallel request for interpretation of a prophetic sign: Ezekiel anticipates the people's request to explain the staffs. In the earlier acted sign the people likewise ask what his conduct means, and he interprets the mourning sign; the visible actions themselves differ.

Ezekiel 37:19
say to them, Thus says the Lord: Behold, I will take Joseph’s tribe through Ephraim’s hand, and Israel’s tribes attached to him, and place them upon Judah’s tribe. They will become one staff in Judah’s hand.
  Hosea 2:2 — parallel reunion of Judah and Israel under common authority: The staff's interpretation joins Joseph-Ephraim and attached tribes to Judah as one staff in Judah's hand. Hosea likewise joins Judah's and Israel's sons gathering and appointing one authority; it does not use Ezekiel's staff imagery.

Ezekiel 37:20
The staffs upon which you wrote will be in your hand before them,
  Numbers 17:24 — parallel public presentation of the tribal staffs: Ezekiel must hold the inscribed staffs before the people. Numbers describes Moses bringing the tribal staffs before all Israel, who look at them; the earlier context concerns the sprouted staff, whereas Ezekiel's sign concerns joining.

Ezekiel 37:21
and say to them, Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am taking all Israel’s house from among the nations where they entered, gathering them from all those around them and bringing them into Israel’s land.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — parallel gathering from nations into Israel's land: Both promises gather the dispersed people from nations and return them to their inherited land. Ezekiel expressly names all Israel's house and Israel's land.

Ezekiel 37:22

  Hosea 2:2 — close parallel reunion under a single ruler: Ezekiel promises one nation and ruler instead of two kingdoms. Hosea likewise promises Judah's and Israel's sons gathering together and appointing one authority.

Ezekiel 37:23
so that they no longer defile themselves with their idols. I will deliver them from all their lawless deeds in which they sinned and cleanse them. They will be My people, and I, the Lord, will be their God.
  Ezekiel 36:25-28 — repeated combination of cleansing from idols and covenant belonging: Both restoration promises cleanse the people from their defilement and idols and declare that they will be God's people and He their God. The preceding oracle additionally describes clean water and a renewed heart and spirit.

Ezekiel 37:24
My servant David will be ruler among them, and one shepherd will be over all, because they will walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.
  Ezekiel 34:23-24 — close parallel one shepherd called God's servant David: Both passages appoint God's servant David over the people and speak of one shepherd. The source also connects this rule with the people's keeping God's ordinances and judgments.

Ezekiel 37:25
They will dwell upon their land, which I gave My servant Jacob, where their fathers dwelt. They will dwell upon it, and David My servant will be their ruler for ever.
  2 Samuel 7:12-16 — related promise of enduring Davidic rule: Ezekiel calls David God's servant and promises his rule for ever. The oracle to David in 2 Samuel promises an offspring whose house, kingdom and throne are established for ever; the passages describe the ruler differently, so the link does not settle an individual identification.

Ezekiel 37:26

  Isaiah 54:9-10 — parallel enduring covenant of peace: Ezekiel promises an everlasting covenant of peace. Isaiah likewise declares that the covenant of peace will not be removed and grounds it in divine mercy; Ezekiel also places God's holy things among the people.

Ezekiel 37:27
My dwelling will be among them. I will be their God, and they will be My people.
  2 Corinthians 6:16 — close parallel dwelling and reciprocal God-and-people declaration: Ezekiel promises God's dwelling among the people and declares that He will be their God and they His people. Paul's temple exhortation repeats divine dwelling and the same reciprocal declaration, also mentioning God's walking among them.

Ezekiel 37:28
The nations will know that I am the Lord who sanctifies them, when My holy things are among them for ever.’
  Exodus 29:43-46 — related holy presence and recognition of Israel's God: Ezekiel joins God's sanctifying the people with His holy things among them and the nations' recognition. Exodus joins consecration of the Tent and priests with God's being called upon among Israel and the people's recognition of their God; its audience of recognition differs.

Ezekiel 38:2
